PASTOR'S FAREWELL

- Rev. J. S. Chedburn Says "Good-bye" to Latchford.

Having resigned the pastorate of Latchford Baptist Church, the Rev. J. S. Chedburn preached his farewell sermon to a. large congre- gation on Sunday evening, when he was also presented with a purse of gold at a special meet- ing, held after the service, which was bright and impressive. PASTOR'S VALEDICTORY REMARKS.

Better Things and More Glorious Victories. After preaching an eloquent sermon, the Rev. .7. S. Chedburn said that that service brought his sojourn with them to a close. It was with considerable sadness that he realised it. It had not been a long sojourn, but it had given him time to form many close and happy associa- tions, to break which would be very hard. He had experienced much kindness at the hands of the congregation, who had borne with his weaknesses and faults. If he had been enabled

to teach anything he had also learned many valuable things. In some respects their work together had not been attended with anything that might be regarded as brilliant results, but rather had it been of a quiet, plodding nature. The seed h&d been sown, and where one stored, anothe might reap. ' He' had been considerably encouraged by the kindly expressions which had been made to him by many of his congregation, and he was strong in the assurance that what had been sown would bring forth good fruit in human life and chat- acter. lie prayed that the church, would be even more successful in the future than it had been in the past. He trusted that in the time of difficulty that was before them they would be ' wisely guided, and would' allow themselves to be controlled by the Spirit of God in all things, and that his successor, whoever he might be, would lead them to better things and more glorious victories. He could best put the feel- ings he experienced into the words: " Good-bye —God be with you." A PURSE OF GOLD, Mr. Wells,: who presided at the 'presentation, said that me Chedburn 'had fulfilled the prom- ise he made when he entered upon the pastorate. During 60, years they had only had five pastors, so that they could not be accused of being fickle people. Mr. .Citedburn had been of great ser- vice to them, 'find 'his work had met with suc- cess.:

"NO PLACE LIKE HOME." "Old Poor-Law Boys" Ask to Spend Christman in Children's Quarters.

CAROL SINGING.

At the fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians on Tuesday, an application was made on behalf of the children for them to be allowed to go carol singing at Christmas time. The request was granted so. far as the district was concerned, excluding Runcorn. Mr. Gibbs: Why this boycotting of Runcorn?

(Laughter.) The Deputy Clerk: The proceeds are to be given to the Christmas Tree fund. Permission, he continued, was also granted for the holding of the annual entertainment on the 29th inst., and the Guardians were invited to attend. Three of the boys who were formerly at the homes, but are now employed by Messrs. Pil- kington Bros., St. Helens, wrote asking if they could spend a day or two at the homes at Christmas time. The committee decided that the matter be left with the Superintendent.

The Shameless Member? Mr. Morris: I am not ashamed of the refer- ence to Runcorn and carol singers. Mr. Woodward: It takes a lot to shame you.

(Laughter.) Mr. Morris: Consider what happened last year! Had anyone seen them walking round Runcorn they would have been of the same opinion as myself. Mr. Antwis : If the weather is like it is now it would be undesirable for them to go. It was pointed out that the children would

not go out if the weather was had. Referring to the application of ttle boys to visit the homes at Christmas, Alderman Nor- man said the feeling at the committee meet- ing was that the boys would like to keep in touch with those with whom they were broillht up at the homes. Young Men's Hostel or Children's Home? • Mr. Gibbs: Is this a young men's hostel or a, children's home? Alderman Norman said he thought if they let the boys come it would be establishing a prece- dent which might involve difficultfes in the future. The Chairman submitted, as an addition to the resolution: "And that the boys be allowed to remain at the homes for a day or two, if there is accommodation." Mr. Morris: Has the superintendent been in touch with the superintendent of the hostel at Messrs. Pilkington's works? The Chairman: No; the boys have made the application. Mr. Morris: I don't object to the principle, but I should like the superintendent to write a letter to Mr. Firth, asking him for his permi,_ Sion, and getting to know how long the boys will have holiday. , Would Not Improve Their Holiday.

Mr. Gibbs moved that the resolution be de- leted. He objected to the children's homes being used as a young men's hostel. The youths were now earning their own living, and ought not to go to the children's homes' at the expense

of the Guardians. Mr. Pilling seconded Mr. Gibbs' motion, and said he did not think it right that the youths should go to the homes. They would not im- prove their holiday, because the arrangements at St. Helens were fine. Mr. Morris: Yes, there is ample provision made for recreation.

It Cannot Always Be Guided By Sentiment. Mr. Hallows: There is no place like home. .- It would be hard if the youths could not come

IN THE COURTS.

BOROUGH POLICE COURT.

SATURDAY.

Before Aid. Tinnion and Mr. T. Pemberton. Mary's Last Chance.—Mary Swift, 23, Dakin's- yard, again appeared before the magistrates, and the Chief Constable explained that early on Thursday morning P.C. Barr saw the woman wandering about in Orchard-street. She had been drinking heavily and seemed to be border- ing on delirium tremens. Since then she had got some of the drink out of her, and he (the Chief) suggested that the Bench should send her about her business and warn her not to drink; as she had been doing for a long time. On Wednesday she had received ..,64 or £5 through the Post Office from her son, who was in the: Army. She had told fictitious tales about the money, but the police had ascertained that she had banked £2 16s. of it. It was a hopeless case. She had been drinking for years and years. She should be told that if she repeated the offence' the Government would be urged to stop her allowance.—Prisoner: You cannot stop my son's allowance.—The Chief Oh, yes, they can Chairman: Unless you mend your ways it will be stopped.—Prisoner: I promise to be better.— The Chairman: You will be discharged then. A Violent Prisoner.—For having been drAnk and disorderly and assaulting P.C. Wise, Wil- liam Polland of no fuced abode, was sent to prison for one month. He kicked the officer and struck him in the face, and had to be brought to the Police Station, on a handcart.

WEDNESDAY. Before Mr. C. Jackson Holmes (in the chair) and Mr. W. T. Williams.

Boy Who, Tried To Escape.—A small boy, who had been previously charged with stealing a purse, a metal ring and a tie-holder from the Market, was brought up on remand at a Chil- dren's Court and ordered to be sent to the St. John's Industrial School at Walthamstow until he attains the age of 19.—The Chief Constable mentioned that the lad was- a great source of trouble and had tried to break away from the Police Station whilst he had been detained. " Swopped " Stolen " Fags " for Ice Cream.— Two boys, aged 11 and 13 respectively, were charged with stealing a quantity of cigarettes, valued at 4s. 71d., from the shops 37, Sankey- street, and 58, Horsemarket-street. Two other boys, each aged 11, were charged with receiving some of the stolen property, and Lewis Monfredi (16), 101, Winwick-street, was charged with re- ceiving Is. worth of the cigarettes.—lt was stated that two of the boys had pestered the assistants at the shops in question for 4*(Mpty boxes, and had crept in the shops, tliken the cigarettes and ran off with them to the others, who were wait- ing outside. They had given Monfredi two sixpenny packets of cigarettes in exchange for ice cream wafers.—Mr. T. S. Steel appeared for Monfredi and said the youth informed him that the lads said they had found 2s. and had bought the cigarettes. He gave them four ice cream wafers for the two packets, and did not think they had been itolen. He (Mr. Steel) thought justice would be met by placing Monfredi under probation for a short time.—The magistrates dismissed the case against Monfredi on payment of costs, and sent one of the boys, who was ad- judged to be the ringleader, to an Industrial School. The other boys each received six strokes of the birch.

FLOUR MILLERS' GIFT.

Two Complete _Hospital Trains.

The fund for the relief Of the wounded and sick soldiers raised by the flour millers of the United Kingdom now amounts to £23,837. The primary object of the fund is to provide the two British sections of two hospital trains for service in France and Belgium. Both trains have been ordered at an estimated cost of £22,000, and are to be presented to the British Red Cross Society for the use of the wounded of the Allied forces. It is expected that the first train, which is built by English railway companies to the order of the War Office, will be delivered within a few days, and a cheque for £ll,OOO on account has been paid to the British Red Cross and St. John Ambulance Societies. Both trains will be labelled "United Kingdom Flour Millers' Hospital Train" on every coach.

MILK AND DAIRIES ACT.

The Local Government Board on Tuesday night antwunced that they. have decided the Milk and Dairies Act (1914) shall come into operation on October 1 next.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 26, 1914.

EGYPT UNDER THE BRITISH FLAG

THE KHEDIVE DEPOSED.

Appointment of a Sultan.

The following was issued by the Press Bureau on Friday evening In view of the action of his Highness Abbas Hilmi Pasha, lately Khedive of Egypt, who has adhered to the King's enemies, his Majesty's Government have seen " to depose him from the Khediviate, and that high dignity has been offered, with the title of Sultan of Egypt, to his Highness Prince Hussein ,Kamil Pasha, eldest living Prince of the family of Mehemet Ali, and has been accepted by him. The King has been pleased to give directions for the following appointment to the Order of ! St. Michafd and St. George His Excellency Hussein.Rushdi Pasha, Presi- dent of the Council of Ministers of H.H. the Sultan of Egypt, to be an honorary Knight Grand Cross of the Order. Prince liussein Kamil is a son of Khedive Ismail, and uncle of the late Khedive Abbas Hilmi. Born In 1854, he was educated in Europe, and lived for a time at Paris and after- wards at Naples. Upon the dethronement of his father, Ismail, he followed him into exile, but was authorised by Great Britain to ::turn to Egypt in 1884. For some years he ,sen.;3wrld politics, and devoted his attention to ag-la- ture, but was eventually elected President of the Legislative Assemhly. Differences of opinion with his nephew Abbas "Hilmi• induced him, however,, to resign these functions. Prince Hus- sein Hamil has long enjoyed respect as the ablest and most trustworthy member of the Khetlivdl family. THE KING AND )-FIE SULTAN._

The King has sent a message to the new Sultan of Egypt, conveying to Hussein Kamil .the ex-

pression of his Majesty's most sincere friendship and the assurance of his unfailing support in safeguarding the integrity of Egypt and in securing her well-being and prosperity. The Sultan has sent a telegram in reply, ex- pressing to the King his gratitude for his Majesty's assurance of friendship and support, and pledging, himself to co-operate whole- heartedly with the Protectorate in promoting the welfare of the Egyptian people.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
THE TRIBUTE TO TURKEY.

The termination of the Turkish suzerainty over Egypt, and, the declaration of a 'British Protectorate, is, says "The Times," a purely practical administrative step, dictated by the appearance of Turkey as a combatant. It is not tantamount to annexation, as the "Man- chester Guardian" and other newspapers erro- neously suppose, and there are very good reasons why it should not be so. The declara- tion presumably does not relieve Egypt from the payment of a sum amounting annually to something over £650,000, designated as tribute to Turkey. That moiiey is not directly received by Turkey. It comes to London, and is devoted to the service of two Imperial Ottoman loans secured upon these annual payments. •We may further point out that the declaration of a Pro- tectorate does not directly concern the Soudan, where the nominal suzerainty of the &Man of Turkey was never restored after the reconquest of the Soudan provinces by Anglo-Egyptian forces. King George has exercised sovereign rights in the Soudan in conjunction with the Khedive, and that fact has, and will continue to have, a very important bearing upon the future of British relations with Egypt.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
GRAND CADI REMOVED.

The Egyptian Government has decided to re- move the Grand Cadi, the supreme religious authority in the country. They will appoint in his place an Egyptian. Hitherto the Turkish Sultan has jealously maintained his privilege of choosing the Cadi, and has on occasion selected men unacceptable at Cairo. Because of his in- fluence over all Mahometpi Egyptians the Cadi Kis the powiT to play' ha important part in counteracting any propaganda attempted from Constantinople. ,

===== 0002601_19141226_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RAID ON THE EAST COAST.

Mr. Churchill's Comment. "AN INSTRUCTIVE AND ENCOURAGING

EVENT."

The casualty list from the bombardment of the North-East Coast by German cruisers on Wednesday last, week continues to grow. At the Hartlepools alone the death-roll is about 100. The jury at the inquest on the victims at Scarborough strongly favoured a verdict of "Wilful murder," but were persuaded by the Coroner on technical grounds to alter it to one of "Killed by the bombardment of the town by an enemy ship." The German Press makes an exuberant dis- play of headlines in order to celebrate the bom- bardment.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
TIIE BRAND ON GERMAN SAILORS.

The following letter has been sent by the First Lord of the Admiralty to the Mayor of Scar- borough : " Admiralty, S.W., Dec. 20, 1914. "My Dear Mr. Mayor,—l send you a message of sympathy, not Only On my own account, but on behalf of the Navy, •in the loses Scarborough has sustained. We mourn with you the peaceful inhabitants who have been killed or maimed, and particularly the women and children. We admire the dignity and fortitude with which Scarborough, Whitby, and the Hartlepools have confronted outrage. We share your dis- appointment that the miscreants escaped un- punished. We await with patience the oppor- tunity that will surely come. "But viewed in its larger aspect, the incident is one of the most instructive and encouraging that have happened in the war. Nothing proves more phitinly the effectiveness of British naval pressure than the frenzy of hatred aroused against us in the breasts of the enemy. This hatred ha si already passed the frontiers of rea- son. It clouds their vision, it darkens their counsels, it convulses their movements. We see a nation of military calcUlators throwing cal- culation to the winds; of strategists who have lost their sense of proportion; of schemers, who have ceased to balance loss and gain. 'Practically the whole fast cruiser force of including some great ships. the German Navy, vital to their fleet and utterly irreplacable, has been risked for the passing pleasure of killing as Many English people as possible, irrespective of sex, age, or condition, in the limited time available. To this -act of military and political folly they were ithpelled by the violence of feelings which could find no other vent. This is very satisfactory, and should confirm us in our courses. Their hate-is the measure of their fear. Its senseless expression is the proof of their impotence and. the seal of their dishonour. Whatever feats of arms the German Navy may hereafter perform, the stigma of the baby-killers of Scarborough will bran'd its officers and men while sailors sail the seas. "Believe me, dear Mr. Mayor, • Yours faithfully, • " WINSTON S. CHURCHILL."

===== 0002601_19141226_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
SCARBOROUGH'S REPLY.

" As Fully Determined As Ever."

The Mayor of Scarborough (Mr. C. C. GrahaM) forwarded the following letter to Mr. Winston Churchill, the First Lord of the Admiralty : " Scarborough, Dec. 21, 1914. "Dear Sir,----On behalf of the inhabitants of Scarborough I have to thank you for the kind messages of sympathy conveyed in your letter to me of the 20th inst., which will be greatly appreciated. It is evident that the enemy did not dare to face our Fleet, and so attacked this undefended town in this way: • 'Scarborough has taken her part in the great struggle that is now proceeding. Whilst we deplore the loss of life and property, mourn for our dead, and Sympathise with our wounded, we are neverthe- less as fully determined as ever that the war must be fought to a suCcessful finish. "Our surprise at the attack was the greater as we were led to believe from the conduct of the plucky, commander of the Emden that Ger- man sailers understood something about the glorious old tradition of the sea. It is evident, fir opr Wednesany„ that this is flo• so, Some newcomers into honourable pro- fessions first learn the tricks and lastly the traditions. As their commanders get older in the service they will find, that _an Iron Cross pinned on their chest, even by King Herod, will not shield them from the shafts of shame and dishonour./ Again I thank you for your letter, "Yours faithfully, "C. C. GRAIIAM, Mayor."

===== 0002601_19141226_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
ARCHBISHOP ON RECRUITING.

The Archbishop of York, in the course of a written message to Scarborough, says that if the object of the Germans was to create general panic and alarm, they who had quietly stayed at home had already proved its vanity. If its object was to hinder the discharge of fresh re- inforc,ements to the battle, line in France and Flanders, be was sure that the Ilion of Yorkshire and the country would make it futile by enlist- ing in even larger numbers, so that for every man who was sent abroad another would be ready to take his place and defend our land.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
ANTI-GERMAN RIOT AT SUNDER- LAND.

An anti-German riot took place at Sunder- land on Saturday night. It was due to intense resentment at the bombardment of llartlepool and the freeing•on appeal of Ahlers, the Sun- derland German consul, who had been convicted .of .high treason and sentenced to death. A crowd of 2,000 attacked the German pork butchers in all parts of the town. Windows were smashed, and the contents of the shops scattered. The occupants had to flee, but no one was hurt, and the police were unable to effect arrests owing to the size and demeanour of the ,crowd, which was largely composed of pit- men.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
EMPLO)'MENT IN DECEMBER.'

tlettgr Man Last 'Year.

In the trades compulsorily- insured against un- employment—viz., building, works of construc- tion, engineering, shipbuilding, vehicle-making, &c.—the percentage of unemployment at the 18th DeceMber was 3.28, as compared with 3.44 a week ago, 3.77 a month ago, and 4.32 a year ago. These figures relate to the whole of the United Kingdom, and include all unemployed workmen in the insured trades. It will be seen that the rate of unemployment in these trades remains below the level of a year ago. As regards the uninsured trades, the number of men and women on the registers of the Labour Exchanges at the 18th December shows a de- crease on the figure a week ago, being 42,634, as compared with 47,384. For men alone the corre- sponding figures were 17,162 and 18,401, and for women 25,472 and 28,983.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
A YEAR'S SHIPBUILDING.

Tyne and Wear Returns "Vet' Satisfactory."

• The Wear shipbuilding returns for the year now drawing to a close were made up on Tues- day. They indicate that the output had been very satisfactory. _ -

Seventy-four vessels, aggregating 319,225 tons, were launched. This is five vessels and 36,141 tons less than last year, and also lower than several pp3viooB years, but work has not been continuoug in the closing months owing to the war. Sir Theodore Doxford heads the list of builders with twelve vessels and 62,368 tons. The output on the Tyne is also regarded as very satisfactory. The returns show that apart from Admiralty work—of which no details can he given-93 vessels have been launched of a total tonnage of 346,445, as compared with 100 vessels and 441,826 tons last year. How far Government contracts would have augmented the returns cannot be even roughly estimated, but,it is not likely that had war vessels been included the total output would have been as large as last year's record figure.

ST. HELENS WOMAN'S STRANGE LETTER. Body Exhumed. EXTRAORDINARY INQUEST STORY.

On Tuesday extraordinary evidence was given at an inquest held at St. Helens by Mr. S. Brig- house on' the body of Mrs. Jane E. M. Bernhardt, aged 56, of 20, Cropper's Mil, who died on the 9th inst., and was buried, but whose body was exhumed for post-mortem examination by order of the Coroner. The Coroner explained that Mrs. Bernhardt was a lady of independent means, who was somewhat eccentric and lived alone. For some years she had been troubled with asthma, hut would never have a doctor. On December 7th she complained to a girl named Kirkham, who was then staying with her, of having pains in the side, and added: "I have been poisoned two or three times, and if I have no doctor there will be an inquest before I am buried." Her landlord, Mr. Thomas Gerrard, who called the same, day, seeing that she was ill, advised her to see a doctor, but she declined. He sent a telegram to her relatives asking them to come over, and on December 9th her niece, Miss Green, came over and found her in an uncon- scious condition. Dr. Latham was called in, and said she was dying. Soon after he left the house death took place. Dr. Latham gave it as his opinion, after an examination of the body at his (the Coroner's) request, that he could see nothing to lead him to think that she died from anything but natural causes, and the death was accordingly registered. " I MUST BE EXHUMED." _ • . . -

However," 4;n —die 15th inst., proceeded the Coroner, the Chief Constable sent him a letter received from Mrs. Bernhardt's niece, Miss Green, enclosing a letter written by Mrs. Bern- hardt, which had been found in the house dur- ing the niece's stay there. The letter was as follows: _

"If I die suddenly I wish you to see Mr. Eller- ington (the Chief Constable of St. Helens), but on no account must I be buried without an in- quest. If I am buried before, you know, I

must be exhumed, and a post-mortem held. If I am left in the ground for &elve months it will be too late. You will find all that has transpired since you left in three pads like this sheet of paper. They must be put in the hands of Mr. Ellerington to use against the man they are about. I shall not take my own life. It will be taken as others have been taken, and to prove this another body will have to be taken up. All is written clearly in the last pad. The pads are sealed up, and are at the address given on the enclosed card. They will be given to no one but you when you take this card. I have nearly lost my life twice since you went away. You know what we were both like, you and I, when we were in Biekerstaffe-street." The Coroner added that no card had been found, but when he received that letter he ordered an exhumation of the body and a post- mortem. It was Mr. Gerrard who, in the first instance, found the letter, and it was said that he suggested that, it should be burnt. There was also found .what purported to be the last will of Mrs. Bernhardt, in which she said "Should I die I wish an inquest to be held , over me, no matter what the doctors put on my death certificate, and if buried my wish is to be exhumed and examined before twelve months , expire. To exhume me after twelve months is over will be useless."

THE MEDICAL EVIDENCE. Dr. Unsworth stated that his firm conviction was that Mrs. Bernhardt died from ,natural causes. The only possible alternative would be for a person in a dying condition to receive a dose of poison which would hasten her end, but he could not say there was any poison. The Coroner said that under the circum- stances he would order an analysis of the organs of the body. Gerrard gave evidence that he advised Mrs. Bernhardt to see a,doctor, but she would not do so. After the death he advised the rela- tives to have an inquest. He denied that he suggested that the letter should be burnt, and said he forgot to tell the police about the letter and the will. The witness, in reply to Mr. Brewis, who ap- peared for him, said that although he was named as the executor to the will, he had not acted, and did not benefit under the will. Ho did not poison Mrs. Bernhardt. The inquiry was then adjourned for a month, to enable an analysis of the organs to be made.

NIETZSCHE AND THE CON- SUMMATION OF TRAGEDY. Mr. Whithaus R,ply to Mr. Parker.

To the Editor of " The Examiner."

Sir,—A tender spot that seems to exist some- where in my anatomy has recently had some- what roughly handling. I feel sore about it. Mr. C. E. Parker is the party to whom I must look for redress and comfort. He it is whom I expect to cry out, "tea Culpa" after he has read the effort I now make in behalf of poor dead and gone Friedrich Nietzsche. Nietzsche and his teachings is now my theme. These teachings have been before the world for quite a number of years, and I, for one, have been one of his admirers, and, oftime:, puzzled readers.

The writings of Bernhardi and of Professor Cramb are definite, positive; so also are those of Treitsehke. A twisted and peculiarly Ger- man method of reasoning ha S made the words we have heard bandied about so often during the past half-century—" Survival of the fittest" —fit and mix with their wretched philosophy that "Might is right." There is nothlng that is original in the writ- ings of the three men named above. Dr. Red- beard, of Chicago, years ago, wrote a book, the title of which was "The Survival of the Fittest." I have not the book by me now, but I remember the salient features of his interpretation of the phase were: "You must ride or you will be ridden. You must drive or you will be driven." Mr. Parker, in his letter, connects the two names—Treitschke and Nietzsche—together. Why, I nnot make out. He gives no quota- tions from any of the latter's writings.. He simply tries to heap obloquy that is not deserved on the name of Nietzsche. I quote from Nietzsche's book, "human, all too Human," and my quotation applies to German thought at the present time : "The keenest thorn in the sentiment of sin is dulled when it is perceived that one's actS have contravened human tradition, human rules, and human laws without having thereby endangered the eteival salvation of the soul and its relations with deity. If finally men attain to the conviction of the absolute necessity of all acts and of their utter irresponsibility, and then absorb it into their flesh and blood, every relic of conscience pangs will disappear." Nietzsche's Scorn of Culture. Now, I ask Mr. Parker to analyse, especially, this last short sentence, and compare it with the Christian feeling of England at this moment, and let us have the result. Nay, do more'than this: •tell in and wherein it differs from Daniel's reading of the writing upon the wall? Nietzsche had nothing but a deserving scorn for the Teuton's culture. In his book, "Twilight of the Idols "—see the comparison between Teuton and Slav—" The only great nation of to-day that has some lasting pinkei. and grit in her, that can bide her time, that can--still pro- mise something, is Russia." ,

Nietzsche so-a,ght a cosmopolitanism that would evolve the f` good European." He saw clearly the evils of dominance of the strong over the weak.

This again from "human, all tic) Human" : " The doctrine of the Army as a means of self-defence must be. abjured as completely as the lust' of conquest. Perhaps a memorable day will come when a bation renowned in wars and Victories, distinguished by the highest develop- ments of military order and intelligence, and accustomed to make the heaviest sacrifice to these objects, will voluntarily exclaim 'We will Veak our sword,' and will destroy its Whole military, lock, stock, and barrel. "Better to perish than to hate and fear, and twice as far better to perish than to make one- self hated and feared--z-this must some day Come the supreme maxim of every political community." There is none of the mailed fist or of the Prus- sian jackboot about Nietzsche's thoughts and writings. • ,

The Mailed Fist in England. My readers need not go one yard outside the British Isles to find the booted and spurred Junker. All the days of my manhood I have been fighting him. The Scotch crofter could tell yOu Of,his croft leased away from under his feet so that an American millionaire, could have sport. The Welsh tithe payer and the Passive Resister,could tell their views of "Might Mak- ing right." Last, hut not least, the 'collier and the factory hand know what the mailed fist means when it ,writes out the words--".A. Minimum Wage." ,

HENRY WHITHAM. San Francisco, November 2nd, 1914.

BILL'S PIPE.

Come of Bill? Do you E iee that there On the mantel-piece? Just 'and it 'ere! That's Bill's clay pipe: it ain't much good, But the bowl an' stem are stained wi' blood. Bill was an 'ero, people said; Well, that's 'is pipe; but Billy's dead. I don't know 'ow it come to pass, But Billy an' me loved the same sweet lass; But which of the two she loved the best Nobody know, an' nobody guesst. She kissed old Bill, an' she kissed me, too, When we went to bid 'or a last adieu. She was only a lass of eighteen years, But she 'old our 'ands through :er falling tears, "Be true to each other, whate'er betide, Shoulder to shoulder, an' side by side! God 'elp you, my lads, to win the fight, An' keep Old England's honour bright." We fought in many a deadly fray, At Mons an' Marne, an' then Cambray; No matter what we heered an' sor,— This ain't no 'istory of the war,— Let the darkness 'ide the ghastly sight. Though it comes to 'aunt me in the night, But fades wi' the peaceful morning light. One day we was drorn up in a line To storm the trenches: Said Bill, " It's fine! Now we shall make the Germans feel The taste of good, sharp English steel, Strike 'ome, my lad, an' make 'ern squeal!" An' they got it, too, not 'arf, you bet, For we owed them beggars a 'eavy debt; They fell in 'undreds, wounded, dead, An' the rest of them took to their 'eels an' fled: , But their guns kept up a 'eavy fire, An' we got the word, "My lads, retire!" They followed us up wi' shot and shell, The shrapnel mowed us down like 'ell, An' soon with a groan I staggered an' fell. But Bill he seen me an' urried back, "Buck up!" see he, "I'll save you, Jack !" He lifted me up, a battered wreck, An' I flung my arms round Billy's neck. Bray,. as a lion he staggered along, Never 'eeding the shrapnel's song, Till we reached the trenenos safe an' sound, An' Billy be laid me on the ground, An' then sank down in a deadly swound. I thought he was dead, an' took 'is 'and, I could do no more, for I couldn't stand; An' then I saw 'is bleeding side, An' is khaki tunic all stained an' dyed. But I couldn't stop the crimson tide. At last old Bill he opened 'is ?yes, An' looked at me in glad surprise Cheery •as ever he smiled an' spoke. I say, old chap, I want a smoke!" I found 'is pipe, an' baccy, too. An'. struck a light in a sec or two. He smoked like a good 'un, Billy did, An' as for rue, I took a quid! Silent an' still old Billy' lay, An' I knew 'is thoughts were far away, But what he saw I never knew, Per'aps the face of a girl in blue Who kissed 'im once, an' eld 'is 'and When he went to fight for Motherland. But all of a sudden he raised 'is 'ead,— " What were the words the lassie said?— Be true to each other whate'er betide, Shoulder to shoulder, an' side by side! God 'elp you, my lads, to yin the fight, An' keep Old England's honoAr Uright I Tell her I tried without a •lout, An' now,—Good-bye! My pipe is out!" Oh, shame upon England, shame, shame, , shame, If she sells 'or 'onour for a game! You may bring your troops from the seven seas,— An' England 'as need. o' men like these,— But she needs more sons like brave old Bill, Gallant of 'cart an' strong of will, "To keep the old flag flyin' still! You asked o' Bill, an' I've told you all, 'Ow 'e gave his life at 'onour's call. But what of the lass? Well, let that rest, But I think she loved old Billy best, For she keeps 'is photo in her breast. • ' Per'aps, some day, when the time is ripe, Fll•finish the story o' Billy's vine. ALFRED CORNY,

KILLED WHILE CYCLING. Inquest on the Late Mr. D. H. - Greenman.

At the LymmPolice Station on SatUrday, Mr_ E. A. White (deputy coroner) held ,tin inquest on the 1)64 of David Henry Greenman, of Booths Hill, Lymm, whose death had taken ,place the previous Thursday as a result of a fall whilst cycling down Station Brow.

On His Way to Work. Mary Elizabeth Greenman, the widow, said her husband had been employed as a time- keeper by the Manchester Ship Canal Company. He left'hume for his work on Thursday morn- ing at about ten minutes to six. He was then in his usual health. She next. saw 'him about eight o'clock in Dr. Thorpe's surgery. Later he was conveyed home, and died at nine o'clock in the eVening. He had ridden a bicycle for several years.

How the Accident Happened. , Henry Joseph Broome, a waterman, of Canal Bank, said that he, in company with another waterman—George Dodson, of Rush Green— noticed Mr. Greenman cycling down Station Brow at half-past six on the morning in ques- tion. It was then quite dark. They both moved to allow the cyclist to pass, and he turned as if to avoid them, but fell off.. Dodson then sum- moned Dr. Thorpe. George -godson gave similar evidence.

1

The Cause of Death. T-Dr. Thorpe said that arriving upon ,the scene, he found Greenman lying alive but uncon-- -scions upon the footpath. De had the man carried into his surgery. Greenman was bleed- ing from the nose, and there was subcutaneous licemorrhage under and around the eyelids. The rapidity with which the bleeding took place pointed to extensive injury. Witness haTh charge of the man until half-past eight, at which time Dr. Burrows—deceased's medical attendant —arrived. The patient was removed home -about noon 7 Death was• due to extensive hemorrhage at the front of the base of the skull, caused by heavy and direct.v,iolenee: such as might have been the result of the fait

A verdict of "Accidental death" was re- turned, the jury requesting the coroner to con- vey their deepest sympathy to the.widow.

4. 4. • • CHAT and GOSSIP.

• since the war much of this supply has stopped and the very few British manufacturers have heen,overwhelmed with orders. Perhaps if once the trade is recovered it may be possible to keep it in the country. - In this, as in glove-making, bead and button-making, and many other minor industries, it is the low wages and long hours, of the workers abroad, that give the foreign manufacturer the advantage.

Women Voluntary Aid Detachments. The Territorials, as is well known, mobilised their twenty-three hospitals when war broke out, but these were not enough, and no fewer than forty-eight auxiliary hospitals have, been neces- sary to meet requirements. These are under the St. John Ambulance Association, and receive from twenty to eighty patients each. Local committees watch over them, and women who have gone through the course of training laid down by the association are able here to render the aid so many are anxious to give, although they have not acquired a practical knowledge of nursing. Voluntary Aid Detachments of women are- doing good work by providing rest stations in connection with the transport of the wounded. At junctions, or on short delays, tea, soup, warm milli and cocoa, with other comes- tibles, are always ready to be served to the wounded in need of such comforts.

Tea Gowns and Dressing Jackets. The tea gown is a garment that has been trans- fbrmed by the war. It is, except in a few cases, a simple, practical, but always comfortable affair, in which it is possible to rest. It has now a short skirt, or rather one that just clears the ground, and is rarely made with a train as for- merly. A girdle often confines the waist, which is always very loose. Ruches are used as trim- mings, and when composed of soft satin ribbon in two colours, are very effective at the neck and sleeves; sometimes they are used to outline the waist, and to trim the skirt. A cosy dressing jaiket is a necessity in cold weather, and a very pfetty new model is made in the cross-over style, the ends tying behind. A delightful wool crepe is among the lovely new materials that British manufacturers have designed especially for these useful garments. Some of these materials ap- pear to have velvet flowers embroidered on them, but a close scrutiny reveals the fact that they

aro in washable wools, swansdown, nari w edg- ings of white fur, or frills of chiffon, trim these dainty models.

The Prevalence of Ermine.

Ermine has always been considered expen- sive fur, but its general \use this winter seems to point to the opposite being the case, since scarcely an afternoon or evening gown, or a hat, is without a touch of this dainty fur, or a clever imitation of it. Yet it is not a particularly be- coming daylight pelt; nor does it combine well with certain colours or with some materials. When soiled—and it quickly attracts the dirt of the city—it has a dingy appearance, but if re- served for evening wear only, it is handsome and fascinating. It is undoubtedly at its best when combined with black velvet, and looks well as a turn-down collar on any short-haired black fur. Whole skins are often used for this pur- pose. Milliners, too, twist it into the form of a buckle as a decoration for a small black velvet hat. Muffs of tailless ermine, of white fox, or of any white fur, are much used for evening or theatre wear, and are quite an addition to a dainty toilette.

Useful Renovations. Broad braid is used as a bordering for many coats, also for belts, for which it is particularly suitable. It is often employed in conjunction with fur. Frogged ornamentations are also seen on many coats, more especially on those of a military type, such as the Hussar, which is very smartly trimmed with braid. If an open coat .is to be brought up-to-date, braid is of great consequence in effecting its transformation. Thus the open neck and revers of a double-breasted coat may be closed if a piece of braid is put down the front, the fastening, as before, being at the side. The addition of a close-fitting fur col- lar, and cuffs for the sleeves, make of it the newest thing in this season's tailor-mades. These fur collars add considerably to the com- fort of a winter garment, and a closed coat is warmer than an open one. Much may be done to brighten a dark suit or frock, by judicious touches of bright Oriental colours, -or bril- liantly-coloured flowers worn at the corsage, or on dark furs. Artificial flowers this year are exceptionally beautiful and brilliant, and not expensive.

By MARJORIE.

Christmas Cards and Cosaques. The royal Family is making no exception this season i a sending out the usual Christmas and New Year greetings, but from the public gener- ally there has been a great falling off in the practice of sending Christmas cards, and in the buying of crackers. Few people realise how much thought, skill and ingenuity go to the making of these goods. Clever artists design covers for boxes and pictures for crackers, as well as schemes for table decoration& . No sooner is one Christmas over than the stock for the next festival is begun, with the result that by August all is in readiness for the salesman. This year business during that fateful month— in crackers, at any rata—was at a complete standstill, and for the most part the designs were quite unsuitable for a nation engaged in a life-and-death struggle for existence. There has been little or no demand for the familiar cosaque, which is usually such a conspicuous feature at a Christmas party.

The Artificial Flower Industry. Artificial flower-making is one of the many . industries which to a great extent have been captured by Germany and Austria. It was orig- inally introduced into this country by French refugees, and France still excels in the art of .fiower-making, but for some time past British manufacturers, with a few exceptions, have been ,content with importing and mounting, instead of making the blossoms. Children, as well as women and girls, are largely employed in this industry in Germany and Austria, but they are miserably paid, 'and the goods are sold at prices -at which it is difficult for our manufacturers to -compete. More attention has been given abroad to the production of special kinds of cambric .and dyes, and the hands are very expert at the work. In the ordinary way we import artificial ;flowers to the value of .t 250,000 annually, but

HOME NOTES.

Profitable Recipes and Useful Suggestions.

Dried fruits, such as dates, sultanas. figs, ex., ,eaten raw or stewed, with wholemeal bread, are ,enjoyed by children, and form an excellent meal. Fresh salads of uncooked vegetables, such as ,cabbage, red or white, onions, carrots. turnips, &c., grated, or finely shredded, are wholesome, 'cheap and purifying. ,

Onion Soup. Peel and cut up rather small three good-i.l 'onions, and cook them in 2 oz. of clariliNl drip- ping in a covered pan for one holfr til! tender, -then moisten with one and a half pints of hot water in which a cauliflower or beans have been cooked, or even plain water; add some slices of bread cut diagonally, and season with pepper and salt; then cover down the pan again, and let the contents simmer for at least an hour. Now crush the onions, Sze., through a sieve (this can be quite well omitted for ordinary pur- poses), and just before serving add gradually a tablespoonful of grated cheese, stirring this rapidly into the soup to ensure it blendim:.

To Take Stains Outof Skirts. If these stains are dried mud, wash with a -little soapy water to which you have added a 'few drops of ammonia. If grease, soak some fuller's earth in turpen- i ine until you have .a thick cream. spreild on the stain, and leave until it is dry. Then brush off. If the ,stain has not quite gone, try again. If candle-grease; use a piece of brown paper and a hot iron. If tar, rub on a little lard, leave for a time, and wash in warm, soapy water.

Stuffed Shoulder of Mutton. Have the blade bone removed from the shoulder, then stuff the cavity with the foil:w- ing mixture: One cupful of bread:renths, one tablespoonful of finely chopped parsley, one tea- spoonful of chopped onion, one tea- spoonful of salt, one-third of a teaspoonful of pepper, half dozen chopped fresh mushrooms (if obtainable), and sufficient melted butter— about one-quarter of a cupful—to moisten. Sew or fasten up the opening. Put a bed of vegetables in the bottom of the pan, and on this lay the meat. Pour in me cupf of

water or stock, cover closely, and place in a Ivit oven for half an hour. Uncover and finish the cooking, basting well. Thicken, season and strain the gravy, and send to the table in a boat.

Steamed Halibut.

Take a, three-pound piece of white halibut, cover it with a cloth, and place in a steamer. Set the steamer on a dish of fast-boiling water, steam for two hours, remove to a hot dish, gar- nish with parsley, and serve with egg sauce.

HERE AND THERE.

What Men Would Like to Know About Women. As she has so slight a sense of humour, what animates her frequent laughing and smiles? Is it serious, or only affectation of mirth or amia- bility? ' Why does she remove her coat and hang it over her arm when she steps on the scales to be weighed? Is it true that .she believes the advertisements on the panels of cars? Why is she so afraid of a harmless mouse aad without fear of a dreadful man? Are her guesses better than man's reason? If one would securely hide a secret from her, would it not be well to incorporate it in an editorial article? Why is it that she has no concern for past or future, but only for the present? If she were never born, bow would man fare? Is.it mot so, that with all her faults.we love her still—the stiller the better?

The Wedding Ring. Society wonieji in Denver have been looking into the history\ of the wedding ring and their researches have disclosed that the finger on which the circlet is worn was chosen because it is supposed to contain the blood vein that runs directly to the heart. One woman who looked the subject up for her club reported that the old rhyme, " As your ring wears, you'll live out your cares," aided her in learning the evolution of the practice that distinguishes married women from the single ones. The wedding ring has undergone many changes from the one of iron to the splendid beveled gold circlet that iS worn to-day, but the wedding ring finger always has remained the same. The origin of the bridal veil is a subject of dispute in women's clubs. The Denver woman asserts Mary Tudor wore the first wedding veil in Western Europe.

HINTS ON HEALTH. Useful Information in Popular

Language.

Strength With Age. At 70, General George A. Wingate, lawyer and sportsman, abandoned tennis. He still played golf, walked 15 miles in a day through the forest, field and swamp, with gun on shoulder and dog at heel, and waded mountain streams with his trout rod in his hand. If other men would adopt his system, the General said, they would be just as hale- and hearty and full of fun and life at the threescore and ten mark. Here is his system s—Take "setting up" exercises 15 minutes, night and morning, before an open window. Keep the pores of the skin open by scrubbing them every day with a flesh brush. Take plenty of outdoor exercise. Have some fun. If you're getting along toward the 50 mark associate with young people. Sleep well. Eat and drink moderately. Don't worry. Take up some altruistic hobby. Men getting along in years can often do more and better work by working five instead of six days a week. Prevention of a Cold.

A person who habitually "catches cold" should have his nose and throat thoroughly ex- amined. The nasal, post nasal, and accessory sinuses should receive particular attention, and any necessary operation should not be shunned, in order that these parts should be placed in thorough working order. Have a good supply of fresh, pure air at all times. Vitiated and con- taminated air tends to infection by lowering the local resisting powers. Hot, crowded, badly- ventilated rooms, halls, or theatres, predispose likewise. The healthy action of the skin should be maintained. A cold rub down with quick reaction is a splendid prevention of colds. Clothing should be regulated according to the demands of the weather. Just enough should be worn to keep one comfortably warm. Too much clothing conduces to infection as well as too little. Direct infection should be carefully guarded against. Keep your distance from a person who sneezes or coughs, especially in the first stages of a cold. Likewise should one in- fected avoid close contact with others. Keep the nose and mouth always clean by washing them out occasionally with a good antiseptic gargle. Fatigue, hunger, mental depression, and anxiety favour infection, while a happy, jovial, optimistic temperament has much to do in preventing infection.—" Good Health."

CHRISTMAS AT LAST.

MY DEAR CHILDREN,-

A cheerful Christmas to us all, with many a happy one to follow! May it bring to us, and each of us, the recollection of those blessings we enjoy as Christians, and as the subjects of a Christian country, great and glorious. May it rekindle in our hearts that hope of peace which so swiftly passes from us. May we learn to know its worth. Well, this is our Christmas Corner; and I trust it will prove to your liking. I now give the results of the Christmas competition, and I am very pleased to say that there has been a large number of competitors. The essays, gen- erally, show that great care has been taken in preparing them; and the writing is very neat in almost every case. One or two of those who •sent in essays, however, missed the point, which had, you remember, to deal with: "How should I spend this Christmas?" You all know why this Christmas is different from the usual ones, don't you? Perhaps you are expecting me to give you my own ideas about the subject now? Well, I won't: because if you read the winning essay you will know exactly what my views are—and so I need not give them here. I congratulate my successful little niece upon her nice essay:, she will receive the prize--a book—in time for Christmas Day, as a useful present. With the closing words of "Good Cheer" to all who have not this time been quite successful, and with the hope that they will persevere until Dame Fortune smiles at last upon them, I now leave you to enjoy this Christmas Corner. A cheerful Christmas to us all!

Your loving

===== 0002601_19141226_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
UNCLE NED.

THE WINNING ESSAY.

Dear Uncle Ned,—You ask me how I should spend this Christmas? Well, when I first looked at it, I thought: What a simple question to ask! But it is not so easy, after all; it is not how I should like, but how I should. Well, I do not think it would be right this Christmas for me to make it a day of pleasure, but to be quiet and thoughtful, and not selfish. When I hear my father read about the poor Belgian children, many who have lost both parents, and who will be in strange homes and in strange lands this Christmas; I think I should go to chapel in the morning, to thank God that I am an English girl, and to ask God to send peace to earth and good-will to all men quickly. In the afternoon I think I should write a long cheery letter to my brother, Joseph, who is training in Ireland, and who is shortly going to the Front. Then I should help mother to get the tea ready and to wash the dishes and straighten up the kitchen. Then I think I should sit down and read my prize which I shall get from Sunday School for regular attendance, and when bedtime comes I should kneel down by my bedside and ask God to take care of our soldiers and sailors, and ask Him to bring them all safe home again. And I should pray for their wives and little children, that they may not be sad or frightened, but that when another Christmas comes round they, along with myself, will have their loved ones with them again.— NELLIE MASON, 174, Longford-street, Warrington.

RIDDLES ON THE WAR.

Why have the Germans the largest fleet of air- ships?—Because they are the biggest "gas-bags." Why does theaiser want to rule the North Sea?—Because it's the German notion (ocean).

TURKEY, NOT LARK.

The captain of a well-known football team had a fine turkey presented to him, as a Christ- mas gift, by the other members of the team. On going, a little time later, to the bag in which he had placed the turkey, he found to his sur- prise that it had gone. Suspecting three com- panions who were fond of a joke, he went and asked them about it; and received the reply:

"It was only a lark." "Lark be bothered," replied the captain; "it weighed twenty pounds."

WOMEN IN WAR. In days of old, when women served their country in war, they undertook the same service as men: they went into the field and fought: in some cases they were born commanders. Deborah, Boadicea, and Joan of Arc must have had military genius of a high order, for men followed them willingly; and in every count. 7 there are tales of bands of heroic women who have taken part in fighting, generally in def,P.tice of their own cities and homes. But the world is :.ot in that stage of evolution now when NY omen can best serve their country in the field. They are no longer called upon to take part in the physical conflict. Their task is different from that of combatants, but none the less real; and it has been very striking during this war how not only women themselves are conscious of their national responsibility, but that the whole com- munity has been conscious of it also, and his encouraged women to shoulder it couragoously and firmly. The general recognition of the national value of women's services in war time is comparatively new. The great pioneer was, of course, Florence Nightingale.—Mrs. Henry Faw- cett in the "Contemporary Review" for Dec- ember.

SOUTH AFRICAN REVOLT.

How to Deal With the Unsuccessful Rebels.

BOTHA AND THE "DEAD PAST."

General Botha states that now the rebellion is over, no fear need be entertained of a recrudes- cence of the trouble. The one thing now need- ful—and on this General Botha lays great stress—is that all sections of the loyal popula- tion, Dutch and English alike, should refrain from words or actions which might exacerbate feeling. lle says, in effect, "Let the dead past bury its dead." Continued denunciation of the rebels may wound just those whom Englishmen have no desire to wound. "For the loyalist Boer," he says, " in these days it has been an unhappy and, indeed, tragic ordeal to have to hunt down and fire upon men, some of them their relatives, and many of them their comrades in arms. These men in many cases have already %net their just punish- ment. Their wrong-doing and their fate are matters of the most acute grief to their kins- men, and bitterness may unwittingly be pro- voked if our English fellow-countrymen con- tinually emphasise to them .the infamy of acts which they are not alone in detesting. "The loyal commandos have had a hard task to perform, and they have performed it. The cause of law and order has been and will be vin- dicated. Let that be enough. This is no time for exultation or for recrimination. Let us spare one another's feelings. Remember we have to live together in this land long after the war is ended.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
UNITED AGAINST GERMAN FOE.

" In suppressing the rebellion the Government have had the most hearty co-operation of both races. Let us have the same co-operation in German South-West Africa. The undertaking before us is a difficult undertaking, but, if we all do our duty, it will be carried to a success- ful conclusion. Now that German territory has become a refuge forqlaritz and the other rebels it is more than ever necessary that we should persist in our operations there. We cannot tolerate the existence of a nest of out- laws on our frontier, a menace to the peace of the Union."

Speaking of the services rendered by General Smuts, General Botha declared: "Nobody can ever appreciate sufficiently the great work General Smuts has done—greater than any other man—throughout this unhappy period. At his post day and night, his brilliant intel- lect, his calm judgment, his amazing energy, and his undaunted courage, have been assets of inestimable value to the Union in her hour of trial."

===== 0002601_19141226_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
REBEL LEADER SHOT.

Fourie Court-martialled and Pleads

6uilty.

A field court martial, under the presidency of Colonel Truter, Commissioner of Police, was opened last Friday morning for the trial of the rebel leader -Fourie, who was captured on Decentber 16 with 44 other rebels, after a hot fight. - His brother was also placed on trial.

The one is a captain and the other a lieutenant in the Defence Force. Both pleaded guilty to rebellion. The elder prisoner made a statement in which, as a Dutchman, he bitterly reproached the British for their conduct in South Africa, and alleged that whenever they had the oppor- tunity,they injured the traditions of tee Boers.

lie decla-red. that no Englishman could feel other than he felt if similarly placed under a foreign Government, and he considered that it was a greater honour to stand there as pris- oner than as an officer of the British Army. It was his conviction that there were enough people on the veldt "to rescue South Africa."

- He did not intend to ask the Court for mercy for himself, but he strongly pleaded for mercy for his young brother, who was under his in- fluence, and also for other men under his influ- ence and command.

The court martial found that both brothers were guilty, but recommended Lieut. Fourie to mercy. _. . _

Capt.-Fourie was shot at dawn on Sunday. Sentence of death was also passed on his brother, Lieut. Fourie, but it has been com- muted to five years' imprisonment.

THE KING'S CHRISTMAS.

The Prime Minister called at Buckingham Palace on Tuesday evening, and was received in audience by the King. Mr. Asquith re- mained over half an hour. This, in view of his Majesty's prospective absence from town—, is likely to be the final conference of the current year between the King And his principal ad- viser.

His Majesty held a Privy Council at Buck- ingham Palace on Wednesday. In the afternoon the King and Queen, with those of their children now in town, left for York Cottage, Sandringham, where they will spend Christmas very quietly. Queen Alexandra remains in London for Christmas.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
TRAGEDY FOLLOWS TRAGEVY.

Former Officer's Death at Private's Funeral. Captain James Grant Malcolmson, of the National Reserve of Territorials and formerly captain in the 3rd Battalion Gordon High- landers, died suddenly while attending, in Pad- dington Cemetery on Tuesday, the funeral of one of his men accidentally killed last week while guarding the railway at Wimbledon. Arriving at the graveside Captain Malcolmson fell forward and died almost immed4ately in the arms of his son. He was 52 years of age, and was the eldest son of Captain Malcolmson, V.C., late of the Royal Bodyguard, and cousin of Lord Erskine.

ANGLO-RUSSIAN RELATIONS.

Authors' Appreciative Address.

WHAT WILL HAPPEN AFTER THE WAR?

Leading British authors have addressed a striking manifesto to "Our colleagues in Russia."

"At this moment," the statement says, "when your countrymen and ours are alike facing death for the deliverance of Europe, we English men of letters take the opportunity of uttering to you 'feelings which have been in our hearts for many years. You yourselves, per- haps, hardly realise what an inspiration Eng- lishmen of the last two generations have found in your ,literature.

"Many a writer among us can still call back from ten or twenty or thirty years ago that feeling of delight and almost of bewilderment with which he read his first Russian novel. Perhaps it was "Virgin Soil" or "Fathers and Sons," perhaps "War and Peace" or "Anna Karenina," perhaps "Crime and Punishment" or "The Idiot," perhaps again it was the work of some author still living. . . . "When at last our victorious fleets and armies meet together and the allied nations of East and West set themselves 'to restore the well- being of many millions of ruined homes, France and Great Britain will assuredly bring their large contributions of good will and. wisdom, but your country will have something to contri- bute which is all its own. It is ,not only because of your valour in war and your achieve- ment in.art, science and letters that we rejoice to have you for allies and friends, it is for some quality in Russia herself, something both pro- found and humane, of which these achievements are the outcome and the expression. "You, like us, entered upon this war to defend a weak and threatened nation, which trusted you against the lawless aggression of a strong military Power; you, like us, have continued I it as a war of self-defence and self-erhancipa. tion. When the end comes arid we can breathe again we will help one another to remember the I spirit in which our allied nations took up arms I and thus work together in a changed Europe to protect the weak, to liberate the oppressed and to bring eventual healing to the wounds in- flicted on suffering mankind both .by ourselves and our enemies. _

" With assurances of our 'friendship and gratitude we sign ourselves: -

THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, DECEMBER 26, 1911.

FREEMEN TURMOIL IN TIIE COUNCIL.

At last night's meeting of the Council the proposal to make six new Freemen of the Borough was rejected by fifteen votes to thirteen. As it is necessary to have a majority of two to one to make a Freeman, it will readily be seen how hopeless were the chances of success on the part of the promoters. SIR GILBERT Gnr.ENALL wrote declining the honour, as the time was inopportune, and Mr. CROSFIELD wrote urging post- ponement till happier days. The pro- moters persisted, however, and the town was provided with an experience ,it could well have avoided. The adop- tion of the advice "The Examiner" gave a month ago would have saved much unnecessary "turmoil," as the MAYOR fittingly called the dispute, but —the advice was not taken.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
THROUGH THE FIRE.

Last Christmas we -ventured to draw attention to the paradox of a world pro- fessing Christianity and formally recOg- nising its great festival of peace and good-will when the leading nations were demanding arms, and yet more arms, for possible, if not probable, use upon their neighbours. That is only . twelve short months ago, in five of which the greatest war of all time has been raging. If we except the Turk, it would, be fair to say that the peoples who are now flying at each others' throats and killing or maiming one another by the million are among what we called the most highly civilised peoples of the globe. The . heathen Chinee and the unspeakable Turk never did anything so heathenish or so incom- prehensible as the things now being (lone in Europe in the name of Culture. While we are reflecting upon the sad fact Mat this Christmas few homes will be left untouched in our world-wide Empire, across- the immeasurable regions of Russia, in the extensive territories of Austria-Hungary, the broad acres of Germany, the fertile lands of France, the once happy Bel- gium, distracted Servia, and rugged Montenegro, we can, at all events, rejoice in one fact—that, this, welter of bloodshed is none of our seeßing. When the war broke out the case of England was stated fearle-sly and fully,

and that case has stood the attack of the enemy just as our troops have stood the onslaughts of the hordes of trained soldiers who have been hurled at them in all but overwhelming and irresistible numbers. In the field and in the Coun- cil Chamber we have remained un- ihaken and unshakable. All neutral :ountries have acknowledged the ustice of our position, and, as a matter if fact, our position before the eyes of he world grows stronger as time goes on And obscure points become clear. We have indeed been going through the fire in a double sense, and it is a matter for congratulation that we are emerging from the ordeal stronger and, it is to be hoped, purer for the process. Our Navy has maintained its proud role that enables Britain to proclaim herself mistress of the seas ' • our Army has added to its glorious traditions. But great as have been the triumphs of our arms on sea and. on land, they have been excelled in the other and perhaps a more important direction: 'We refer to the moral side of the question, the side which will make a special appeal to the imagination of us all with greater force in the midst of this Christian fes- tival. Never did the reputation of England—and We use the name Eng- land as signifying the heart of the great living and expanding Empire of which she is the c'entre—stand so high as it does to-day. In the midst of unprece- dented prosperity we stepped aside from our occupations and our profits, and risked our existence in an encoun- ter with an oppressor of small States, and to vindicate by all .the power that we could command the word we had given to a friend.. It has been a long, a bitter, and a terrible struggle,. The end, is not yet------but we. shall, go on- to • the end. It is the knowledge of that determination on our part that has heartened our Allies, who are going on with us to the end, however distant it may be. The strength of our position may easily be proved by the employment of a few negatives. It has not been neces- sary for us to make a formal raid upon neutral countries with the object of courting their good opinion • we have stated our case, and left it .ill the assurance , that an honest tale speeds best being plainly told. We have not bombarded undefended coast towns with mighty , naval guns, nor dropped murderous bombs from the clouds upon unsuspect- ing and sleeping cities. We have not, in sheer desperation, had to ally our- selves with the Turk„at once the scum of Asia and the abomination of Europe, who will soon be pitched finally upon the scrap-heap of Empires for which the world has no further use. It will soon be our privilege to carry the war into the enemy's country. When we get there we shall show the KAISER and his barbarians the measure of our moral superiority, as we have already shown him the measure of .our predominance on land and sea and in the air. Such sins have been committed against man and before GOD by the Germans as call for vengeance, but our greatest moral strength will be proved by the restraint that we shall show. We shall not be arson-mongers and murderers when we enter Germany. We have,a reputation at stake, and we shall be on our guard against lowering that reputation to the level of the German reputation. One great nation is enough for Europe to have grovelling in the mire, and we must leave Germany gasping alone in the slough of dishonour in whieh' she has involved herself. Fortified towns will\ doubtless be bombarded, and we , shall go to the extremities allowed by the rules and usages of war, having been forced into war, but we shall not rhurder, burn and ravage. That is a -comforting reflection at this time of the year. • , War may seem a queer 'theme for Christmas Eve treatment, but there are -many, and we include ourselves in the many. who believe that out of this colossal wrong will come great and last- ing good. We now know that Germany was plotting for world-wide power, and we have learnt by sad experience how the Huns applY the power that comes . their way. We have attacked that power before it has got beyond our strength ; andy under the guidance of PROVIDENCE, we shall assist in putting the world on a better and surer founda- tionit from the menace of thesb\JO' rffLeTlig ich has acted as a brake upon, the wheel of social progress for more than ath generatio, At home a danger has welded together common all classes as nothing else could have done ; we now see clearly that the interest of one is the interest :of all. There will .be less grumbling in future about the call that is being increasingly made upon .us 'to bear one another's -burdens. The good time coining is coming all the more quickly for the affliction from which we now suffer.

ER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 26, 1914.

The Mayor (Alderman Peter Peacock) having received a requisition signed by nineteen mem- bers of the Council to call a special meeting for the purpose of considering the creation of several Freemen, his Worship called the meet-. jug for last (Wednesday) night. The notice convening the meeting stated that it would be

For the purpose of passing a resolution to confer the Freedom of the Borough, under and pursuant to the Act 48 and 49 Victoria, cap. 29, on the following gentlemen, viz. Alderman T. Burton, Alderman W. Bolton, Alderman J. Smethurst, Alderman W. Pierpoint, Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., C.V.0., and A. H. Crosfield, Esq., in recognition of eminent services rendered by them to the borough.

The requisition was signed by the following : Councillors Archer, Pemberton, Fox, Cunning- ton, Gough, Chrimes, Jenkins, Lashford, Osborne, Skelland, Barton, Chantler, McKee, Hal Ewell, Broadhurst, Shaw, Hawthorn, Tay- lor and Henshall.

His Worship presided, and there were also present: Aldermen Bolton, Pierpoint, Bennett, James Evans. Tinnion, John Evans and Smeth- urst; Councillors Craik, Lindley, Barton, Skel- land, Shaw, Locker, Joseph, Jenkins, Roberts, Broadhurst, Gough, Chrimes, Chantler, Archer, Lashford, Cunnington, Osborne, Hawthorn, Pemberton, Wilkinson, Dickenson, McKee, and Henshall; Mr. J. Lyon Whittle (town clerk), and Mr. J. Fairhurst (borough treasurer).
