SIR GILBERT GREENALL DECLINES Says Time Is Inopportune.

The MAYOR read the following letter which he had received from Sir Gilbert Greenall:—

December 20th, 1914.

• Dear Mr. Mayor, I see from the "Warrington Guardian" that my name is mentioned amongst those selected for the Freedom of the Borough. This is the first and only intimation I have had on the subject, and as the proposal does not seem to have general support I should be obliged if you will kindly delete my name from the list. I thi:ll the time inopportune for the bestowal of civic honours, and I scarcely know on what grounds I am consid- ered eligible for so high a distinction. As the matter came before me through the Press, I am sending a copy of this letter to the "Guardian" for publication.—Yours faithfully, (Signed) GILBERT GREENALL.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. CROSFIELD ASKS FOR POSTPONEMENT.

The Mayor said he had also received a letter From Mr. A. 11. Crosfield as follows : December 21st, 1914. The Right Worshipful Mayor of Warrington. My Dear Mr. Mayor—As I am informed there is at the moment a strong difference of opinion about the creation of Freemen, will you not use your influence with our friends in both political parties who are urging the question, to persuade them to leave it in abey- ance? The delay would probably make all the difference to those who have objected, and in that way a unanimous decision of the Council may be arrived at.—Very faithfully

yours. A. IL CROSPIELD• lie (the Mayor) had told Sir Gilbert Greenall that his name could not be deleted from the list, and therefore the proposal would have to come before the Council, in accordance with the notice which had been given. Councillor JENKINS : Will you allow me, Mr. Mayor The MAYOR: No. Councillor JENKINS : It is only The MAYOR: No. I call upon Councillor Archer.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PROPOSAL.

Councillor ARCHER formally moved the adop- tion of the resolution as set out on the notice. He thought the present time was most oppor- tune to confer this honour upon the gentlemen named. The borough was incorporated in 1847, and, although there had been great progress in the meantime, only 'ton individuals had been made Freemen. They had been backward in conferring these honours for those who had served the borough. In 1897 they made five of their ten Freemen, which proved that on this occasion they were not making these honours too easy. Two of their Freemen were yet alive—Mr. Charlton Parr and Mr. Robert Pier- point. Mr. Pierpoint had been, a fervent worker for their welfare, and the same applied to Parr. It was argued that, war being on, the time was inopportune, but Parliament told them to go on with their business as usual, and so show the Germans they were not down- hearted. As to the relative merits of the men put forward for this honour, Alderman Burton had been in the Council for seventeen Years, and outside, in connection with the Infirmary , and other work, had done much good, and blushed to find it fame. Alderman Bolton had served them for 31 years and had done solid, sound work for the Corporation and town. Alderman Smethurst had placed the Electricity and Tramways Committee on a sound and solid bed-rock. His social life revealed him as a man who stood high in the estimation of his fellow-men. Alderman William Pierpoint, the veteran, had been a stedfast friend of the town for the whole of his life. It was said they should wait until the war was over, but the war might last twenty years. Sir Gilbert Greenall was one of those who was added to the list as what might be regarded as an after- thought. Sir Gilbert was a proud son of War- rington, who had had such honours conferred upon him that anything they could confer was quite small. Mr. Arthur Crosfield was con- nected with a worthy family and had served the town in Parliament the same as Mr. Pier- point, who had already received the honour. lie hoped the Council would do justice to these men, who had served the town faithfully and well. (Applause.) Councillor LASIIFORD seconded. COUNCILLOR IMOADHURST SUG- GESTS OTHER NAMES.

Councillor BROADHURST said he was surprised to hear the letter from Sir Gilbert Oreenall, and he was of the pinion that Sir Gilbert had been misinformed as regarded the general opin- ion of the town. He (the speaker) might. not move amongst the classes, but he moved amongst the masses. (Hear, hear.) He had never heard one single expression of opinion against the gentlemen named being made Freemen of the Borough. (Hear, hear.) The only thing he had heard was that a few members of the Council had tried to turn public opinion against those men being made Freemen simply because the names had been introduced by certain members of the Council. Objections were raised that the time was not opportune. The greatest orders were conferred upon Generals Joffre and French on the field of battle. The King did not wait until the whole of operations were finished be- fore lie bestowed the honours on those men who deserved it. (Hear, hear.) The King had also conferred the " V.C." on the Commander of Submarine 1311. lie did not wait until the time was " opportune." He would like to see other names on the list; and if he had attended the General Purposes Committee he would have pro- posed the, name of one of Warrington's most

gallant men—Captain Crosfield. And the time was not far distant when he shoilld The MAYOR: Order! • - • • • --

--- Councillor BROADHURST : And there is another gentleman whose name I should have brought forward—that of Councillor Roberts. His action during the last war endeared him to me for all time. The MAYOR: Keep to the question. C 010161101" BROADHURST then said that Sir Gil- bert had not refused the honour; he had simply asked that his name should be deleted, and he (the speaker) hoped that it would not be deleted. Councillor GOUGH next spoke, his remarkif giving rise to frequent interruptions and inter- jections, and necessitated the free use by the Mayor of the order-bell, despite the fact that the speaker assured his Worship and the Coun- cil in general that he knew what he was talking about, and that whatever he said should be! common-sense. " The progressive members at the General Purposes Committee meeting," he ! said, " discussed the nomination of Aldermen Bolton, Burton, Smethurst and Pierpoint. Did we object to calling a special meeting of the Council to confer the honorary Freedom of the Borough on each of those gentlemen? The only members of the Council who spoke against the selection were Alderman Tinnion. and Council- lors Wilkinson and Lindley. They desired to have their names—other names—put fm-ward. If these men had generously received our selec- tion and proposed their own selection the mat- ter might have ended in peace, and wiCiout trouble. Alderman Tin nion suggested or pro- posed that Councillor Roberts should be added, but the progressives refused to support this resolution, and since then the Roberts-ites have put the cat among the pigeons. (Laughter.) They have tried to put the clock back ten years. We have witnessed actions of spleen and jeal- ousy and selfishness. Is no man ever to be con- sidered save Councillor Roberts?" COUNCILLORS GOUGH AND ROBERTS.

Councillor ROBERTS (to the Mayor) : I must ask you to stop this kind of persunal remark. It is not in any sense connected with the sub- ject before the meeting. (Hcar, hear.) The MAYOR: Councillor Gough is referring to statements made at the General Purposes Com- ruittee's meeting, Councillor ROBERTS: was not present. The MAYOR: lie is speaking the truth when he says that your name was mentioned: Councillor ROBERTS: That is a fact, I know; but I will ,not allow either you or Councillor Gough The MAYOR rang his bell. Councillor GOUGH : Don't offer the Mayor insult. Councillor ROBERTS: Well, don't insult me. Councillor GOUGLI : You are the!first to insult. (To the Council) : They are 'trying to push him through. Again the MAYOR rang his bell, adding the observation: "That is your idea. Go on with something else now !" Councillor Gouon went on to say that the names placed before the'Council that night were those of men who were doing honest work for the town without pay or reward, and some of at great cost and them personal sacrifice. "I have known Councillor Roberts for fourteen. years"lie added, when The MAYOR again interrupted, observing "I told you to drop his name. We have had quite enough from you. You must take notice of tde, or you will not be allowed to proceed." Councillor Gouon : I expect I must obey your ruling and keep out personalities, although there are personal matters. Each one of these men in the Council who have raised opposition to these four or six gentlemen are simply jealous. They won't let others have the honour because others won't propose them. (Laughter.) ALDERMAN TINNION AND THE PREVAILING " SPIRIT."

Alderman TINNION said that never during his experience in that Council Chamber had he ap- proached a matter which, in his opinion, was of such supreme importance as the issue which they were discussing that evening. It was a most important and momentous question, affecting the honour and dignity of the town, the Mayor and the Council, and also, the dignity of the gentlemen whose names had been proposed. He did We that they would have been able to keep themselves free from the unfortunate and miser- able positioh in which they were placed that night. He had hoped that it would be possible to postpone that important decision during the continuance of the war, and it had been his in- tention to propose the adjournment of the mat- ter—to move it as a previous question, without going into the personal element at all, and ' theleby relieve everyone of a very unfortunate difficulty. But the Mayor had informed him that if he took that action with the intention of relieving the strain, it would only mean that, say. next week another requisition would be! presented asking him to call another meeting to discuss the matter. Under those circumstances it was not worth while now to attempt to come to any amicable, reasonable settlement, not- withstanding the very great importance of the question to all concerned. There was not one single gentleman vtrho had been proposed for the honour of thi Freedom of the borough against whom he was going to say one word, either on the score of honour, character or ability, but his great objection to proceeding further in the matter that night was on account of the inopportuneness of the time for confer- ring such. honours. He called the letter from Sir Gilbert Greenall to witness to that. The time was inopoth.ne for the bestowal of civic honours.

Councillor GOUGH : Why? Alderman TiNavioN: I will tell you, Mr. Coun- cillor Gough. Councillor Goran : Because you are squab- blers. Alderman Tomotsi : We are now having a sample of the spirit and disposition in which the matter has been discussed from its very linception—(hear, hear)—and on other occasions when the public and the Press have not been here that spirit has been more virulent, vio- lent, disgusting and objectionable than it is to- night. It had been said, the Aiderman con- tinued, that it would be opportune to confer these honours because honours had been con- ferred in connection with Pie terrible struggle at present going on—the most terrible struggle that ever any nation had for an existence; one of the greatest struggles that civilisation and Christianity had ever witnessed. The King had thought fit to confer certain hopours, not only in recognition of the gallant serVices rendered by so many splendid fellows at the front, but also as an incentive to others to go and do their duty as nobly, but it was absurd to argue that as a reason why that Council should be sitting there to pat other people at home on the back. lle agreed with the motto "Business as usual," and it was "Business as usual" that he wanted in this Matter, and he contended that it would not be acting up to that motto to confer the pro- posed honours, seeing that it was as far back as 1907 since the last Freemen were made. If the proposition meant anything it was not "Busi- ness as usual," but "Unusual business." See- ing that reference had been made as to how this proposal was brought about, he thought he would be justified in giving his version of the matter.. At the very end of a recent meeting of the General Purposes Committee, Councillor Archer, as some members had put it, sprung his proposal on the members present. Councillor Archer had assured him that his proposal was spontaneous, that the whole scheme was of his own thinking out, that he had not then con- sulted anybody, and that he took whole and sole responsibility. Although he (the speaker) had heard to the contrary, he freely accepted the, Councillor's explanation, but it struck him as strange that immediately the matter was men- tioned four names were proposed. Then le (the speaker) got up and amongst other names sug- gested those of Alderman Bennett, John Evans, and James Evans, and Councillor Roberts, as those of other men who were Worthy of con- sideration when the Council were conferring such an honour as the Freedom of the borough. But how was the suggestion met? In a spirit of compromise? No! The answer was—there being present a majority of the friends of the

_

gentlemen who_ brought the matter forward—

"You must take these or none" Another phrase used was "Make a batch of your own!" Councillor Gotran: That's it; perfectly right!

, - Alderman Tuariox : Are we to confer this, the highest honour in the power of the town to con- fer, in batches—to confer it and approach it in that spirit? There was, the Alderman went on, no attempt to get the best opinions of the Coun- cil as to who were most fitted and suitable for the honour, until the Mayor had called him (the speaker) in to assist him in arriving at something like an amicable and proper decision on the matter. After the four names originally submitted had been proposed, after the pro- poser's friends had in effect said, "That's our batch; take them or leave them; we will have none added! Make your own batch !" they went on to settle the point in a most disgraceful way in the ante-chamber, a way which shocked the members of the Education Committee. The same spirit in which the thing was launched was exhibited that day. Then somebody evidently thought "They may object to the four w have named, and to make them swallow the proposal, to make them have our batch willy-nilly we will add the names of Sir Gilbert Greenall and Mr. 1 Arthur Crosfield," the idea being that as they were the leaders of the two political parties there would be no fight agat them. of hose two gentlemen, he suggested, were make-weights, and Kii• Gilbert had declined the honour and Mr. Crosfield, if not definitely declining it, at any rate had said postpone it until such a time as the Council could be unanimous. Did the Council imagine that it was possible for any gentleman to value the honour properly unless it was given unanimously. The Alderman went on to contend that there were other men in the town whose claims might justly have been brought forward, and expressed the opinion that it would have been better had the matter been discussed at a special meeting of the General Purposes Committee before being brought into the Council Chamber. In committee they could have fully and freely expressed their opinions, and so have come to some better arrangement, and possibly have got a unanimous decision. Some of the members who signed the requisition calling that meeting, the speaker went on to con- tend, were under a misunderstanding when they put their signatures; indeed, four, he believed it was, of them had told him that when they lent their signatures they honestly believed the matter was to be discussed the lines he had _ .

indicated, and that a public discussion of the kind taking place that evening would he avoided. Reiterating his belief that the time was not opportune for carrying the proposal .before the Council, the speaker, after mention- . . . _ _

jag that he had a son who had' offered his ser- vices to the country, and that three of his nephews were now serving at the front, re- minded the Council that the time would come when the gallant lads who had gone forth to fight would come home, and that it might be thought fit to confer the Freedom of the borough upon many of them, and if the prestige of the honour were to be dragged down in that miser- able, narrow-minded, dogmatic discussion, would men, who were really deserving it, accept it?

===== 0002601_19141226_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
DR. JOSEPH SAYS "CALLOUS."

The DEPUTY-MAYOR said that because he was not unmindful of the grave position of the country, because he could not help looking fur- ther afield to the terrible death-struggle on the Continent, because he could not forget that this country was fighting a powerful, cruel and un- scrupulous foe, and because he felt that the war was a far more important subject of debate and consideration for the Council than the confer- ring of honours—however worthy the gentlemen named might he—he was opposed to the pro- posal at this time. He thought it was callous, almost heartless, to come forward with the pro- posal at this time, and, although he would like to see the gentlemen named honoured, he would not like to feel that at such a time he had taken part in assenting to it. Councillor JENxixs explained that when he signed the requisition be was unwell and had ' not the remotest idea that by lending his sig- nature he had committed himself in any sense whatever to voting for any particular individual or individuals.

Councillor ROBERTS said that in moving the resolution, Councillor Archer had had a good deal to say about the opinion of the lads at the front with regard to the Council's conduct. Well, he (Councillor Roberts) could say a good deal as to what he thought the opinion of those lads was likely to be, and he ventured to say, knowing something about the lacks in question, that their views on the Council's action would not be the views which Councillor Archer had expressed. (Hear, hear.) He (the speaker) repu- diated with all the strength of his nature the suggestion that business could be carried on as usual under present conditions, and he did not envy the man who could be so absorbed in his business that he could think of nothing else but it, when the country he belonged to was in peril. Was this a fitting time to consider in the comparatively light spirit that it was being con- sidered by some, the desirability of conferring. the Freedom of the borough on certain gentle- -men, whose services had been, like the .services of many others, whose services did not stand out more distinctly than the services of many other men? He would give the gentlemen named all credit for, what they had done, for their loyalty and faithfulness in the offices they had held; but, at the same time, he contended, there were others who had quite as strong a claim to con- sideration. He suggested that when peace !lad been signed, and they had welcomed the brave Warrin4ton lads back again, the time would be more fitting for conferring these honours than was the present time. Councillor Roberts went on to say that he did not think it was quite fair treatment to Sir Gilbert Greenall or to Mr. Crosfield that their names should have been made subjects of discussion without their con- sent; indeed, what more complete an answer did they want to the resolution berbre the meeting than that contained in Sir Gilbert's letter? • Councillor HAWTHORN said he was sorry to see that the olive branch held out by Alderman Tinnion could not be accepted, and during the course of further discussion Alderman BOLTON ejaculated the remark, " I should like to tell some of these canting humbugs that they are insulting ray friends here." * Alderman BENNETT said he felt very jealous of the dignity of the Council, and he could not help feeling that if they were not more careful they would inflict irreparable injury on an honour which they ought to respect, viz., the Freedom of the borough. The honour was a high one, and ought to be given very occasionally and sparingly, and he thought the batch of names put forward that night constituted a rather large order. The Alderman concluded by ex- pressing the opinion that if the honour were not conferred unanimously it lost all its grace; indeed, unless it was unanimous he did not think it was worth the having.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SIGNING OF THE REQUISITION.

Councillor PEHMERTON said some of the mem- bers had not thought seriously of what they were going to say when they got on their feet. They had men who had done things deliber- ately and quietly, and then got up and said they did not know what they had been doing. How had the matter got out in the Press? It was stated that "it was well known that the names of Sir Gilbert Greenall and Mr. Crosfield were not submitted to the General Purposes Committee." That was a lie he wanted to nail down. The whole trouble was that a young member of the Council had dared to name any- one for the Freedom of the Borough. When the four names were first mentioned there was not a single, word said about the war. The compromise which was suggested when Coun- cillor Archer submitted the only list was the total withdrawal of it. Members of the Council had been threatened by little cliques in the wards. Why were the men who had signed the requisition not present? Everything that human ingenuity could do and everything a clever brain could think of had been done in the matter. They had been told to tell tales and make excuses. Sir Gilbert Greenall said he had had no intimation.

Alderman BOLTON: I had none. Councillor PEMBERTON : I don't know how Sir Gilbert got to know, unless someone informed him with the deliberate intention of misleading him.

Councillor Govon : Perhaps' he sa* the leader in "The Examiner." Councillor PEMBERTON : The nineteen men who signed the requisition should keep to their sig- natures. Councillor SHAW: When I was approached I said I should go to the meeting with an en mind. Is. that so, Councillor Archer?

Councillor ARCHER: Nee; quite right t Councillor SHAW: I know which way to rate now.

Marriage of Miss C. Jones. Working Men's Mission Church, Bank Quay, was on Tuesday the scene of a pretty, though quiet wedding, to which the contracting parties were Miss Charlotte (Lottie) Jones (youngest daughter of Mrs. Pritchard, of Wellfield-street, Warrington) and the Rev. Robert Dawson, pastor of the United Methodist Church, Hart- land, Devon. The Rev. Wesley Hall, of Crewe, was the officiating minister. The bride, who was given away by Mr. C. Pritchard (step- father), was attired in a travelling costume of grey cloth, and wore a heliotrope velvet hat. She was attended by Miss Mabel Robinson and Miss Verna Gleave (niece) as bridesmaids; and Mr. Peter Gleave (brother-in-law of the bride) acted as best man. The happy couple were the re- cipients of many handsome and useful presents.

Corporation Gardener's Death.

The dea-th occurred on Friday of last week of Mr. Emmanuel Cliffe, a well-known servant of the Corporation, and whose residence was at 22. King-street. The deceased, who bad reached the age of 78, was formerly gardener at Bank Park and latterly at the Queen's Gardens, and he followed his occupation up to within a few days of his .death. He is survived by a widow and a grown-up family. Prior to entering the service of the Corporation, the deceased was employed by the late Mr. Edward Beck, of Springfield-street. .

T. Among the numerous floral tributes was one from the 'members of the local constabulary. '

Tnion of Lancashire and Cheshire Institutes. • At a meeting of the Council of the Lancashire and Cheshire Institutes it was reported that further advisory committees of representatives of teachers engaged in grouped-course teaching within the Union's area had ben formed. Let- ters having been received from several Lanca- shire associations of the National ljni6n of 'Teachers asking for direct representation on the Council of' the Union, it was ,h.,cided to reply • that teachers were being co-opted on the Coun- cil on the nomination of specially convened meetings of teachers engaged in the teaching of subjects included in the Union's syllabus rather • than from any particular association of teachers.

Secondary School Extension. At a meeting of the Education Committee on Monday the minutes of the School Management Committee, which were confirmed, included the following item: A letter having been submitted from the Town Clerk stating that certain land in Museum-street had been purchased by the Corporation, with a view to the extension of the Secondary School, it was resolved: "That the ,Street Improvement Committee be informed that the Education Committee, after carefully considering the matter, see no prospective use for the land referred to."—Alderman Bennett said he regretted that resolution. As it had been arranged that the Street Improvement Committee should meet that committee to dis- cuss the matter further, it was not desirable that anything further 'should be said at pres- ont.—The deputation will meet the committee on February 4th.

Councillor Roberts's Christmas ‘Vish. At the close of the Education Committee meet-1 ing on Monday, the Chairman (Councillor Roberts) said: It is customary at the last meet- ing of the year for the Chairman to wish the members ,of the committee the coxnpliments of the season. I do so. as you can understand, with very mixed feelings. One cannot antici- pate that Christmas this year will be anything like what it has been before in the memory of every one of us. In spite of the grave circum- stances whith obtain at the present time, I may say that I hope each of us will have a happy Christmas, and that the happiness will be in- tensified by circumstances which will definitely satisfy us that the future of the country is assured, that the future of the Empire iS safe, and that our arms on sea and land ma,- meet with such success, either before this.year is out. or early in the year that is to come, that will make this year a pleasant memory in that re- . _ .

spect and ae next year a year of. happiaess to Ilk all. (Hear, hear.)

MAYOR AS SANTA CLAUS.

Over 700 Children Receive Christmas Presents. GAY PARR HALL SCENE.

"Daddy Christmas" and "Fairy God- 'mother.' These were the new names given re- spectively to our worthy Mayor and Mayoress by Alderman Bennett at the distribution of dolls and toys in the Parr Hall yesterday after- noon. There is no disputing, the fact that by their generosity and initiative in providing close upon 800 of Warrington's poorer children with an appropriate present this Christmas, they have justified the new titles. When it became known that the ,children of Warritigton would not this year receive any toys from the Man- chester Cinderella Club, the Mayor decided that the children should not be forgotten, and as a start gave 200 dolls and toys. Then, when he and the Mayoress held their "at home" in the Library and Museum, they stipulated that each guest should bring some present suitable for a child. The restilt was entirely satisfactory, for yesterday there were no less than 750 dolls and other toys set out in the Parr Hall ready for the Mayor to distribute. Each present was just the thing to delight a child, and some of them must have been very expensive indeed. A SIGHT TO GLADDEN CHILD-EYES.

The presents for the boys were arranged on one side of the hall, and included toy guns, pistols, trains, building materials, drums, bugles, motor ambulance waggons, painting sets, &c.! &c. On the other side were the numerous dolls, tea-sets, games, teddy-bears, dolls' beds, and an indescribable variety of presents, all of which would bring a gleam of pleasure and sat- isfaction into the eyes of the little girl recipi- ents. Mr. Mitchell had worked very assidu- ously in making the arrangements, and he is to be congratulated on the excellent results which rewarded his efforts. As the children entered the hall they were marshalled into their places by members of the Education Office staff, who rendered valuable assistance.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
A CUP OF TEA WITH THE MAYOR AND MAYORESS.

Prior to the distribution, each child received a cup of tea and a sweet-cake, which had been given by the Mayor and Mayoress, who had also arranged that on leaving the hall each child should be presented with a couple of oranges. The Mayor and Mayoress busied thuiselves With handing out the refreshments, and they were ably assisted by their son; Master B. Peacock, and the following ladies: Miss Short, Miss Hewitt, Mesdames Osborne. Lindley, T. Locker, Pemberton, J. S. Hill, W. H. Dutton, Sanders, T. Bunch, J. T. Locker, C. W. Shaw, Percival, J. Halliwell, Hewitt, Pendlebury, J. T. Collin, Brocklehurst, Baden and Mitchell. MAYOR THANKS THE DONORS. . .

During an interval the Mayor and Mra.Yoress, the above ladies, with Alderman Eennett, Mr. A. T. Flallaway (deputy town 'clerk), Mr. J. Moore Murray (director of education), and Mr. A. M. Ker (borough surveyor) assembled on the platform, and in the course of a short speech, The Mayor said he wished to thank all those who had given toys. Last year there 350 toys from Manchester and Warrington friends, but this time they had 750, and ho wanted the children to realise the kindness of the people who had been so generous. There were a lot more children who should have had toys, but the number of the gifts was limited. Through the Education Office staff they had found out the most deserving cases, and although mistakes might have been made, he hoped the people would take the will for the deed. lle also thanked the ladies who had helped in serving out the refreshments. He wanted the children to look forward to a repetition of the affair next year, for he had established a precedent that the Mayor who followed him would have to keep up. He hoped all the children would have a merry Christmas and a happy New Year. The children responded with a hearty "Same to you, sir." CHILDREN'S CHEERS FOR MAYOR AND MAYORESS. Alderman Bennett said it was very good of the Mayor and Mayoress to give the children such a happy time. He would call his Worslpip "Daddy Christmas," for, although he might not have that benign old gentleman's beard, his Worship had undoubtedly got his heart. He would call the Mayoress "Fairy Godmother," because they all knew what the fairy godmother did for Cinderella. The children should give them three hearty cheers. . _

And they did, right lustily! Master Peacock presented thft gifts to the girls, and the boys received their presents from the 'Mayoress.

Hunny. At last the Germans are reported to have scored a little success in the United States. An American coon is said to have been so much impressed by the achievements of the Germans that he has sent a song to the Kaiser, the open- ing words of which are "My Hunny."— " Punch."

YULETIDE SERVICES & SERMONS

"MESSIAH" SELECTIONS AT THREE FREE CHURCHES.

Selections from Handel' " Messiah " were sung at three of the Free Churches in Warring- ton on Sunday—viz., the Bold-street and Bew- sey-road Wesleyan Churches and the Wycliffe Congregational Church. At other of the churches pulpit references were made to the near approach of the Christmas festival, and, of course, to the shadow of war which is over-cast- ing it.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
BEWSEY-ROAD WESLEYAN.

At Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church in the afternoon a musical service was held, when anthems and selections from " The Messiah" were given in a creditable fashion. Several well-known vocalists figured conspicuously in the proceedings, viz.:— Sopranos: Madame Sara Silcock, Miss Annie Winstanley, and Miss Vera Jones; contraltos, Miss Millie Jones and Miss Nellie Wardle; baritones, Mr. W. Dutton and Mr. J. Turton; tenors, Mr. A. Baker and Mr. W. T. Jeffrey. The choir were ably conducted by Mr. J. E. Hannan, and the organist was Mr. J. White. Mr. Frank Hickman presided over tiib service.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN.

Selections from "The Messiah" were also sung at the Bold-street Wesleyan Church on Sunday evening, there being a large congrega- tion. Mrs. Booth, the Misses Brough, E. Tay- lor, C. Hankey and Blackburne, and Messrs. W. Warburton and E. H. Pearce were the soloists, and they each rendered in most creditable man- ner the share of the glorious music falling to them, specially effective treatment being ac- corded the more popular numbers. Mr. Frank Hickman presided at the organ, and the Rev. A. J. Morton gave a brief and appropriate address on music.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
WYCLIFFE CONGREGATIONAL.

At the Wycliffe Congregational Church a very large congregation assembled on Sunday evening to hear the ulsual Christmas choral service— consisting of selections from " The Messiah " rendered by an augmented choir. The orches- tra, which efficiently accompanied, consisted of: Messrs. A. Bennett, W. Brown and T. Frain, first violins; Mr. J. E. Balmford, viola; Mrs. Haviour and Mr. A. Knowles, second violins; Mr. H. Bond, 'cello; Mr. R. Whitfield, bass; and Mr. T. F. Wallington, organ. Mr. W. H. Pay- ton conducted.

The soloists were Mrs. J. Smallshaw, the Misses May Atkinson and E. Hewitt, and Messrs. W. Booth and J. B. Poole, all of whom per- formed very creditably—though the choruses were, perhaps, the best feature of the selections. The Pastor (the Rev. T. T. James) gave a short address upon the spiritual aspect of Christmas. There were, he said, joys which they experienced now, such as they experienced at no other time of the year. They all knew the attractive and familiar stories of the birth of Jesus Christ into the world; and, at such a service as that, they were celebrating an his- torical fact. Yet, after all, the birth of Jesus Christ was rendered an historical fact not so much by their celebration of it at Christmas, as by their willingness and readiness to obey Him. He trusted that they might get a new hold on Him; and then God's blessing would be realised in their midst.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GLADNESS AMID TIIE SADNESS. Rev. I'. T. James On How To Keep This Year's Festival.

At the Wycliffe Congregational Church on Sunday morning the Rev. T. T. James preached on "The Gladness of Christmas." They must, he said, all feel that they were coming to the saddest Christmas the world had ever known. They could not forget that at many a table there would be gaping blanks, places that would not be filled again in any Christmas that life should know. They could not forget that in many a home there would be anxious hearts for those who were distant and to whom every hour brought danger, to whom death itself might came at anY moment. ThOrcould net forget that within their own borders there were towns which had felt the horror of war and had suf- fered from the ruthless spirit of wanton destruc- tion. They could not forget that both in this land and in other countries there were thou- sands who were wanderers on the earth, whose homeland was a blackened heap of ruins, and who were stunned and overcome by the disaster which had overtaken them.

Unchristian Orgy of Bloodshed and Horror.

Granted that they felt this war to be a clear call to conscience, so that it appealed to them as something from which there was no possible escape, the fact remained that its orgy of blood- shed and hqrror still impressed them as an essentially unchristian thing. Granted that every claim of honour, the cause of the weak and the spectre of oppression involved the ab- solute necessity of their share in it, they did not easily reconcile themselves to the climax of inhumanity which war always produced. "We feel," he said, " that it is a far cry from the angels' song to the sound of Christian nations in mortal conflict. We do not easily see the star of Bethlehem shining over the thud and blood of Europe. We do not readily recon- cile the promise of angel choirs with the hymns of hate of the German people. No, let us hon- estly confess that when it comes to this time of memory, arid our minds turn back to the Babe between the sheep and the kine, we feel the contradiction of Christian hope in the bitter- ness and horror of the present hour. Men are tempted to ask how much Christianity has accomplished in the grip of such a problem as this,, and even to bewail its apparent failure and the defeat of Him Whose sad face from the Cross sees only this, after the Passion of two thousand years."

===== 0002601_19141226_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
Not a Time for Forgetfulness.

Going on to deprecate any effort to forget for the time being all the strange sadness and burden of the present time, the preacher said: "It is, of course, conceivable that there are those who even yet have thought little of the heartbreak of this world conflict, who are able to cackle and laugh in empty frivolity and in mindless mirth without let or hindrance. But for the great body of men and women it is not possible. We sit down and think, and behold ! guarding our firesides and the quiet of our homes, the peace of our countryside, the happi- ness of our children and the honour of our womanhood, there is a line of brave men stretch- ing along the trenches in Flanders and in France, and great bulwarks of the sea with their living freight of human lives. And every ruined homestead across the seas, every fallen hero in the field, every brave man buried in the deep, every broken-hearted mother in the cottages or palaces of England, and every home circle that can never be completed more, are here to remind us that we are safe and well and whole through the suffering and sacrifice of others for our sakes. If we try to forget these things, the tragedy of them will stand up Sand smite us in the face."

===== 0002601_19141226_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
How to Discover the Gladness This Year.

They would, said the reverend gentleman in conclusion, -discover the gladness of Christmas, more particularly this year, by making it a season of unusual thoughtfulness for others, a time of peculiar tenderness in our own personal relationships. It was inevitable that there should be about this time of celebration a deeper note than -ordinarily obtained. They should also do their utmost to share the burden with those upon whom it fell most heavily. If they could not practise self-denial in face of such service as was being rendered for them at this moment, he did not imagine any call could ever be made to which they would respond.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

Excellent holiday fare is provided at the Hip- podrome this week, and the audiences are large and enthusiastic. Fresh and sensational acrobatic feats are performed by the Mizuno Troupe of Japanese gymnasts. Each member performs the most intricate evolutions in a very clever and graceful manner, whilst the audi- ences are specially delighted with the antics of a diminutive member of the troupe, who goes through a most amazing performance. Balanc- ing in all its phases is done in a creditable fashion, which calls forth hearty approbation. Edna Lyall, described as the Yorkshire nightin- gale, renders well-chosen songs in a tasteful and artistic manner; and is a great favourite. Albert Wyndham and Co. get the best out of a bright comedy-drama based on an incident in the life of Nell Gwynne. Miss Mona Shepherd makes a charming and fascinating Nell, and Mr. Albert Wyndham plays the part of King Charles 11. with great success. Yettmah is a clever mysti- fier, whose tricks are new and ingeniously con- trived. The quick changing part of his per- formance is neatly and mysteriously presented. "Bertie" Adams, with J. C. Holliday at the Piano, scores a decided success with her popular songs, which are rendered in pleasing style. Day and Deen cause roars of laughter with their witty patter and eccentric dancing, and Marie Masque and Dora sing and pose with pleasing effect Vic and Vie also please as dancers.

TRENCHES.

The men have taken, to singing _Christmas

carols in the evenings (writes an officer), thus rubbing in that it's Christmas time. At night in the trenches we can plainly hear the Ger- mans singing "Der Vaterland " tround their trench fires, but on wet days they do not sing.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
A BATH!

Wonderful Effect of a Wash and Clean

Clothes.

An officer writing of an "extraordinary experience" he had just gone through, says:— . .

4 We all marched up to a building that was evidently a derelict laundry. As soon as the men entered the building each was served out with a bundle containing a clean shirt, pants, vest, and socks. They filed into a long room, where there were eight enormous vats full of hot water with little chunks of soap ranged all round the edges. The men undressed at one end of the room, throwing all their old under- clothing on to a big heap. Their outer clothing was all collected, done up into bundles, and passed up to a room overhead, where it was all gone over with a hot iron. The R.A.M.C. ser- geant in charge of washing arrangements then ordered 14 men into each vat, and for 10 minutes the room resounded with sounds of splashings, gruntings, and such cries as ' Eh ! Bill, you've got yer foot in my mouth!' Lumme, this ain't half fine, this ain't!' As fast as the sergeant thought each 14 sufficiently clean they were ortlered out to dress in their clean clothes and their places taken by another 14 of the 'un- washed.' It's wonderful what an inspiriting effect.a good hot bath and clean clothing has on tired men. The sergeant told me he had 'put through 1,000 in two days, and that we should now get a bath roughly once every 10 days, which I think is a wonderful bit of organisa- tion."

===== 0002601_19141226_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
LISTEN, YOUNG 'MEN!

Warringtonian's Appeal to Fellow-

Toln ns-m- en.

I, as a Warringtonian, appeal to all the men of my town, who are able so to do, to come for- ward and defend their country. There was never a time in the history of England when the Homeland stood so much in need of the help of her manhood. Let the men of War- rington see to it that in the years to come they are able to say, "We helped; we did our share, and we are willing to do as much again if need be!"

Thus does Pte. T. Mullen, of the R.A.M.C., make his appeal to the manhood of Warrington in a letter addressed from the Front—No. 6 Ambulance Train, British Expeditionary Force, Belgium, is the address he gives! The letter is written to Mr. F. Highley, of 103, Catharine- street.

"This is my second experience of warfare," the letter continues, "and I really think if ever we stood in need of men it is now! I should be very much pleased if I could turn round to my colleagues and say: `The men of Warrington have answered to the full to their country's call !' Come on, Warringtonians; let us show that the men of our town are as eager to do their best for their country as are the men of any other town in England. I have seen a lot, and expect to see more before I return, but I am quite content, and I am sure that we are going to win. The sooner we get our Army to the strength that Lord Kitchener wants it, the sooner shall we do that. The Germans thought our Army a farce; we intend to show them that it is a reality..

===== 0002601_19141226_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
Saw Service in South Africa.

"I saw nearly three years' service in South Afttit.a. • . have nearly fourteen years' serviee, tt; my credit, and, if my- country ever needs me again, after this business is over, I shall be ready to do whatever I can for her! "You will have seen that we have started 'digging the Germans out,' as Mr. Churchill called it. They are being watched by our Navy as a cat watches a mouse! Mr. Churchill said: Take no notice of the first three months, but look what six bring—and then nine!' I really think that nine months will \see the finish of this 'terrible war, because the! Germans are al- ready 'fed up.'

"They Don't Like Steel!"

"They don't like steel, of course! I don't say that they do not like to steal, because, as you know as well as I do, they are in their glory when they are looting. But 'wait and see'— as Mr. Asquith says—what their punishment will be when the British, Belgian and French soldiers get on to German soil!

St. John Ambulance Brigade Hospital for France.

7,000 AMBULANCE VOLUNTEERS

The St. John Ambulance Brigade, which has two flourishing Divisions in Warrington, pro- poses to establish a hospital for the treatment of the sick and wounded in France. Mr. A. E. Spann, secretary of the Warrington Headquar- ters DiVision, has just made an appeal for sub- scriptions locally, and as it would be impossible to find a better object to support, we look with confidence to a prompt and liberal response. The honour of having sent the first guinea be- longs to the St. Helens Cable Company, and to this the officers and men of the Headquarters Division of the Warrington Corps 4ave added .t 5 ss.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
TO PROVIDE 520 BEDS.

Colonel Trimble, Deputy Commissioner for No. 4 District of the Brigade, explains the scheme as follows:

"I have received from the Chief Commissioner of the St. John Ambulance Brigade (Colonel Sir J. It. Andrew Clark, Bart. C. 8.) a communica- tion indicating that the Circler of St. John are proposing to establish a hospital, to be named " The St. John Ambulance Brigade Hospital" and to contain 520 beds for the treatment of the sick and wounded in France. Practically the entire personnel of the hospital is to be sup- plied by the Brigade. The approximate cost of the equipment of the hospital and its mainten- ance for six months will amount to £50,000, and it is now proposed that each district of the Brigade be requested to contribute and raise as much of this sum as possible. " It is found that £lOO will maintain a bed, and any individual subscribing this sum will have a bed named after him or her in the hos- pital. The contribution of £2,500, which will be sufficiont to maintain 25 beds, by any collec- tive body, firm, or individual will entitle the contributor to have a ward in the hospital named in like manner. -

During the present crisis the Brigade, which is entirely a voluntary civil organisation, has supplied to the Admiralty and War Office for service in the fleet, naval hpspitals, at the front. and in military hospitals at borne, some 7,000 men. No. IV. District, of which I have charge, and which comprises Lancashire, Cheshire, Cumberland. and Westmorland, has furnished 2,300 towards this total. In addition to this a number of orderlies and nurses for numerous hospitals at home and abroad have been pro- vided. Six motor Cars converted to motor am- bulances have been presented by private owners I to the Order of St. John, and four more prac- tically promised. It is my earnest desire to raise at least sufficient monis, to equip and maintain a ward in this hospital from the die-

trict under my charge, and I venture to ask for help in this direction."

No. IV. DIVISION WARD.

Will you help Division No. IV., to which Lancashire and Cheshire belong, -to possess a ward in this great hospital, that will do endless good and save an immeasurable amount of pre- ventable suffering? If so, communicate at once, sending even the smallest sum, to ; Mr. A. E. SPAWN, 24, Cairo-street, Warrington.

GLASGOW.

Thrilling Description of Chile Battle

BY A WARRINGTONIAN.

Lance-Corporal Alf. Purcell, who is borne on the books of 11.M.5. Glasgow, irhero- ship of the historic engagement off the Chilian Coast, has written another long and interesting letter to his father, Mr. D. Purcell, a former Warringtonian, who now resides at 158, Whet- stone-lane, Birkenhead. The Lance-Corporal has many friends in the town, where he spent his boyhood, and where subsequently he became a telegraph messenger, prior to going to work at the Cable Works, which establishment he left in order to join the Royal Marines.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
Had to ', Run For It" and Lie Low.

"No doubt," he writes, " -3-;c;;;.i;;;;-11;.ard of our engagement in the Pacific Ocean, and I am wondering what version of the affair you got in England. We had to run for it and lie low for a few days, so far as communication was con- cerned; otherwise our wireless would have be- trayed our whereabouts and we should have been cut off. The captain would not report until we were in the Magellan Straits, and consequently we thought we may have caused some anxiety in England, as you would only have the German version of the fight for the first few days. "You will like to hear of our escapade? . . . The Glasgow, being a fast cruiser, 'A elB crdered to proceed to Caronel—a eistanze of about two hundred miles from where we were at the time. Our orders were to intercept ll•?rtrv.n sailing ship making for Caronel, and alsc- to glean cer- tain information. When nearing Caronel our wireless instruments picked up Germ,tn mes- sages so plainly that we came to the conclusion that the enemy-ships were not very far away. We accordingly communicated with our Olive companion ships—the Monmouth, the Good Hope (Rear Admiral Craddock's flag-ship) and the Otranta (an auxiliary cruiser). They imme- diately left their base to join us and came up with us at dinner-time the follow- ing day. They and we then started to sweep the ! ocean in line, and we sighted the enemy.at about ! five p.m. and at once cleared for action. We had lost sight of the Good Hope and could not get into commnnication with her, owing to the Germans blocking our wireless by sending mes- sages of their own every time we endeavoured to send a message. - Enemy's Tremendous Fire. - - - - - -

The Admiral signalled orders for the Otranta to get clear out of the way, as she would have made an easy target for the enemy. The three of us remaining steamed 'line ahead,' about three hundred yards separating each ship. . . . After a while the enemy opened fire on us and we replied. . . . 'They poured in a tremendous fire and their range and aim were disconcertingly accurate, their shells mostly dropping either just over us or just short of us. After a while the Good Hope, which had now come up, caught fire, and her magazine blew up,the flames leaping to a considerable height. By now the Monmouth was in a sinking condition, and the enemy con- centrated nearly all their fire on our vessel. H.M.S. Monmouth's Signal of Distress.

They had four ships in action and were supe- rior in armament. . . . It was now getting dark mid the Monmouth signalled that she was taking in water very fast and could not steam away The enemy ceased fire; being, no doubt, unable to see the target. We also ceased. There was now nothing to do, save either to steam away or be sunk; added to which we were in danger of being cut off by their light cruisers. We decided on the first alternative, and steamed away at top speed until we got clear of the Magellan Straits. " The action lasted an hour, and it is almost impossible to conceive the amount of damage that was done in so short a time. We, on the Glasgow, were very lucky indeed; escaping with comparatively little hurt, though we came in for more than a fair share of their shells. One could bear the shells going overhead, and, of course, could see them falling in the water either just in front of the ship or just behind her. "We Were Very Lucky, But-"

" Still, though we were very lucky, we foun4 that we were more damaged than we thought we were. We had three holes in our side, and one shell had penetrated the captain's cabin, whilst another had hit the funnel-casing. We had beetn hit five times:in all.. One of the boles in. the side of our vessel was below the water-line, and it was so big that you could have walked through it without bending. Ilad the shell that caused it penetrated a little further it would have damaged our engine-room and we should have been unable to get away, and, most probably, would have been sunk. . . . . I don't think any of us who witnessed the battle will ever for- get the sight. I suppose there would be about 1,600 hands lost. Monmouth Fights to the Last.

been-

"The German Admiral reports that it was im- possible to save anybody from the Monmouth, as, being unable to steam away, she fired until her guns were practically submerged. How- ever, I do not think any lives could have been saved even if she had ceased firing, as the sea was rather rough, and, therefore, it would have been dangerous for the Germans if they had lowered boats."

The letter goes on to tell how the Glasgow put into dry-dock at Itio Janiero for repairs, leaving two days later, and how the crew were very much surprised to find how light were the casualties suffered aboard—" when the action was over we found that our casualties were only small; four men being slightly wounded by splinters."

===== 0002601_19141226_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
LIFE IN BRUSSELS.

Business Man's Escape to England And His Return.

THE COMING TRADE BOOM.

SPECIAL: BY OUR OWN REPRE- SENTATIVE.

I recently had the privilege of half an hour's conversation, with the aid of a fr:endly ,nter- preter, with a Belgian who was in Brussels when the Germans took possession .)f that city, who remained at his post there until recently, who only a few days ago escaped the vigilance of the enemy and came to England to see his wife, who had, at the approach of danger, been sent to England and placed in the safe custody of friends.

The Belgian, evidently a keen buiiness man, is the representative in Brussels of a large Eng- lish manufacturing firm. He is so cc.nfident of the successful issue of the great conflict that he has not only started back for Brussels, but has taken his wife back with him. Trade, lie thinks, will boom as soon as the Germans have been "cleared out," and be wants to be at the pal of duty ready at the proper—which is the first— moment. It was to give his firm this personal assurance in respect of the future, to provide them with information, and to receive instruc- tions, as much as to take back his wife—surely one of the first Belgian refugees to return to a distraught land—that impelled him to visit this country at the present juncture. The only condition upon which our friend would be interviewed was that no word that might hint at his identity should appear in print, as it would lead to his arrest and pro- bably to his being shot. For, though the Ger- mans will go, they are not gone yet.

"NEWS" IN BRUSSELS. I was particularly anxious to learn first-hand how a peaceable citizen feels when he sees his land invaded and his home desecrated. He was amazingly cool, to my mind, over such a ques- tion. The Germans came suddenly upon Brus- sels, he said, and were in possession before the inhabitants had time to think one way or the other. There was an entire absence of outrage or atrocity. The Germans simply claimed a free passage and, that being guaranteed by the Burgomaster, no private houses were visited. From the shops and garages they "lifted" everything they wanted, leaving useless acknow- ledgments for the goods commandeered. There ,was not much difference, he said, in Brussels after the German occupation. The strangers advanced the time an hour to make it accord with the Berlin time, which led to the joke being made that as the Germans could not advance with their armies they would do so with the clocks. In the absence ot atrocities or damage, the only change was that life became regulated and irksome. No cafes, no theatres, no news except that manufactured by the Ger- mans. _

This was a point which for me had peculiar attractions. ___ _

-------- "Do you get any English newspapers" I asked. _ _

" Tee," was the reply, "but very dear. Copies of 'The Times' are smuggled in, and for these from -a to ..t6 a copy is paid. These are read and passed from friend to friend, and those who read them retail the news in conversation to those who do not get the chance. The reading of these papers is strictly forbidden, and a man found with one in his possession would be sent to gaol for six months." "And how does the news in 'The Times agree with the news given out by the Ger- mans?" _

"It is exactly opposite," was the ready reply, "exactly opposite in every case. The German news of the war is posted on the walls of the town, and when the English papers come they are the exact opposite." "And which of the two versions ido the Bel- gian people believe?" "They believe the English papers. They have never believed the German stories and • never will." . In answer to my question aa to food condi- tions in Belgium. my informant said that it is. possible for people who had money to buy flesh ,f 'meat, but it is Very -dear, though not double the urdiftary• priee. There is no ordinary bread, as it is impossith ko get yeast or any equivalent. The wheat available is diseased, and the 'bread" is consequently black, dirty and sour. Milk available, but eggs are not. It is possible to . get coal at about a ton. There are about 40.000 people in Brussels without food or money, who are being fed daily on soup and bread by the municipality—the soup being very good. The Germans "permit" this.

GRAND REVIVAL COMING. "As to trade?" "There is no trade or commerce of any kind. One has great difficulty in spending one's time. We visit our customers—such of them as exist— keeping up the friendship and exchanging the news gathered from the English papers and giving such assistance and advice as is pos- sible." "And what are the prospects?" " I prophesy an immense demand immediately the war is over. There will be a grand revival., with the help of England." "You have faith in the ability of this coun- try to see you righted?" We have the greatest confidence in Eng- land—from England will come our deliverance." "How long do you think that will be in coming?" _ _

"It-is difficult tb say, but the people of Brus- sels have the idea that the war will be finished by April. They have every hope it will be." I hinted that I had heard, from a well-in- formed source, that it was expected that the Russians would be in Berlin by February, but he smiled and shook his head.

" The English and French will get there first," he said. 'lll'sieur assured me, from his personal know- ledge, that the infantry, the flying men, the cavalry, the artillery, and the naval men, were not the only departments in which the British had asserted and established their superiority over the enemy. The British, he maintained, live a far better secret service on the Continent than most people are aware. They know a good deal more of the plans and the doings of the enemy than the enemy know of the Allies.

HOW HE ESCAPED. M'sicur, being of military age, was forbidden to leave Brussels, and being anxious to take his wife home and make his arrang .ments for th - future, be "escaped." lie did so without di zuise on a boat on the Scheldt, going fro -Brussels to Antwerp. While the boat was bei searched by Germans he concealed himself in mattress, and was actually touched by the hand of one of the searchers. From Antwerp 12 went to Vlake, and then entrained for Rotte darn, where, being on neutral ground, he cc free Ho visited his brother, a Belgian soldie interned in Holland, and then travelled b train to Flushing,, whence he sailed for Londo On the journey across the Ninth Sea they we challenged by British warships, but allowed pass when assurances were given that evert' thing was in order.

"To look after our home," he explained in the most natural tone imaginable. " Then you are not afraid of the Germans molesting you?" "I don't expect anything serious." "Nor are you afraid of the Germans taking revenge upon the town when they have to leave it, as at Rheims and elsewhere?" "No, the American and Italian Consuls are there, and the Germans are anxious to avoid the bad opinion of America and Italy. It might amount to an act of war against America and Italy if the Germans interfered in some of the matters where their Consuls are involved." "Is it true that the Germans have mined Brussels?" "No. I don't believe it is." "Did you pay the indemnity demanded by the Germans?" "Yes, we paid them the fifty million francs, quickly.
