THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 26, 1914.

By MAY WYNNE.

"I hate snow," declared Mine Graysham vindictively, "and I simply hate country mud and dulless. As to the prospect of spending the next month here—really, Bob, I can't do it."

Her husband stood before the library window, jingling the money in his trousers pockets and surveying the wintry scene. "It'll be all right when Christmas is over," he replied, cheerfully. "The Ailing- tons and Barwells will be here on the 3rd for the shooting." "January!" retorted Aline with disgust. "And to-day is the eighteenth of December. Why didn't we stay in London?" "There were the kids," suggested Bobbie.

Mine grimaced. "They would have been just as happy," she snapped, "as long as there were the shops to look at, and the prospect of a pan- tomime." _ _ .

Bobbie did not reply, he was watching his seven-year-old son and heir zealously snow- balling his still smaller sister. "I'll tell you what," quoth his wife, sit- ting up suddenly on the snug Chesterfield and clasping two pretty hands round her knee. "You might take me to Paris for Christmas. It would be delicious, and just take 'the bone' out of this dreary time. You really must now—it is quite an inspira- tion on my part—a recipe for putting me in a real good temper for your old shooting , party." Bobbie Graysham turned his back on the window and surveyed the dainty .figure on the sofa. How pretty she was, how charming, how , perfectly gowned. She certainly was not intended for country mud and dulness. He himself, too, was not at all averse to the idea of a frolic in Paris. But--- _

"Paris," he murmured. "Of course, it's an awfully jolly idea; all the same there are—"

"It's delicious," interrupted Aline jump- ing up and coming to his side. "We'll hire a car over there, and when -we are tired of shopping—" • "When," interpolated Bobbie. His wife shook him playfully. "We will go motor- ing," she went on. "Such fun it will be— and the theatres and operas, and all the nice things that always seem going on in the city of enchantment! Elsie Margrove told me that Christmas in Paris is just ideal."

"Of course, there are the children," murmured Bobbie, but he did not put the suggestion with the same enthusiasm he would have shown half a minute previously when he was face to the window and back to his wife.

Aline was a past-mistress in over-ruling objections when she wanted her own way. "I was thinking of them," she replied airily. "And they shall have the jolliest of times." "In Paris?" demanded Bobbie, with raised brows. "Goose! Of course not. They would hate it, poor little things. No, they shall have their treat at home. They will he quite happy, with their turkey and plum-pud- ding, snowballing and Christmas stockings. That is all kiddies think about, and I shall arrange with nurse and Miss Partington that they have a little tree on Christmas Eve. How pleased they will be." Bobbie said no more. It was not for mere man to diagnose child-nature in contradic- tion to his wife, but he had an uncomfort- able memory of having promised to play the "awfullest excitingest games" on Christmas Eve, and building a snow man almost to the skies, if the old woman in the clouds plucked many more geese. "I suppose that'll be all right," he replied dubiously, and Aline laughed merrily. "More than all right," she declared; "they'll have the time of .their liyos—so shall we. Bobbie vou are a positive .duck." And she kissed -him as she had somehow forgotten to do for the last year or two, though they got on capitally in the ordi- nary sense of the word, and never interfered with each others' annisements. Of course, now the matter of the kids was clenched, they were both agreed about Paris. Bobbie thought snow beastly stuff, and country neighbours the limit—the style who played bridge for nuts or counters and thought week-end parties one of the dis- graces of the Century. "We'll start on Thursday," Aline arranged. "I'll explain to the children; the thought of Paris toys will more than con- sole them for our loss." Unfortunately, Bobbie came into the room just as she had finished explaining, and it seemed neither Phil nor Mollie were think- ing of the delights of a Christmas tree or toys from Paris. "But you won't be here," Phil was say- ing in bitterness of spirit; "and daddie won't be able to be a Christmas ogre." Tears were not far off blue eyes, though their mother made haste to 'check them. "He can he a New Year's one," said she, "and that will be much nicer. You have no idea, children, what lovely toys I'm going to bring you from over the seas." Mollie began to whimper. "But s'posiug you get drowned on the way," she argued. • This pessimistic view could not be enter- tained, and the children were packed off to make a snow-man of their own in the gar- den, with a vague promise from daddie that he would come and "stick on" the head. "They'll be perfectly happy when we have gone," Aline repeated cheerfully. "Children are always like that. 'Out of sight, out of mind.' And both Nurse and Miss Partington adore them." Her husband was whistling "The Last Post," and did not hear her. Clearly some ' of Mollie's pessimism had descended on to his shoulders. The next day they started for Paris. The children wept buckets at parting, and though, as their mother remarked later, it was "only natural," the incident cast a slight gloom over the earlier part of the journey. But Aline could not remain low- spirited for long—.and she had the gift of wit when she chose to exert it. So Bobbie was soon roaring delightedly over the eccen'ricities of fellow-travellers at viewed by his wife, and quite forgot hoa Phil—amidst throttling hugs—had sobbed out that a New Year's ogre could never. never be the same as a Christmas one. But Aline was bent on seeing everything throurgh rose-coloured spectacles. Even when it poured dismally she vowed Paris was delightful, and steered e damp and de- pressed Bobbie through half-flooded streets to do her shopping. The only places he really got excited in were the toy-shops— where he fulfilled the role of "mad English- man" to perfection—and another elysium Where one bought specialities in the shape of foies gran, truffled boars' heads, caviar and all sorts of other insidious palate- ticklers. Mine allowed him to launch forth with an indulgent smile for masculine weakness. After all, Christmas comes but once a year, and he had been a pet in allowing her carte blanche with regard to frocks, hats, and furbelows. By the twenty-third, shopping was ex- hausted, and Aline, knowing how her "goose's" thoughts would be drifting back , to snap-dragon and Christmas stockings, suggested a motor rue for the next day. "It has stopped raining," she laughed "and it is going to be fine. Paris on a fine day is charming. lam glad we are far from the slush of country lanes.. "I y.ender how the kids are gettimr on," murmured Bobbie, between puffs of his cigar. o.iine produced a letter. "Miss Partington says they are just as happy as they can be, she replied; and Bobbie thought a motor ride' next day would be "all right." Aline put on one of those "ducky" new motor bonnets, "just to please her old man," she declured, ;lough Bobbie was horribly unobservant of that sort of thing.. But th idea of a giAn was pleasant to him. and they

THE GRAYSHAM'S CHRISTMAS

'were golng to have "all evening ollt on their return to Paris.

Aline literally thrilled with enjoyment. Fine day, new bonnet, gay Paris, a ripping little car, and the prospect of a night's ex- citement in visiting all sorts of jolly places. How different from a long dull day at Harcourt Manor, reading a novel, or wading through slush and mud to pay calls. Bobbie acted chauffeur, and they had lunch at the dearest little café miles away from Paris.

"I)oesn't look unlike a storm," said Bobbie, as he pulled on his gloves. "We shall have to bustle." They did bustle, but unfortunately it was in the wron.o. direction. Aline prophesied this catastrophe long before Bobbie would yield the point. "We're all right," he kept saying. "We're all right. I've been here dozens of times before."

But he had to give in at last, and took to abusing foreign negligence in the matter of sign-posts and the idiocy of passers-by in not understanding his French. It was the best French, after all, but the confounded peasants talked such a jargon. And the jargon had been confusing in the matter of roads. He admitted he was not very sure of his bearings at the moment, when a deep booming of thunder and a growing darkness warned them of the ap- proach of a thunderstorm. It was no use for Bobbie to swear that thunderstorms were quite out of season at Christmastime. A. fact remained a fact—a very disagreeable one, too. Thunder, light- ning, storm, and rain had not entered their calculations, and Aline would have been in tears over the destruction of her motor bonnet, had she not been obsessed by the fear of being struck by lizhtnine. Some too-utterly-wonderfut palmist had once told her to beware of thunderstorms. It was plain that disaster was at hand. She was quite right there. The car stopped after the eccentric manner of a car which is not thoroughly understood, and though Bob, dririen to swearing in his impotent wrath, tried to coax it, they found it merely proceeded in a crazily wobbling fashion which was more alarming than the lightning. "Gales," gasped Mine; "drive through— I mean between them, Bobbie. There may be a house. Oh, I do hope there is a house. Fm7-Fin drenched."

"A merry Chi.: growled Bobbie, sotto v.-r: lity of the old Adam ia him. But Aline WILS Adam, she only lamented a 60,1 -,ci the pro- spect of a speedy ii —Ai. There was a house, though. A dear, delightful old chiteau which came into view round a turn of the carriage drive. The car proceeded thitherwards in the manner of an intoxicated duck, and finally stopped short a dozen yards from the en- tranoe.

"Come on," called Bobbie. raising his voice against the increasing tempest. "You'll have to run for it." He was viciously hoping she had had enough of Paris as she crawled beneath a smothering of rugs and scuttled through the drenching rain to a haven. Yes, it was a haven, quite dear and romantic, though to begin with Aline was not inclined to gush. A solemn-looking man-servant admitted them into a gloomy hall, where both were guiltily conscious of the fact that small rivulets of water trickled from their clothes over the parquet flooring. Eventually, however, Monsieur de Mar- court, the owner of the chateau, appeared, listened- politely to explanations, remarked (no doubt) that Madame was young 'and charming. and invited them to partake of the hospitality of the château till morning. He was not a prepossessing personality— grey-faced, grim and depressing, at least, Aline so summed him up. Bobbie, being a man, required longer for his diagnosis. A French maid took possession of "Madame," and brought a consignment of her mistress's clothes, smart, pretty clothes, well-fitted to her slender person, so that Aline's spirits rose. It really was quite an adventure, and she was smiling radiantly as Suzanne handed her over to the butler to be conducted to the salon. In the salon was Madame de Marcourt, the owner of the clothes.

Aline wondered why so charming a woman should give her an instinctive shock. Madame was American, her ago wae on the right side of thirty, her personality bizarre.

She rouged her cheeks, she dyed her hair, she wore extravagant clothes, which Bobbie would have termed "flashy," and her laugh was as defiant as her eyes. She seemed a woman who had a grudge against life, and she laughed loud to persuade the world that she had the best of the squabble. She greeted Aline with careless effusive- ness, measuring her critically and talking fast to Bobbie, who appeared, English fashion, after his wife.

Monsieur de Mar-c-o—u.rt was not eo enter- taining a companion for Aline. In evening dress he appeared more dejected, more grey- faced, than ever, and when he looked across the table to where his wire jested loudly with the visitor, k there was no love in his eyes, rather biliernea and reproach. .e menu was dainty and varied. To herself she was say- ing there must be tragedy in this old chateau. What ghosts haunted its dark rooms and dismal passages, for ghosts there certainly were? Madame de Marcourt's laugh rang shrill sad metallic, but there was no gaiety in it —it was the mirth of a reckless woman, reckless, defiant, yet covering a secret heart- ache. They did not play cards afterwards, and Mine could hardly keep her eyes open to listen to her hostess's conventional babble. _

Once, and only once, that babble was checked. It was when Aline mentioned Christmas. Then suddenly the reckless de- fiance died out of the beautiful face oppo- site her own, and the rouge and powder could not hide the scars of tragedy which showed in drawn •features. "I hate Christmas," said Madame de Marcourt. "Bah! Do not talk to me of it. I will not think of it." And, going to the piano, she began to sing some comic ditty of the music halls, which seemed quite out of place in that aristocratic and sombre drawing-room. The jarring notes added to Aline's weari- ness and she made excuse to go to bed. Madame quite understood. It was true her little guest looked tired out, she was kind in wishing her a good night, hut Aline wondered at the curious stare those hard blue eyes bent on her. Bobbie had latinehed into a scientific dis- cussion would w tcho come ur .i:letoMbaerdcoursto.on, but dared Aline hoped li not say so, "I shall be asleep as soon as my head touches the pillow," she consoled herself by thinking, but she was mistaken. The gloom of the chateau had got on nerves already unstrung by the storm and many adventures. And Bobbie, selfish creature, had. not come to bed. To and fro, tossed Aline. She could not sleep. Ghosts? Were there really ghosts in this gloomy château? She was quite sure there must be. Behind those black panels the crumbling bones of some poor murdered creature might be reposing. Bobbie was a brute not to come to bed. He might have known she would never get to sleep in this hateful, haunted château. Presently wolyshueld kill her.wkoiuldsce the h beams creeping in from behind drawn cur- tains made the darkness more awe-inspiring. She was positive a murder had been com- mitted in this room, and that was what made; M. de Marcourt and his wife so "funny. They certainly were funny. In a panic Aline sprang from her bed, crossed the room, and opened • the door. She wi\uld wait her till Bobbie came un

and then wouldn't she talk to him! She quite trembled with rage at his incon- siderate conduct in leaving her to such a vigil. The long minutes crept slowly by. Would Bobbie never come? Then another thought came to torture her in her nervous excitement. Supposing Bobbie had been murdered? One never knew what might happen when one came to lonely chateaux in a foreign land. Oh dear! Oh dear! She was sure now that tragedy brooded yonder in those black shadows. She knew something terrible had happened. Then suddenly, as she crouched there, hidden by the heavy portiere, she heard steps approaching along the passage. It certainly was not Bobbie, who always came clattering along as if he were racing to catch a train. These footsteps fell soft and slow—almost steathily. Aline's heart began to beat with new fears. Who could it be who crept so secretly along the passages of the chateau at mid- night? Not down this passage though . . . the , footsteps were passing away to the right— ' and who . • . 'why! it was Madame do Mar- court herself! Madame de Marcourt, no longer in gay attire, but with a long black wrap enveloping her, her hair hanging loose over her shoulders, a candlestick in one hand, whilst the other shaded the flame against draught. It was just such an appari- tion as might set the blood racing through stouter veins than those of Aline Graysham, since the woman's eyes were fixed in a curiously hard stare—the eyes of tragedy— unnamable tragedy which seemed to have frozen up all vitality in this gay and reck- lessly laughing lady of the chateau. Where was she going? What 'was she going to do? Fear had laid its hand on the watcher, but it was the fear that impelled to action. Was it possible this woman was on the verge of some crime? That was the sugges- tion which seemed to become certainty to Mine as she, in turn, crept stealthily up the passage, following the woman in the black cloak. "I must follow her," thought Aline to her- self, "if not, something dreadful will happen, and . . . and I shall never forgive myself. Oh! if only Bobbie were here" But Bobbie was not there, having found scientific discussions too absorbing, and Aline followed alone along those many passages, praying that the woman would not look back and see her. Even if she did, she m see the figure which followed which • which only had the candle-light "I shall never find my ing all these passages," thought and at that moment the ire her passed, turned to the left, )eared. Should she follow? Had r right to follow? Aline hesitated, leaning back against the wall, panting in her excitement. yes, she must go on—for two reasons. To begin with, she could never find her way back to her room alone in the dark; and, secondly, that fixed look of tragedy on her fellow-woman's face demanded it. She could not leave her alone to— * • * *

There was moonlight flooding the room which Madame de Marcourt had entered. No need of candle-light to show Aline Gray.. sham an unexpected picture. With caught breath and startled eyes the latter paused on the threshold looking in. And this is what she saw. A room—a child's nursery—with toys in it; toys on the floor and on the wide window seat, a rocking horse in one corner, a play- • another;box and there in the centre a little bed—just a ^biM's bed with snowy coverings--but no eSild within it. The bed was , empty. The room was empty save for the woman in black who lay crouching by the little bed, her arms stretched wide over the unpressed pillow, her face hidden, shrouded by the scattered tresses of. her hair. That was the picture Aline Gray Sham saw —a woman weeping by a child's empty bed, at the foot of which was perched a senseless stuffed bear -with staring glass eyes watch- ing the tragedy of bereaved motherhood. Alt! the teddy-bear did hot understand the meaning of the picture, but the other watcher did, and instinctively Aline's thoughts flew back in swift, agony to the nursery at home, and the little children who were going to be "perfectly all right" with- out her. A moan broke from her lips, and the sound roused the woman who knelt there. Madame de Marcourt was on' her feet in- stantly. Who was it who had dared to follow her to this secret place? Her eyes were fiercely accusing as they looked acrom at the other woman. "you," she muttered. "You?" Then Aline, still thinking of that nursery at home, broke down into tears. "Oh, don't," she sobbed, "don't be angry. I—l understand." Loud de Marcourt hesitated, swaying a little, uncertain whether to let anger con- quer those other feelings which she hid so jealously from all the world. But, after all, this was another woman, who said she un- derstood. She wondered if that were true. "You have children," she whispered. "You know what this means?" She pointed to the empty bed. Aline shook her head. .. I never thought," she moaned. "Oh, how could I have left them? How could I?" me other gave a little cry. She was by Aline's side now. "You left them?" she questioned eagerly. ..yes. To come to Paris. I left them—for Christmas—without me. I—l thought it didn't matter. I wanted—to enjoy—myself." "You did that. You?" Aline's hands were caught in a sudden tight clasp. 'Listen," whispered the other woman be- neath her breath. "That is what I did. /. He was my only one. My little Pierre. I never knew how I loved him—till toe late. It was Christmas, too. He begged me to stay for his party. He could not enjoy it—alone. I laughed and told him I would return-- next day. It was a ball I wished to go to. A gay, beautiful ball. I danced all night. When, next day, I returned here, he Was dead. My little son—dead. It was a candle on the tree caught his clothes as he tried to reach a toy too_ high above him. He called always for his mother—she was not here. Do you wonder that I hate Christmas? Do you wonder that—l come here to weep? Do you wonder that I tell you to go home Go I home to the treasures which neither money, prayers, nor love can replace. Oh—go! And Aline answered by her tears. Presently she stole back alone along the silent passages. What had passed between , her and that other woman who had once been a mother no one over knew. It was the I secret which only the moonbeams and the dead Pierre's teddy-bear shared with them for all time, but there was no defiance in Loud do Marcourt's face as she whispered good-night into the other mother's ears. Bobbie Graysham had just come up to bed. He was horribly ashamed to find Aline had been waiting for him, and was profuse in his apologies. Of course he had thought her in bed and asleep ages ago, and the old boy downstairs had been yarning on till, he himself was nearly dead asleep. Aline did not answer any of these ex., aS; e...a qu. forgotten she meant to load Bobbio with bitter repreaches for his unkindness. When Bobbie paused for breath—expect- ing long and bitter reproaches—she went to him, laying her hand on his sleeve. "You shall be forgiven on one condition, dear," she said, with a tremulous little laugh. "One condition only—you must take me back to England to-morrow, Atarting early—ear.y, so that we shall be home for the children's Christmas Eve." _

SCHOOL LIGHTING.

Electricity for Oakwood Avenue

District.

COST OF NEW MAIN.

For the present the question of lighting the new Onkwood-avenue Council School is left in abeyance. At their meeting on Monday, the Education Committee had before them the fol- lowing resolution passed at a meeting of the Sites And Buildings Committee: "That the offer of the Electricity and Tramways Committee to provide a main for the supply of electricity to the Oakwood-avenue Council School upon a guaranteed revenue of £6O per annum be accepted, on the understanding that all receipts in connection with this cable be credited to- wards the amount named until such time as the cost to this committee does not exceed the cost of consumption of current."

===== 0002601_19141226_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
"ROBBING PETER-"

Councillor PEMBERTON said that A:qo was ,a large amount, as the school could be lit for £3 per year. He had opposed it in committee, and he thodght that if the, matter were passed it only ought to be done with a limitation. They ought not to go on paying the .4:60 for ever. The Education Committee would have to take the risk as to whether more customers came, and he proposed that the matter be referred back. Mr. JARMAN seconded, and said the charge was very excessive. The Electricity Committee would not ask for that charge from an ordinary trader. They would probably say they could not do it. It was fining one body to pay an- other which was making larger profits.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
INTEREST ON INITIAL OUTLAY.

The MAYOR said the Electricity Committee were giving the Education Committee something for nothing. The X6O was only the cost of the sinking fund and interest on the capital outlay, and the committee would get theirelectricity for nothing. They would gain the 0t35 differ- ence between the cost of gas in the cleaning and decorating of the school without taking into consideration the benefits from a health point of view. The amount would be ,reduced from time to time as additional customers came along. Councillor LINDLEY : There is nothing unusual in this charge. The CHAIRMAN: It is putting a business risk which properly ought to belong to the Electric- ity Committee on this committee. Councillor LINDLEY : You are getting the elec- tricity, for nothing, and you will have the bene- fits which the Mayor has pointed out. Mr. UPSON : Will nothing be paid for the cur- rent?

Mr. JACKSON HOLMES : No; so long as the £6O per year is paid. That amount will be reduced to a minimum as other customers come and we shall only have to pay the usual charge& then.

Councillor WILKINSON said that if a manufac- turer in that neighbourhood was desirous of using electricity the committee would ask him for a guarantee that the £6O was paid every year. The school had been put on the same plane as works. The Electricity Committee were not going to place a £l,OOO cable down just to accommodate a ..€2.0 or £25 per year customer. When the cable was down they would have plenty of customers, and the £6O would be reduced. _ _ _

Alderman BENNETT said he shared Councillor Wilkinson's view. Electricity was so much better, healthier and cleaner than gas.

Councillor ARCTIER said that he did not think they would have many customers in such a neighbourhood, and he did not think the ground would be occupied by manufacturers for many years. Mr. JOLLEY said he would vote against the amendment, bearing in mind the ancient saying "not to strain at a gnat and swallow a camel." The committee had swallowed many financial camels not pleasantly, but forced down its throat by other departments of the Corporation, but he thought that in this case, if there were any loss it would come out of the general pockets. CONDEMNATION OF MUNICIPAL TRADING.

Mr. Ursox : Exactly, but that loss will be a charge against the Education Committee. I would like to ask what the position would be supposing the Electricity Committee were a private company working for profit. They would not charge a customer with the cable. The proposal of the Electricity Committee is a wholesale condemnation of the system of muni- cipal trading. Mr. UNSWORTII : I think the matter can be re- duced into a very small compass. They are ask- ing this committee to take a risk which they are afraid to take themselves, and I, for one, object to it. - -

Nine voted for the amendment and nine against, and the Chairman gave his casting vote in favour of the amendment that the matter be referred back.

With this exception the minutes of the sub- committee were confirmed.

DEEP CULTIVATION.

It would be CiiffiCUlt—io..finci "a—si;onger argn, ment in favour of deep cultivation than that afforded by the appearance of crops growing side by side during a dry season, the one on deeply cultivated soil and the other on that which has only been dug one spit deep. The crop on the latter, should the season be very dry, would be drooping, while the other would be growing strongly, owing to the fact that the roots of the plants can go down into the deeply cultivated soil, and thus find moisture long after that on the shallow cultivated ground had been ex- hausted. This no doubt explains why an allot- ment holder who trenches his ground regularly gets better crops than are raised where the soil is only ploughed to, say, a depth of six inches or so. Now is the time to trench all mtcant ground itot required until the spring, incorpor- ating while the work is being done any kind of garden refuse and burying it in the bottom of the trench. There is no better way of cleanifig ground that has been allowed to become weedy than trenching it, as all the surface which con- tains the weeds can be skimmed off and buried in the bottom of each trench. Old strawberry beds are very liable to get weedy, and the best way to deal with them is to bury the whole in the trenches, which should not be less than two spits deep. Such treatment of the soil will be well repaid by the vigorous crops taken from it the following year.

FARM AND GARDEN.

THICK SOWING OF WHEAT. It has been argued that as thick seeding , means many more plants to the acre, over- crowding and loss must result. This is, how- , ever, not the case, though wheat is not a plant which should be thickly sown if it can be , helped. It depends much on how it grows on „ the land at command. The best returns can " only be expected where each plant has a reason- ' able amount of growing room; but on poor land the plants are naturally thin and small, and consequently can be somewhat crowded without interference with each other, and, in fact, to their mutual support and shelter in exposed places. Pedigree corn, again, goes farther than ordinary samples.—" Agricultural Economist and Horticultural Review."

PREPARING FOR THE CROPS OF 1915. Enough has been written about wheat, but little about those crops which are calculated to ' reduce the cost of farming. Now is the time, ,1 unless the work has been done, to prepare the - soil, on dry days, for sowing sainfoin and lucerne. These crops, which are drought re- H. sisters, should be sown much more extensively where they are suitable. Rye, trifolium, and vetches are all good in their way, but they are annuals, and cost a great deal more to produce. ' With the two plants named, however, there need be no more labour for years, and no loss of crop from dry weather, while more stock can be kept. An increase in the potato crop, too, is desirable, and where soils are of the heavier type they may be made more suitable by ridg- ing up for the winter, if clean, that they maY be better pulverised.—James Long in "Farm •,., and Rome." , . „ LIVE STOCK IN WINTER. • The task of keeping the herds and flocks through the winter (says "The Times") is determined largely by what the preceding sum- mer has provided in the way of animal food. • Weather is not a negligible factor, but its power to count heavily in the balance is subject to the food supply bequeathed by the earlier months " of the year—by autumn as well as by summer ! and spring. With ample reserves of hay, oat straw, and roots the stock owner will face the sternest winter with equanimity. Abnormal snow storms in the higher altitudes and over- flawing rivers on the lower levels will hamper •'• the movement of stock, make the regular pre- vision of food difficult, and may even expose them to greater perils; • but the exercise of ordinary foresight will generally obviate serious , loss from dangers of this type. Even the most domesticated of our breds of cattle and sheep can show, when occasion requires, astonishing fortitude in withstanding searching climatic ,• conditions. They are not indifferent to the discomforts of an average winter, with its t changing temperatures, alternating rains and frosts, and recurring piercing winds, but, pro- vided they are well and suitably fed, they will „•:;• take little harm from adverse external condi- tions. An ample diet of wholesome, well-bal7 anced food will equip a healthy animal for the rigours of the severelt winter.

HINTS ON RICK FIRES. The following hints on rick fire prevention have been issued by the British Fire Prevention Committee :—Rick, fires aye cikus.ed ekther by spontaneous combustion or the ficks are set on fire in various ways from outside. In the for- mer case, the fire generally breaks out inside the rick, and water thrown on the surface does not penetrate sufficiently deep to put it out, but at the same time damages a portion of the rick which might otherwise be saved in a fairly good condition.• The judicious use of the hay cutting knife is the only way to locate the position of these fires; water must then be applied as the flames break out. When a rick is set on fire from the outside, if water is applied quickly it will generally prevent the fire from penetrating the rick. Cover up all adjoining ricks with cloths, blankets, &c. Remove all loose inflam- mable material, and especially clean up the ground, then place round on the sides exposed to the fire some protection in ,the shape of doors, sheets of galvanised iron, &c., and pour water on the top of the ricks so protected, and keep the coverings well damped. When the fire front either cause gets beyond control, the quickest way of dealing with it is to place a wire rope about 18in. from the top of the rick and then attach horses to the ends of the rope, pulling , the rick to pieces, repeating the operation as . often as necessary. Care must be taken to have ° water at hand to put out any burning material when it is thrown down, to quickly remove it away from the stacks, and to deposit it in small - heaps, so that, should any burning material have been missed, it will only endanger a limited ; quantity of the salvage. Buckets of water should always be available, and where spirit: used also buckets of sand.

MEDICAL OFFICER'S SURVEY.

- The monthly fireside conference for the com- bined Adult Schools of the town was held at Stockton Heath School on Sunday afternoon. There was an excellent attendance to hear Dr. G. W. N. Joseph medical officer of health for the borough of Warrington) on the housing question.

THE ESSENTIALS OF THE HEALTHY HOME.

Dr. Joseph held his audience 'with his mas- terly survey of this (lifficult problem. Ile pointed out the importance Of having good sites for buildings, and showed how increasing atten- tion was being paid to the question of aspect. A restricted number of houses to the area would not alone provide satisfactory housing. • Ven- tilation was of great imporance, and die ar- rangements for food storage in the home ought to be planned by the architect with a view to healthy conditions of life. Too often larder accommodation was inadequate or unhygienic.

Few Empty Workers' Houses in the Town. After rapidly summarising the legislative enactments, from working-class housing up to the larger measures touching town planning, the Medical Officer mentioned certain local con- ditions. The unoccupied houses of Warrington below a rental of ss. had dropped from 332 in 1911 to 28 in 1914. Dr. Joseph was able to make some interna- tional comparisons regarding housing progress, and he pronounced in favour of the self-con- tained house wherever possible. Many difficul- ties were faced, such as the increased price of land and building materials, the necessity for housing men near their work, and the difficulty of supplying a decent house at an economical reiit which the general labourer could afford to pay. Altogether the address touched • suc- cinctly and •luciqly on the main problems.

BATH-TAX OF 3d. A WEEK. A number of questions were addressed to the lecturer, and opinions were very freely ex- pressed. There'seemed to be a very strong feeling that the water-carriage system ought to replace the present - sanitary arrangement s. Discussion specially- centred round the point of low wages and proper houses. Some thought the enact- ment of a minimum wage the only sound method of procedure. Others wanted good houses built, whether they paid their way or not., A few would have advocated a loss to '. the rates, if necessary. Evidently -there was .a strong feeling that the time had come for a forward move in the matter of building more houses in the town. One point raised dealt with the local tax upon cleanliness imposed by the extra water rate for houses containing a bath. One speaker referred to it as a tax of 3d. per week.

IN PRE-RAILNI, AY DAYS.

When the Newspaper Was Not "In Time for the Breakfaist Table."

The lecturette at the Stockton Heath Adult School on Sunda week was given br Mr. It. Lanyard, who took as his subject : Glasgow in the old Coaching and Posting Days." The speaker gave a rapid review of the man- ner in which the mails were conveyed in the early .days, explaining that at one period the 'mail service between Glasgow and Edinburgh was maintained by a boy, who covered the dis- tance of upwards of 40 miles on foot. The carelessness in the carriage of mails in the old days was in striking ,contrast with the super- vision which was now exercised over their movements, and the discovery of lost mailbags on the roadside was no uncommon occurrence.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
Days of the Unoificial Postman.

Development of industry and the consequent growth of -population in the cities and towns demanded an improved means of communica- tion. For a considerable time, lichrever, the postal services left much to be desired, and for some time private individuals sent their com- munications under the seal of his Majesty's mail by illegal arrangement with certain postal offi- cials. The system was, however, end to by the action of certain toll-bar keepers, who demanded tolls from the mail coach drivers, on the ground of the illicit conveyance of private communications. Their position was vindicated in subsequent legal actions, and the practices were stopped.

Daily Papers 36 Hours Old. The story of the development of the stage conch as a means of communication was of con- siderable interest. In the early days of the coach the journey from London to Glasgow occupied a week. In the year of the Reform Bill the record was broken, and the ,London newspapers containing full reports of the Par- liamentary proceedings reached the Gallogate, i in Glasgow, n 361- hours-20 hours in front of the previous best journey. The journey between Carlisle and Glasgow was accomplished at a speed of eleven miles per hour—a remarkable speed, in view of the state of the roads in those days. Numerous incidents, pleasant and otherwise. in the experience of coach travel, were related by the speaker. The Bible lesson took the form of a continua- tion of the study of the life of St. Paul, and dealt with the Apostle's message. The discus- sion was thoughtfully opened by Mr. T. Brown, and the members followed up the arguments most profitably.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
By G. B. BURGIN.

1-lave you ever spent a night, with a new [inner wrapped round you, in Christmas week on that bench upon the Thames Em- bankment—the one just opposite Cleopatra's Needle—where the wind comes down from the North Pole and mercifully endeavours to end the misery .of all poor social outcasts who have not the price of a "fourpenny doss"—.--in other words, a night's lodging? If you have clone so, it is an experience which, should you live through it, you will not readily forget. Most people, however, who have undergone it, do not live to do it again. Just about four nights before Christmas, as he shivered on the bench in question, Jimmie Cochrane was Oot troubling himself about whether he could forget such an ex- perience. What he did ask himself, how- ever, was whether he could live through it. That seemed to be the all-important point, and he was still endeavouring to settle it when the Benevolent Stranger, a fine portly figure of ,a man, came along.. "Hum!' he said, pausing opposite Jimmie's listless figure. "So this is the way England utilises strong young men like you! Let's 'em catch double pneumonia on the Thames Embankment."

"You needn't be quite so particular about ' double ' pneumonia. If I don't look , out, single will be quite sufficient," Jimmie said, staring with lack-lustre eyes at this opulent, well-fed, fur-coated being. "You look so comfortable in that fur coat I should think it would take a quadruple` pneumonia to have any effect on you." "Usual story? Drink, I suppose?" queried the Benevolent Stranger. "No, it isn't the usual story, whatever that may be. Have the goodness to go away and not ask impertinent questions," said Jimmie , angrily. But the ,Benevolent Stranger smiled and sat down beside him. Jimmie noticed that he carried a coat on his arm. .

"Better yut this on," said the opulent one. "It s only squirrel fur, but it's warm."

"I daresay it is, but , I'd rather .have the pneumonia than your offensive charity." "Offensive charity! My dear fellow, you're mistaken. Twenty years ago I sat on this same bench while the Christmas bells rang out. I • had been unfortunate, my partner bolted with all my money, when an old schoolfellow happened to pass just • as, I was resolving to make a hole in the water, and set me on my legs again. I vowed that if I ever succeeded in anything, I would come back here every Christmas and help the first man I met., This year, you arc the first man." •

"Just my luck." Jimmie cheerfully roused himi4elf as the Benevolent Stranger helped him on with the fur coat. "I thought it had deserted me." . _

"Oh, no, it hasn't; not by long way. You're all right, if you only knew it," the Benevolent Stranger said cheerfully. "Look in the right hand pocket, and you'll find a packet of sandwiches; look in the left, and there's a flask of sherry." "Thanks." Jimmy rapidly dived into the right hand pocket, fished out the sand- wiches, tore the paper off and swallowed half a one at a gulp, the Benevolent Stran- ger watching him with evident interest. "I beg your pardon for wolfing, down your food like this," said Jimmie; "but the fact is. I've had nothing to eat for twenty-four hours."

"Just so. Just so. And the most hope- ful fact 0-out the whole thing is that you began on the sandwiches and not on the flask."

"Why, of course. I've had nothing to eat for twenty-four, hours, as I've just told you." Jimmie • stared at the Benevolent Stranger. "What do you take me for? I may be 'down on my uppers,' but I don't drink; in fact, I haven't any vices except that of constancy." The Benevolent Stranger looked a .little puzzled. "Human nature's a queer thing. Do you mind telling me how you got. into this Mess?" "Not if you don't miud my having a nip of sherry first." "Of course. That's what it's for."

_ Jimmie's blue eyes (lie was a handsome young fellow of about five and twenty) twinkled as he unscrewed the top of the silver flask. "I'll give you the flask back when it's empty," he suggested, "but there's about half a pint of sherry here, and half a pint of sherry to a half-starved man might be fatal." "Oh, that's all right. The flask is a pre- sent to you. You're in for a sort of Christ- mas collection." "Then I'll have a couple of glasses and save the rest for to-morrow," Jimmie said cheerfully. "Ntiw, do you really want to know how I got into this mess?' "I shall be delighted to listen to you." The Benevolent Stranger lit a cigar. "Of course you'll have one when you've finished your sandwiches," "You're very good." Jimmie finished the sandwiches, drank his second glass of sherry, and received a remarkably fine cigar from the Benevolent Stranger. "Don't hurry," said the latter, as he gave Jimmie a light. "Now unfold your 'plain unvarnished tale. What's it all about? You're a gentleman ; any fool can see that. What's the trouble?" "The old story." Jimmie revelled in the cigar: D woman, in our hours of ease, Uncertain, coy, and hard to please, But seen too oft 4 familiar with her face, We first endure, 'then pity, then embrace." "That leaves me just as wise as before." "I'm ashamed of myself. It was but a momentary cynicism. I proposed to a girl who refused me, on the ground that I was unable to support a wife. So, to prove that she was wrong, and because I had been madly in love with her for years, I went out to Canada two years ago, and promised to be back at Little Pendleton the day before Christmas in order to spend Christ- mas Eve at her aunt's. That leaves mfie four clays in which to do it. I'm very fond of her, but she's what you call practical. When I said I'd come back to her, dead or alive, two years later to ask her again whether she'd -marry me, sho wouldn't be- lieve me. She's well off and has a keen appreciation of the good things of life. A CHRISTMAS COLLECTION

THE 'IAX ON BEER.

Breller Traders to Appeal for

Substantial Reduction.

The following resolution was passed at a meeting, in Manchester, of the North of Eng- land section of the Allied Brewery Traders' Association, consisting of 743 members,. repre- seating over eighty industries and trades supply- ing the raw materials and appliances used in brewing : " While recognising the abnormal financial requirements of the nation, this meeting views with alarm the operation of the increased beer duty, which the Chancellor of the Exchequer estimates will reduce the consumption of beer by 3.5 per cent. By such reduction widespread loss of capital and unemployment has already been caused in agricultural, manufacturing, and productive industries supplying goods to brewers. "As the new taxation has undoubtedly re- sulted in much greater loss than the Chancel- lor's estimate allowed for (notwithstanding his statement that ' the Government propose to levy no taxes that will interfere with any produc- tive business '), this meeting invites the assist- ance of members of the association with a view to presenting a full statement of their case to the Chancellor to induce him to entertain a eouslderable rechwtiou of the tuz."

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

' " Florence Nightingale." What I read of the extraordinary and exceed- ingly gratifying socUess of the admirable let- tore on this subject sent me afresh to the sec- tion concerning the "Lady of the Lamp" in "Prophets, Priests and Kings." That emin- ently readable work is by Mr. A. G. Gardiner, in whose character sketches I have long taken great delight, and who, by-the-bye,. has lately administered a timely and well-deserved rebuke to the head of our mischievous Yellow Press- - a mild way of putting it, indeed, for ire meta- phorically "mopped the floor" with him. In the writings reproduced in such, convenient form by "A.G.G.," Florence Nightingale has a remarkable plea for flowers, in the course of which she says: "I have seen in fevers the most acute suffer- ing produced from the patient in a hut not , .being able to see out of a window. . . . I re- - member in my own case a nosegay of wild flowers being sent me, and from that moment recovery becoming more rapid. People say that it is the effect on the Natient's mind. It is on the patient's body, too,

-

V iv isectlon. The discussion as to this, revived at a meet- ing of the Wycliffe Brotherhood by an. expert opponent of the practice, strikes me as prac- tically interminable. On the one hand, it is readily enough conceded that vivisection, unac- • conipanied by pathological investigation, is not merely useless but vicious. The claim is that a combination of these methods forms the best means of elucidating the mysteries of disease; that medicine owes to vivisection a great deal Of ' its most accurate knowledge—that physiology; which is at the root of both Tiranches of the science, has, by such practices, discovered many important truths. On the other band, the question whether these experiments on living animals have or have not led to the aevisition of useful knowledge does not so much interest antagonists, their opposition being based on the broad moral ground that the infliction of pain on any sentient creature, unless for its own ad- vantage, is wrong—that, ip short, as the late Sir Wyke Bayliss expressed it, whether it is eon- •_ venient to be cruel has no more to do with the ! matter than whether it is convenient to be a Dar, or pleasant to be a libertine. The vivi- sectionists'. plea of justification is based wholly on the ground of utility—in our own country, that is, for on the Continent the pursuit of knowledge has all along been frankly considered • sufficient to warrant all _possible means of at- tainment—but opponents hotly repudiate the claim and denounce it as entirely fanciful. Still, somehow, we seem to get no " forrarder." Life Assurance. Last week's reference to the "Pru." set me thinking of this subject. Years ago I was par- ticularly struck by an article in "Home Notes:. the writer of which, taking for granted that a married man should insure his life, addressed himself chiefly to the question: "Do men in- sure for as much as they should?" His conten- tion was that in the majority of cases the 'amount so reserved is absurdly small. For my- self, in only a solitary instance have I known, . one 'err by going to the opposite extreme—that of a village clergyman who was so heavily in- sured that the payment of the premiums on his policies left the family too little for necessaries, and did harm to their reputation with the trad- ing community. Personally, I Tiae all along been handicapped by bad health, being long treated by insurance companies as a "bad life," and finally rejected altogether. Quite a quarter of a century ago I was warned that I had only about six months to live; but here I am still. There has not been a siirgle week's break in* our relations since my first contribu- tion to these columns, and I hope to continue to rely on the kindly forbearance of my very in- dulgent "Examiner" friends for many more happy years. As to my main point, my belief in life assurance remains firm and immovphle, and I envy those who are able to pass the essen- tial and reasonable medical test and to take advantage of the facilities nowadays offered by the Prudential and other great companies. Weather Note.

The one in last week's issue suggests another. When I was editor of a weekly paper in a dis- tant part of Lancashire, a gentleman often .sent me charts, Jct., purporting to .3foretell .the • weather for a year or so ahead ; but of course I never tOok that particular prophet very seri- ously. There is one point that is well worth considering. Elderly people are .constantlY say- ing that the seasons are changing, and really I should myself be disposed to supliort that opinion if my investigation of the subject had rot rendered such position untenable. Some years ago Sir J. W. Moore specially examined every existing record from the fourteenth cen- tury onward, including the observations taken continuously at Greenwich over the last 140 years or so, and demonstrated beyond cavil at there was no evidence at all in favour of the supposed gradual change. To qubte trom his own address at the ensuing meeting of the Brit- ish Association: The facts prove that within the last three centuries, at all events, no appreciable change has taken place in, the climate of the British . Isles. There is no scintilla of evidence to show that such change has taken place in the past or is likely to take place in the future." As one commented on the occasion, our climate is erratic, but over a long term of years remarkably constant.

"The Messiah." Now that so many local choirs are Once more manifesting their allegiance to Handel (an Anglo-German be it remembered), I will run the risk of confessing that in my judgment his Christmas masterpiece is fairly entitled to its indisputable supremacy in the realm of oratorio. Here we .have majestic grandeur allied with technical simplicity, so that the least preten- tious ef choirs fearlesidy enter the lists with more capable bodies of exponents, and in all conditions and circumstances the common people hoar gladly the beauties invariably re- vealed, whether the performers be more ad- vanced or the attempt be of the less ambitious • character. Despite the passing of the years. this monumental work not only retains all its freshness, but is assured of ever-deepening regard and wonder. Well may Handel's mighty genius arrest the indifferent, win the approval of the scholarly, and confound the hypercritical. Not " Made in Germany." • In giving readers the season's greetings, I may just state that during the week I have spent some time in looking into that matter of" the • borowed nature of our annual feast," referred to in last week's paragraph headed "One- to our Allies." I think it has been too readily assumed that, because the late Prince 'Albert in- troduced it to England, the Christmas tree owes its origin to Germany. The custom in question is older than Germany and than Christianity itself. I have traced it to ancient Egypt, from whence the Romans borrowed it; in fact, like - many others, it belongs to Paganism. My space is going rapidly; if I had room left for details; I think I could further prove without difficulty that, while undoubtedly the patron saint of Russia, Saint Nicholas, was commemorated in ! England hundreds of years ago as the children's patron; that the early Dutch settler.; in America carried the custom with them, and that Santa Claus—Father Christmas as secularised by us— came to us again direct from our Transatlantic cousins. Moreover, although evolved from the old French cosogne. the modern Christmas ' cracker, with its endless variety and triumphs of skill and ingenuity, is incontestably British.• . Again, the turkey comes to us, not from Turkey as is too commonly imagined, but from America. and the kiss (not under the mistletoe, thongh) is first mentioned in literature by an ancient . Indian poet. And .94 I might go on. Here, in a nutshell, is what I would fain enlarge upon Round Christmas Day, established in the 4th century to commemorate the birth of 3"-- Christ, and of set purpose but without hist warrant fixed for December 250, there gat). gradually all the customs and observances the Roman festivals of December and New - Year, and the task orilie Church was to purl y -.4.; these heathen customs and give the.m..11"., pos- sible, a religious, i.e., a Christian signi.fi Lance. Boom was found for every-filing, but the aim was to render every observance innocent, if not actually an act of good-is-ill and self-denial, as many of them are. Songs, presents, feasts, de- corations, the cheery fire on the beartli— all have come over into the Christian Christmas of , ' modern England from the heathen world of• , ancient Rome. The Germanic peoples had none of these customs previously, but learned them tom the Rome iu heath= times.

As the door of the private room closed behind tho young man with the white face, twitching lips, and bowed shoulders, the Junior partner of Marlow and Lockyer, West India merchants, let out a half- strangle A oath. "Mari' 6w, you're a soft-hearted fool !" lie said harshly. "If ever there was a case for prosecution you had it yonder. I held my peace because of your seniority, but—Pah !" lie broke off in disgust and flung himself back in his chair, fumbling at his cigarette case.

The senior partner was wiping his eyes. "I couldn't do it, Lockyer • I simply couldn't do it," he said unsteadily, though not apologetically. "I couldn't ruin a young fellow so utterly at the beginning of his career—nor could you." "Me?" snapped Lockyer. "But for you he would have been on his way to the police- station by now. I draw the line at encou- raging dishonesty." "But—he confessed."

"After he realised that he was suspected! I tell you, Marlow, it,. was our duty to have him punished." " Ah, Lockyer," said the older man with a sigh, "don't be afraid that he won't be punished. Think of the shame—the struggle —before him. Where can he find employ- ment without a character, without a refer- ence? I'm bitterly sorry for Dennison. I always liked the boy." "Von always liko everybody. You have no discrimination, no judgment. The wonder is that you have not . been swindled • right and left." 'Mr. Marlow did not • seem to hear. "Ldekyer," he said softly, "it is Christmas Eve."

The junior partner—a man of -five and thirty—gave a short laugh. "I was won- dering when you were going to mention that most extenuating circumstance! Unfor- tunately, to my plain mind, crime on Christ- mas Eve is just the same as crime on any other afternoon." "That is very true," the other sadly ad- mitted. "But if one cannot be merciful on Christmas Eve—eh, Lockyer?,!' Lockyer, lighting his cigarette, merely grunted impatiently. "What is to happen to the boy?" Marlow whispered. Abruptly he rose, stepped \to the door, and opening it an inch stood listening. "Feel like giving,' him a, ten-pfmnd-note and your' blessing? Mr. Lockyer ironically enquired. The elder man winced, but did not retort. Presently he increased the opening of Vie door and peered out. The office was dimly lit by a single light. The staff had left an hour ago—all save the disgraced young clerk. And he was depart- itlfr, now, after having collected a few per- sonal belongings from his desk. His hand was groping, seemingly, for the hancEe of the door—the familiar door which was about to shut him out for ever from tbe place he had enlered three years previously, se full of youthful ambition, so determined to satisfy his employers. Something like a sob escaped his labourino. breast. As his fingers at last turned the handle the senior partner came quickly a.iong the passage. "Dennison," he said in a husky whisper, "what are you going to do?" The young man shook his head, itnable to utter a word.

"I —I can't let you go like this," said Marlow. "Will you come back here .in hai- an-hour? I wish to speak to you—in private. Promise me you'll come back." With an effort the young man controlled himself and spoke. "Let Inc go, sir." "Promise me you'll conic back—iu half- an-hour. I shall be alone then." . There was a pause. "Very well, sir. I promise." •

Concerning that interview in the deserted office, amid the stillness of Christmas Eye.. Mr. Marlow would fain have kept silence, but he could not refuse to answer the (Ines.-- tions of his partner when the latter rc-- turned from his holiday. , "Believe me, Lockyer," lie .said gently, "Dennison is truly repentant; but apart from that, he has a mother—"

But for all the uncongenial natures of its partners the firm of Marlow and Lockyer went on and prospered. Perhaps the diffe- rences in temperament, the clashing of ideas, were providential in a way. A mere onlooker would have said that they were. Certainly there were occasions when the hard head of Mr. Lockyer—to leave his heart out of the question—seemed needed to balance the daring mind of his senior; for Mr. Marlow was by no means averse from speculative transactions which, it must nevertheless be admitted, generally turned out on the right side. Still, if Mr. Lockyer lessened the probable gains by putting on the break, he also lessened the possible losses, and he was a man who always looked first to the latter.

"I happen to have a wife and young children, he would remind his colleague; but this was perhaps more an excuse than a reason, for he had been born cautious. Moreover, he knew that the argument ap- pealed to the 'older man as no other argu- ment of his could be expected to do. Very well," Marlow would say, good- humouredly; "we'll reduce the present stakes; but all the same, Lockyer," he would add, "we have got to take risks—big risks—in this concern of ours. I've been in. it longer than you, and I know. Things are going easily at present, but there will be years—as there have been years in the past—when no risks will simply spell no business."

Lockycr, howeer, had convineed himself that his partner was wrong that satisfac- tory profits might always be secured with- out any extraordinary hazards. And this was the rock on which—though neither sus- pected it for years—the partnership was des- tined to split. Yet the collision -might have been longer avoided, or altogether averted, had there been only a litt' sentiment and sympathy on Lockyer's part. There are men who will not—or cannot—give something for nothing, who regard as fools and weaklings those that do so. Again and again Lockyer would have quarrelled outright with Marlow .had the latter failed in soft answers. Now it was the case of an elderly clerk who ought to be got rid of because a third part of his salary would pay for the same work done better by a young man; now that of a young clerk who .had cost the firm money through a stupid blunder which merited no- thing but dismissal; now that of an agent of the firm on a West Indian island, who begged home-leave three months before it was due, lest he should not see his father alive—an unjustifiable request, considering that the agency had not yet begun to pay. But the cases over which the partners differed need not be enumerated here. They occurred frequently, and the accompanying friction did not decrease with repetition. Lockyer became more and more irritated by what he termed Marlow's unbusinesslike methods; Marlow began to experience an in- creasing sense of disgust at Lockyer's ;ap- parent lack of common humanity. Something like a crisis was reached on a certain morning, nearly seven years from the date of our first meeting with Marlow and Lockyer. Ma.rlow looked up from a letter consisting of a few lines which he had taken from an envelope bearing a foreign postmark. His countenance was radiant. _ _

"Lockyer ! What do you think of this? I've heard from Dennison at last!" "Dennison? Who is—oh, you mean the thief." The other winced. "Let that be forgotten. I always believed he would redeem himself." , "Has he written to say so?" "He has returned the firm's money, and the money that I gave him—with interest!" cried Marlow in a ' tone of triumph. "Now what have you to say?" Lockyer gave his shoulders a shrug. "Let us hope he came by it honestly," he said coolly. "Does he mention its source?" Marlow made a movement as if he had been stung. "Confound you!" • he ex- claimed, "are you as devoid of justice as you were of mercy? Won't you give Denni- son any credit at all?" "If you wish me to do so, I'll see that he gets credited in the books against the record of his , defalcations." The older min drew in big breath. He restrained himself with difficulty. At last he said: "Lockyer, if Dennison were to enter this room now, what would you do?" "Give him a receipt for the money." "Nothing else?" "What else should I give him?" "Your hand, your goodwill--" "Oh, rot, Marlow ! It isn't Christmas Eve, you know ! " Marlow's countenance flushed red and went pale. "Lockyer," he said pasiionately, "you're .a fool—a heartless fool !" Which was scarcely the criticism expected by the junior partner. Six. months later came the split. There was no quarrel. Marlow mildly declared that he had had enough of non-speculative, humdrum business. Ile would retire, and spend the next few years of his leisure in seeing the „ world. Lockyer did his liest to conceal his gratification at the rzospect of running the business alono undcr the old name, but by no means on the old lines. They. parted on quite. amicable terms. IV.

In the dusk of the 24th 'of December Mr. Lockyer emerged from the portals of n great bank. He looked the crushed and beaten man lie was. The manager of the bank, sincerely regretful, for the busines.s relationship had been long and friendly, had blasted his last hope. The bank could not extend further credit, and the heavy bills payable on the 27th would be dishonoured. After that—bankruptcy. To a modern business many things may happen in the course of three years. The history of Marlow and' Lockyer over that period, would mean a long story. Enough to say that Lockyer, handicapped by his initial borrowing, had found the first two years so profitless that he had dropped his principles and entered desperately into big risks. But a certain genius is required for successful speculation, and that genius seemed to have quitted the business along with Marlow. The hazards of Lockyer resulted in nothing but :disaster.

He was alone in his private room—alone on the premises. The clerks—their number reduced since Marlow's time—had gone an hour ago, the youngsters in holiday mood, the elders vaguely uneasy. Lockyer trusted ni one with his secrets, but such things leak out, and his manner on his return from , the bank had been very strange. He sat at his desk, motionless, his head in his. hands. There was nothing to detain him there. He was simply afraid to go home. Ile had kept his wife in the dark regarding his affairs. The younger members of his family—the oldest, a girl was not yet six- teen—were having a pay that evening. How could lie face them all? He felt that the mere sight of him would blight their happiness. My • God!" he whispered, "what a hideous thing to happen on Christmas Eve! His tired mind went back to its feverish, futile search—the search •that had occupied it throughout those recent torturing weeks— the search for a way of escape, for the name of some fellow being who might possibly be induced to lend a helping hand. But it was only another spasm of vain beating against the' bars. There was no way out; there was no fellow being who would aid—save, per- haps, one, Marlow. Surely Marlow would do it—if only because it was Christmas Eve. But Marlow was still wandering abroad, Lockyer knew not where. His senses dulled by his mental misery, Lockyer scarcely heard the opening of the outer door. When footsteps drew near the private room he did not stir. A clerk re- turned for some article forgotten—the char- woman—it Mattered not. But when a knock fell on the door, he sat up, groped for a pencil, and feigned to be busy. He mois- tened his lips and got out the words "Come in."

A stranger entered; a man still young, well-dressed, but apparently ill at case. "I beg your pardon," lie said, halting awkwardly just within the threshold,"" but I saw the light in the window, and I was anxious to have a word with Mr. -Marlow." He paused, paling -a little as he met the curious dull stare of Lockyer's eyes. "Has Mr. Marlow gone home? I should like very much to find him to-night." Lockyer cleared his throat. "Mr. Marlow is abroad. I do not know his address." "Abroad!" The word was charged with dismay. "Can you tell nio when he will return?" The question came after another pause. "I cannot." The stranger sighed. "I had hoped," he said softly, "to offer—to do Mr. Marlow a little service. I arrived from Buenos Aires but an hour ago. The steamer was delayed at the last." "May I ask who you are?" Lockycr had been asking himself who the stranger, faintly familiar, might be, but his over- wrought memory had offered no answer. The stranger sighed again. "Don't you remember Dennison, Mr. Lockyer?" "Dennison?" "The thief on whom you ,had such great mercy, eleven years ago' this very night?" "Mercy?—l? " "You, Mr. Lockyer. Ah, Mr. Marlow told me how you sacrificed your principled to bo lenient and how you joined with him in giving me that hundred pounds—" "Stop!" For a moment Lockyer's pallid countenance was scarlet. "I see, you don't wish me to refer to it, said Dennison. "Well, my thanks. would never give you any idea of my gratitude." "You owe me no gratitude." Dennison smiled faintly. "Nothing Fan can ever say," he said gently, "Will con- vince me of that. But now I can say in you what I wished to. say to Mr. Marlow. I had Mr. Marlow first in my mind because he was senior; and also, perhaps, because he-- shook hands with me that night. I er't tell you what that meant, Mr. Lockyer.' There was a silence. The ruined man made as if to rise. What was he now that he should refuse his hand? Had he been so very honest in the risking of other people's money? Legitimate business, no doubt—but could it be called absolutely straight? He sank back in his :.chair. At least he •seed not act the hypocrite. "I must repeat," he said stiffly, "that you owe me no gratitude—quite the reverse." Once more Dennison smiled faintly and ‘hook '.his, head. "As you will, sir. But I know what Mr. Marlow told me. And now—"' "I ought to have 'informed 'you that Mr. Marlow is no, longer a member of this firm. He retired more than three years ago." "Retired! Then he is not involved—l beg your pardon, Mr. Lockyer. It may seem impertinent, but is—is everything well and prosperous with him?" "I have no reason to doubt it, Mr. Denni- son. He left the firm at the height of its prosperity." Lockyer was writing errati- cally on the blotting-paper. "At the height of its prosperity," lie muttered. "Height of its prosperity." He had become ghastly. "Height of—" "Mr. Lockyer, you're ill!" cried Denni.

son. _ "I'm perfectly well—perfectly well—per- , fectly—' Dennison took a step forwald. "You are in great trouble. On board the steamer I heard—no, never mind that. But if You can tell me a little, perhaps--" Lockyer pulled himself together. "What id you hear on the steamer. Then he collapsed again. "Oh, what does it matter? It'll Le in the papers in a few days. We suspend payment on Friday." Dennison's lips quivered slightly as he looked down on the bowed head of the man whom he counted a benefactor. He took an envelope from his pocket. "Mr. Loekyer," lm said shyly, "this is a draft on the Bank of England for eight thousand pounds. I will endorse it to the firm. If it should prove sufficient to saVe your credit, find happiness in the thought till the end of my life. I beg you to accept it."

New Books.

Belgian Poetry, including Verhaeren. The Walter Scott Publishing Co., Ltd. 11- net. This book is a collection of • modern Belgian verse, selected and translated by Mr. J. Bithell, who bas furnished the reader with a very inter- esting introduction, giving particulars of the life and works of the various writers upon whom he draws iii this volume. The great attraction of the book is, of course, the large number of poems by 'Verhaeren, whidh. Mr. Bithell has translated with rare skill. Let us particularly commend to the reader three poems that are terrible in their truth arid realism—" The Town."" The Music 1101," and " The Butcher's Stall." And as an example of Verhaeren's power in dealing with well-known themes; we would instance "The Sovran Rhythm." Apart from Verhaeren, the book contains selections from Severin, Mockel, Maeterlinck, Le Roy, Uirand, Gerardy, Elskamp, and others. The translations are well done, and the book ought. we think, to appeal to a wide public at a time like the present when Belgium hies estab- lished for herself a position unique .in modern times.

The Walter Scott Publishing Co., Ltd., have just added to their famous collection of reprints —the Scott Library—Silvio Pellico's "Prison Memoirs," with an introduction by Frederick j. Crowest.

TEACHER-SOLDIERS.

Education Committee's Generous

Allowances.

The question of the amounts to be paid to teachers serving in the .Army was discussed at the meeting of the Warrington Education Com- mittee on Monday, when Councillor Roberts pre- sided.

The minutes of the School Management Com- mittee contained the following resolutimi: That the nominee of each unmarried teacher be paid such 4% 811 M, if any, as, with the total amount of the Army pay, will ho equal to the salary of the teacher concerned; such payment to take effect from the date of the teacher's en- listment."

_ Mr. Upson said he -was sure the committee would approve of the action to pay all teachers, married or -.unmarried, who had joined the ! forces, their full salary, minus what was Paid In' the government. The government pay in- ' eluded not only the actual pay to the teacher, but, in the case of a married Mall it included the separation allowance credited to his wife. It was the intention of the committee to add to- gether all that was contributed by the State, and deduct it from the salary which the teacher was receiving, and pay the nominee the differ- ence. Mr. Unsworth suggested that the words "or ' other allowances " be added after " Army pay." He thought the term " Army pay " would not be applicable in the case of other. dependents and mothers. "Or other allowances" would cover everything. Alderman Bennett said the committee • had done the right thing, for they all felt that they should do all they could to ensure that those who were willing to serve their country in the present grave crisis, should not be the sufferers. Mr. Kingdon: ThiS means that the married' teachers are going to cost the town less than the unmarried teachers. The ' Chnirman : ,Yes. BACHELORS AN EXPENSIVE LUXURY. Mr. Kingdon : The committee might make a note of that—bachelors are an expensive luxury, and make arrangements' accordingly in the future. (Laughter) The Chairman having asked Mr. Upson if lie was agreeable to Mr. Unsworth's suggestion being adopted. Mr. Upson said he was quite willing to do that so long as they had established the principle. What they wanted to ensure was that teachers who had joined the forces should not be in a worse position than if they had not joined. ' Mr. Moore Murray (director of education): It will be put quite right. The Chairman : I am afraid we cannot add. those words. It will be better to withdraw the resolution for the present, but act as stated in the meantime. The committee agreed to this course, and the minutes were confirmed. DR. LUMB'S COMMISSION. The following item appeared on the minutes of the Medical Inspection Committee: " It was noted that Dr. J. Lumb, the assistant school medical offlicer, had obtained a COMMiSSiOII in the Royal Army Medical Corps, and that his leave of absence had commenced on the sth December, 1914.-!' Mr. Kingdon said they were sorry to lose Dr. Mud), but they felt he had gone in a good cause. One of the satisfactory features in the present difficulty was the way iii which medical men had willingly given their services. The minutes were confirmed. Dr. Manson has been appointed teacher of sick nursing at the Evening Technical Institute, for the remainder of the present session at a salary of £8 Bs., in place of Dr. J. Lumb.
