SLAV v. TEUTON.

Instructive Debate by the Philomathic

Society.

RUSSIAN ATTRIBUTES.

There was a good attendance at the Trinity Chambers on Wednesday evening of last week, when the members of the Warrington Philo- rnathic Society held an interesting and. instruc- tive debate on" The Slav and Teuton Races," The President, Mr. N. Nield, occupied the chair, and the virtues of the respective races were extolled by Messrs. J. B. Storey and F. Pox. The Chairman said he had been struck by the strong antipathy shown by the Russian nation •to the Servians—who were a Slav race. His im- pression was that the Russians were a sort of barbaric race; although, on the other hand, many writers of the present day did not think so. In any case, their old ideas of the Russians needed to be altered a lot. The Germans, they had come to consider, were the victims of cir- cumstances.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RUSSIAN CHARACTER.

Mr. J. B. Storey said there were (.7i.);;tinental people who claimed that the present war was a gigantic struggle between Slav and Teuton. They in England were of a different opinion; but there was no denying the fact that the murder, by Slays, of an anti-Slav ruler, was its indirect cause. What of this great Slav race, of which Russia was the head, and whose members were found scattered all over South-Eastern Europe? Russia-in-Europe alone had as many Slays as there were inhabitants, of the whole of the German states; and belonging, as they did, to the Aryan branch of the human family, they were of the same stock as the inhabitants of the British Isles.

He took the Russians to be the typical Slays. In appearance they did not suffer by comparison with any other people in Europe, and were re- markable for their power of enduring extrema heat and cold. They were all brothers, and, as a matter of fact, the, English were liked in Russia—for they could fraternise, having many things in common; but they must not concern themselves with Russian polities. The poorest and humblest Russian could rise to the highest State position, and all were apt and ready stu- dents. They were reverential—affected by the vast extent and seeming mystery.of their snow- clad Steppes, their enormous forests, stretching for scores of miles, the ti'age of infinitude over- hanging the vast plains—and all this foond ex- pression in their music. In no branch of art could they imitate. In drama they were realists, and, as time went on, one was likely to hear more of Russian drama. The literary output of Russia was very great. - An outstanding feature of the Russian charac- ter was its capacity for self-sacrifice for an ideal. In this respect the Russian, .perhaps, went to greater lengths even than the Anglo-Saxon. The revolutionaries had shown that,, and also the Russian Government by the recent suppression of the sale of vodka. At thb same time the Rus- sians had a tendency to take life easily—though this peculiarity was not inherent in their char- acter, being rather the result of their system. Profits were divided amongst the community as a whole, and thus there was no incentive for individual effort—Russia's handicap ! Russia was a country that furnished many surprises for the traveller. One had only to go a few miles from a Russian town, into the country, to find that one had dropped from the twentieth to the sevefiteenth or eighteenth cen- tury. Life in a Russian village or hamlet was, in these days, 'not unlike what it had been in this country two hundred years ago, so far as Modern conveniences were concerned. This, , however, did not trouble the villagers. Whatever their mission in the 'world, the serious, kindly and manly Russians would never be 'cursed with the spirit of militarism, for this was diametrically opposed to the temperament of the Slavonic race. Their recent awakening was due to oppression in the Balkans, indirectly by the Prussians and directly by the Turks. The future welfare of the wald depended upon the mutual esteem and friendship of the Anglo- Saxon and Slavonic' races. (Applause.)

GERMAN PROGRESS. Mr. F. Fox said that a people at war, espe- cially such a highly-strung and nervous people as the Germans were,•exhibited characteristics not otherwise seen even amongst Ithe people themselves,, and certainly not shown to the casual visitor. They must to a certain extent, however, consider one race in the light of the other; and from that point of view the present time was unfortunate for him, in view of the adverse reports of the German behaviour. After touching upon the history and constitu- tion of the German Empire, and accounting for the king's desire to control a world-empire, the speaker said there was no reason for the nobil- ity to be pacific. All their interests lay on the side of aggression; they could not even imain dormant, because, with the prosperity of the country and the awakening of the democracy, their very existence was menaced.

The natures of the peoples of Germany were as diverse as those of Great Britain, and more so. There were the warm, generous and cheer- ful people of Southern Germany, as opposed to the cold, domineering Northerner. At the same time, certain features stood out which differed from those of most other races. The outstanding characteristic of the German was, undoubtedly, his thoroughness—his he exhibited in —his capacity for patience 'organisation, and the earVing out his work to the minutest detail. That, however, did not imply inventive genius, of which he was deficient: the Germans had built up their Empire . by imitating other nations. The conditions of labour were splendid, the organisation of the industries being brought to perfection; for with the German, the question was :"Will this work lead to something new?" whereas the Englishman asked: " Will it pay?" suited to to linestlie That class of work iwaosddin eminently a on; German—a patient il which might .lead to immense profit; or nothing at all! In less than twenty years the foreign trade of Germany had nearly trebled, and she was now the second manufacturing country of Europe. Education was compulsory, and they found less than half per cent, of illiterates in the Army. On the technical side, their education was more thorough than that of the English schools.' Even toys were often made with the object of stimulating the children's interest in mechanics. The universities, also, had accom- modation for 60,000 students. Again, German, progress was due, to a cam• bination of commercialism and science, leiding to reduction in waste of material and labour— with a corresponding fall in the cost of produc- tion. Great facilities were given by the banks. The success which the Germans had attained had made its ,mark upon their character: they: were proud of their nationality, ambitious, and confident of their ability to lead the world. They felt they were a great nation; and everything was done on a large scale. The effect of mili- tary training was seen throughout the country, and in all classes. It made them methodical and amenable to discipline in the highest degree —especially the working classes, which it. had made servile.

The Germans were also passionately devoted to music. From highest to lowest, every village had its choir, and every tOwn its opera house. : Children were taught Music in the free schools. Music ht one form or another was with them all the time; it was part of their nature. On the other hand, they could not boast of a highly- developed aesthetic sense, although one might have expected great artists from such lovers of Nature as they were. The peasantry were deeply religious; whilst, amongst the other classes, religion was more a matter of form. Politically, the German was not brilliant; and, apart from the social democrats, polities , did not interest them; because, on the whole, they were fairly comfortable, and considered that the Government was looking well after their interests. Itf any case, the people had practically no influence over the policy of -the' Government, us could he seen from a study of the constitution of the Empire. But the people bed been awakening, as Bernhardi said; they had been becoming too peace-loving,. so there was nothing for it hut war. (Applause.) Au interesting discussion ensued, in which Messrs. Ni Niel& James Walsh, F. Chadwick, Sherburn, Winstanley,' H. H. Taylor and Brookes took part; the two principal speakers afterwards giving lengthy replies to the various , questions and arguweas, •

A SCORELESS FIRST HALF. The first half was scoreless, and the fault—if

fault it be---was mainly Wairington's, for some excellent opportunities that came their way were allowed to slip by; whilst they also failed to make anything like tho use they ouklit to have clone of the very houful slope down which they were.,playing. The greasy state of the ball and _ . _ _ _

the slippery condition of ground, plus the

aforementioned mistiness, no doubt had much to do with Warrington's discomfiture, but really one was at a loss to know how they. managed to miss some of the openings that offered them- selves. Time and again Dainteth. and Young prepared the way for them, but they would not, or could not, take it. Tranter, too, gave them more than one useful lead, but it was of no avail. Jolley proved a veritable pillar of strength to his side in the first half, giving thoroughly sound exposition of the Rugby game and taking the sting out of more than one ugly Batley attack. When he had to leave the field he was sorely missed. Towards the end of the first half he applied the check in most effectual fashion to a most promising run on the part of • two of Batley's fast wingers, thus averting what looked like a. certain score; a' feat for which, by the way, he was warmly applauded by as many of the three thousand spectators as were permitted by the fog to see it.

BATLEY ASSERT THEMSELVES. The second half opened with some brisk work on both sides of the line, each team attacking in turn. Gradually, however, the fervour died out of the Warrington onslaughts; and Batley, noting this, made good use of the fact and re- doubled their efforts to score, and at length, as a 'reward for their persistence; the opportunity was given them to force home one of their at- ta;cks. Brannan and Grandig,e got fairly going and indulged in some pretty passing„ until pre- sently the first-named saw his opportunity and, seizing it, dashed over, the line, after some smart dodging. This, the first score of the match, was lustily applauded by the crowd, who found in it an exceltent opportunity for warming their cold-benumbed fingers after the usual 'fashion. The goal-'kick was taken by Lockwood, who made no mistake. The second and last piece of scoring came close on the heels of the first. Battersby intercepted a swinging pass and gave the ball to Loughlin, who was in a .favourable Position and had little trouble in nipping over the line. Grandige took the kick, and, with a fine lift, added the minor points. His kick was accorded a round of plaudits, both in recog- nition of its merits as a piece of punting, and also of the fact that it was his first score for his new team, for his appearance in the 'Batley colours on Saturday was by way of being a slebut.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
resourcefulness, as well as plenty of pluck, whilst he was Altogether superior to his Vis-a- vis—Lyons. He was greatly missed by his side, and also by the crowd, for his dash and tricki- ness are always sources of satisfaction to the spectator who goes to look on at a match with- out taking violently-partisan .spectacles with him.

HALF-BACKS' GOOD GAME.

Dainteth and Young, the Warrington half- backs, worked together with better understand- ing than they have displayed before this season and were among the most useful men in the whole bake,r's dozen. Had a tithe of the open- ings they created been taken up, the score-board would have read quite differently when the long whistle 'sounded. Lately the pair. have shown signs of improving with practically every match and they are to-day one of the best pairs of half-backs within the jurisdiction of the Northern Union. The Warrington three- quarter line, on the whole, played a creditable game; Brookes and Tranter being the outstand- ing pair in the quartet, though Waywell and .Bradshaw also put in good work and gave evi- denee of &tent powers that the slippery state of the ground prevented them from putting into exercise. Among the visiting forwards the two Cunliffe's worked hard, as also did Cox; indeed, the trio were quite indefatigable, and had their skill and speed been equal to their energy the game tight easily have ended quite differently. The elevation of Cunliffe to a place in the first string was amply justified in this, his first match with them.

JOLLEY'S SUBSTITUTE OUT OF HIS ' ELEMENT.

After Joney had been placed hors de combat, Green left Warrington's front rank in order to go to the rear and do duty for him. The sub- stitute tried hard, but it was quite obvious that a front-rank position is the one for which he is best adapted. Parker was the pick of the home threequar- ters; indeed, he was easily the best threequarter on the field. Lockwood and Thomas worked con- scientiously, but they were obviously inferior, as a pair of 'halves, to Da,inteth 4tnd Noung• Batley's front rank proved themselves resource- ful and speedy, and in the second half they quite outshone the visiting sextet, and, to them, in the main, was the Yorkshire men's victory due, for they were not content to wait for chances to be made for them; but, whenever posidble; set about making theM themselves. The next encounter between Batley and War- rington will be looked forward to with interest, for one cannot help thinking that, especially in so far as the second half was concerned, Satur- day's game was not a real trial of the respec- tive merits of the two teams, Warrington ob- viously allowing themselves to be, shall we say, overawed, or overcome, by the uncomfortable climatie conditions, and rendered unduly timor- ous by the treacherous state of the turf. More- over, just when he was most needed, they were deprived of the services of their " jolly reliable old Jolley."

TIIE TRUE WAR SPIRIT.

Supporter Pays His "Sub." and

"Another Member's."

Mr. Pemberton, the hon. treasurer of the Warrington Football Club, has received the fol- lowing letter from Driver Swinton, 50 Co., M.T.A.S.C., British Expeditionary Force in France:—

"In a border town.

" Dear Mr. Pemberton,— "Just a short note to let you know that I haven't forgotten the old club. I think you are doing very well, considering the weak- ened forces at your disposal. I occasionally meet some of the old members out here, and thcy are always anxious to know if the ' Wire- pullers " are doing well. I enclose twelve shil- lings (in British Postal Orders) crossed per a/c. Six.shillings of it is my membership sub- scription and the other six shillings of it is to pay another member's (your selection), who, through being called up for service, has not been able to renew his subscription. Hoping to get back in time for next season, and wishing you every success both in matches and finance also a Happy• Christmas and a Brighter and Pros- perous New Year to all, I remain, yours sin- cerely, J. SWINTON (of Cuerdon Hall, Thelwall, and Long-row, Orford)."

NORTHERN UNION CUP.' RYLANDS' RECS. v. SPRING VIEW.

This match in the second qualifying iound of he N.H. Cup Competition, was played at Gorsey- lane 'on Saturday in wretched weather, and before a small attendance. Both sides were well represented, though A. Horrocks was mis- sing from the home team. Spring View kicked off_ and .I'. Horrocks returning, good play by Gallon took the ball to the visitors' end, but it was cleared. From a pack formed on the "25" line, H. povey got over. The goal-kick failed. Potter, shortly afterwards, scored for the home team, and Gallop converted. Good play by the Brothers Povey ended-in C. .Povey being pushed into touch. Griffiths, taking a loose ball, ran through the visiting, team and ended by scoring uoder the posts, Warren converting. Half- time arrived with the score: Rylands', 2 goals 3 tries (13 points); Spring View, nil. Brandwood commenced the second half, the visitors returning play. Gallop was tackled in possession and from ..the penalty. the visitors secured a goal. From this point onward the visitors were outclassed, tries being scored for Rylands' by H. Povey, C. Povey (two), and Warren, and Shaw and Brandwood between them converting three out of the four. The final result was: Rylands' 5 goals 7 tries (31 points); Spring View, 1 penalty goal- (2 points). The Brothers Povey were in fine form, passing and re-passing repeatedly. All the forwards played well.

A WAR SPECTACLE.

" KULTUR " ILLUSTRATED IN A GREAT WATER DRAMA.

A war spectacle based on incident in Bel- gium is a notable feature of the Christmas pro- ductions in Manchester. There are few places in the country where such an ambitious work as " Kultur" can be produced, and even the vast resources of the Manchester Hippodrome are taxed to the utmost in staging this magnificent aqua-drama. The author and producer, Leonard F. Durell, has handled a difficult task with courage, and produced a good imitation of some of the stern realities of war. The drama is preceded by a couple of tottmlix, one representing the "Mailed Fist "—a huge hand suspended over a number of dead and dying young women huddled together. The first scene is a town in Flanders, and in- troduces a Belgian inn, a German spy, and a love story. One sees the peaceable inhabitants in everyday life. The news arrives that the Germans have occupied Brussels. Presently it party of Uhlans come upon the scene. One is killed while attempting an infamous asTiult; the others ride off.

Infantrymen find the dead body, and, after shooting the priest for disclaiming any know- ledge of it, withdraw. The town is thiTillaaii- barded, and the curtain rises to reveal the houses in ruins.

King Albert is introduced in an episode near the battle area, and the Kaiser gallops aown the "only open road" before a gallant Belgian officer-lover, and the girl he has rescued from the clutches of the modern Hun releases the sluice gates. We see a thrilling fiefit between the 'Highlanders and Brandenburgers, anil the Prus- sian hordes swept away in the swirling waters to their doom.

The audience get a liberal supply of thrills. The German system of terrorism is amply illus- trated, and there is plenty of killing of defence- less non-combatants and other brutalities. These grim sensations are relieved here and there by touches of broad ,comedy.

The a-cting fs good, and the scenic effects are on the gigantic scale one is accustomed to in the great water pageants at the Hippodrome. "Kultur" should be very popular. After seeing this at the first house our readers who desire to make a "full night" of their visit should journey across to the Ardwick Empire for the second house. Here there is a really excellent pantomime, "Jack and Jill," with the great Lancashire comedian, George Formby, keeping everyone merry with his pecu- liarly quaint humour. .

Longford "Do It On" Hollins Green.

BAPTIST'S "THROW UP THE SPONGE."

Notes by "Linesman."

The most interesting match in the Warrington and District League on Saturday was provided by the return fixture of the two near and keen rival clubs, Paddington and' Fairfield United. This game had been looked forward .to very keenly by the clubs' supporters and players alike, for the Paddington boys intended to re- venge themselves for the defeat they sustained in the first encounter. The United had strength- ened their team by the inclusion of Scott:bitch- field and Poulter, but the familiar figure of the popular Millward was missing. The three other players named, having re-joined the Great Sankey team, were, however, available for this match. On the run of the game play was keen and fast, and both teams had their turn in at- tack and defence, and the result of one goal each may be considered a, very fair reflex of the respective merits of the teams. Harrison, E. Itolmes and Wainwright were ..the most pro- minent players among the Paddington team, Whilst Hunter, Ditchfield and Poulter were the most useful of the United contingent.

Hollins Green were the visitors to the Horse and Jockey ground, where they,, 'opposed the Lowford team, but they were handicapped by having only ten players, and throughout the game the home tpain had the best of the ex- changes. . Ellam, in the visitors' goal, • made many•fine saves and could not be blamed for the shots that eluded him; and he was well •sup- ported by Clarke and Martin. Wright was the best man in the, Greenites' middle line. For the Longford team, Finch, Sherratt and Hands showed up well, although Stevens had the dis- tinction of scoring the best goal of the match.

The Elmwood Cons., who had been eagerly looking forward to their return fixture with the Latchford Baptists, were sadly disappointed by the non-appearance of the latter. This was owing to the inability of the Baptists' Commit- tee to raise a team. As late as Saturday the Secretary of the Leagne was informed of the Baptists' intention to withdraw from the com- petition.

In the North Cheshire League the. Stockton Heath team were successful in annexing a couple of points from Altrineham St. George's. The game was very evenly contested in the first half, when the scores were one goal each. In the second moiety the home team asserted their superiority and added three more goals. Lloyd, on the extreme right, played a very good game for the "Heathens," and scored a couple of excellent goals.

The Orford White Star were disappointed in their fixture with Grappenhall St. Wilfrid's; more so, as the match Was arranged to be played on the latter club's ground. Not a single player of the home club turned up. Do Grappenhall men only play when the weather is fine?

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT

LEAGUE.

LONGFORD v. HOLLINS GREEN.

The above teams met on the ground of the first-named, :when the following players opposed each other • Longford : Sturgess; Lawrance and Mason; Holden, Fearnley and Sherratt; Smith, Smart, Stevens, Hands and Bennett. Hollins Green: Ellani; Clarke and Martin; Mellor, Billington and Wright; Perrin, D. Bil- lington, Atherton and Atkinson. The referee was Mr. L. Thompson, Earlestown. The Longford captain won the toss, and D. Billington kicked off for the visitors. The home team gained possession and their forwards rushed down on the visitors! goal and Hands scored for the home team with a capital shot. From the centre-kick the visitors, who only had ten men, could not make much headway, and they were soon driven back to their own goal area, when Stevens sent the ball just over the bar. Shortly afterwards Smart, receiving a well- timed pass from Hands, scored the second goal. Nettled by these reverses, the visitors made a determined attempt to reduce the arrears, but their forwards were at fault with bad shooting, and the home team again assumed the aggres- sive. but Ellam, in goal, and Clarke and Wright defended splendidly and prevented any further scores, the interval being called with the score 2-0 in favour of the home team.

On restarting, the visitors had the best of the exchanges. Their forwards combined very well and made attacks on the home goal, and Stur- gess was called upon to handle several times. Wright, after a fine run, sent just wide of the posts. The home team carried play into the visitors' quarters, where Ellam, after making several good clearances, had to concede a corner. This was got away, and soon after Sherratt re- ceived the ball, and, beating several opponents, sent in a capital shot, which Ellam fisted out. The ball went to Finch and he scored the third goal, whilst Stevens put on the f.ourth and last, just before the final whistle sounded. Result: Longford, 4 goals; Hollins Green, nil.

PADDINGTON v. FAIRFIELD UNITED.

The return match between the above teams was played on Saturday, on the ground of the first-named club. There is great rivalry be- tween these teams, and that, added to the fact that the latter are at the head of the League, drew a good crowd. Mr. M. J. Kite, of Thelwall, had charge of the game, and the following Players turned out Paddington: Harrison; E. Holmes and Cham- berlain; W. Holmes, J. Taylor and Spencer: Wainwright, Garnett, Darbyshire, Smith and Bra,mhall.

Fairfield United: Potter; Bebbington and Woodhouse; Gleave, Hunter and Lewis; Scott, Taylor, Poulter, Ditchfield and S. Woodhouse.

The visitors started with only ten players, but Poulter and Taylor, backed up by Hunter, made tracks to the home goal area, where Taylor was only inches wide with his shot. Wainwright, on the home right wing, was next prominent with a good run along the touch-line, and be finally gave Potter a warm shot to handle, but it was cleared at the expense of a corner-kick. From the goal-kick J. Taylor got possession and he passed on to Darbyshire, but the latter, in steadying himself for a final shot, was neatly robbed of the ball by Woodhouse, the leather being returned by Holmes. The veteran, Hun- ter, who proved a great stumbling-block to the opposing forwards, transferred to Ditchfield, and lie, along with S. Woodhouse, led an attack on the home goal, the movement ending shot which brought Harrison to his knees. The home team then took up the aggressive and forced several corner-kicks in quick succession, but the visitors! defence prevailed. Lewis transferred to Taylor, and the latter, aided by Scott, gained a little ground, xhen W. ITolmes intercepted the ball and placed to Garnett, and, , from the latter's shot at goal, Behhington ruis- kicked and let in Bramhall, who In:d not much difficulty in scoring, after thirty irinutes' play. From this on, -up to the interval, the game 'a strenuously contested, but np .further goals re- sulted. Hall-time: Paddington, one goal ; Fair- field United, nil. - . .

On the resumption, the game was •ontested keenly and a good pace was set up, the home team being the first to become dangerous. A shot from Wainwright was well cleared by Pot- ter. Then, following even exchanges in mid- field, a mistake by Chamberlain let in Poulter, whose shot struck the upright. The visitors set up a hot bombardment of the home goal, Taylor, Scott and Poulter each shooting in turn; but they failed to beat Harrison and his backs, who eventually cleared the ball. The visitors re- turned to the attack, when Hunter sent in a bard drive. The ball struck Holmes, who failed to clear properly, and a scrummage in goal ensued, from which Ditchfield scored the equalising goal ten minutes from time. A WATER-LOGGED GROUND.

The fixture between the Bank Quay Recs. and St. Barnabas' had been re-arranged and was due for decision on Saturday, on the Lovely-lane ground, but, owing to the heavy rains during the week and in the forenoon, the referee (Mr. (3. Knight) had no hesitation in declaring the ground unfit for play. It was in a shocking con- dition, the water being three or four inches deep on a good portion of the playing.piece.

LATCHFORD BAPTISTS AGAIN DEFAULT. The return fixture between Elmwood Cons. and Latchford Baptists should have been played on Satuiday,.but the Baptists failed to put in an appearance and much disappointment was caused in the Elmwood camp, as this match had been looked forward to as a kind of " Derby " between the two clubs.

NORTH CHESHIRE LEAGUE.

STOCKTON HEATH v. ALTRINCHAM ST. GEORGE'S.

The above teams met in the second division of the North Cheshire League on the West- avenue ground, before a fair number of specta- tors. The turf was in a heavy condition after the recent rains. The following players turned out, under the control of Mr. H. Smith, of Latchford t

Stockton Heath Hooper; Gosling and Ches- ters ; Boylance, Noden and Hamblett; Lloyd, Pearce, Vernon, Buck and Starkey. St. George's: Johnson; Jackson and Ashton; Wallace, Prow and Kelley; Tansey, Bowyer, Harris and Slaier.

The visitors, who played with only ten men, won the toss and set the home 'team to defend the farm goal. From the kick-off the visitors very quickly put the home team on the defensive and their forwards being in front of goal they 'were penalised for off-side. ut they still con- tinued to have the best of the exchanges and gained three corner-kicks in quick succession. However, they failed to p"ofit from them. After a spell of mid-field play, the visitors returned to the attack and Harris opened the score after fifteen minutes. , This reverse nettled the "Heathens," but the visitors, showing the better tactics, held them at bay, and it was only by occasional raids that the home team gave any signs of being dangerous. It was from' one of these breakaways that Buck equal- ised the scores. The interval arrived with the teams on equal terms, with one goal each to their credit..

On resuming, the game was very evenly con- tested. Lloyd got the ball from Noden and centred. Vernon received It and passed across the mouth of the• visitors' goal. Buck and Starkey were too slow to accept the opening, but, shortly after, Pearce put the "Ileatliens'' in front, with cross-shot. Following raid-field exchanges, Noden,sefit Out to. Lloyd, whose speed served him well. Ile beat the opposing back and then scored with a shot that did not give Johnson a chance of saving. A "few minutes later the same player scored again. From the centre-kick the visitors contrived to trouble the home defence, and Tansey gave Hooper a warm shot to deal with. Bowever, it was :gafely got away. Harris missed scoring-from a good open- ing, whilst, at the opposite end, Johnson had to leave his goal on two occasions to clear. The result was: Stockton Heath, 4 goals; Altrineham St. George's, 1 goal.

LEFT IN THE LURCH.

A match between the Grappenhall St. Wil- frid's and Orford White Star had been arranged to be nlaved on the ground of the former. off Knutsford-road. This was not very satisfactory treatment to the White Star team and officials, because had the Grappenhall officials informed them the match would not be played, the White Star team could have had a League match arranged for them.

AN ENORMOUS HELP.

- - . A message was received from Mr. Lloyd George stating that the election of Mr. Masterman would be an enormous help to the Government at this critical period. He described Mr. Mae- terman as an ideal candidate for a constituency such as that of the Swansea district, for lie had deep sympathy with Welsh ideals, and had studied the working class problem more deeply and sincerely than any other member of the Government.

411 MR. MASTERMAN'S LIBERAL RIVAL -4 Mr. T. Jeremiah Williams says he will con- test the seat on the ground that undue influencO' was brought to bear in favour of Mr. Mast man. He says that if the ordinary course 0 events in the constituency hsd to be iuterfer , with action should have been taken througW•.: the Welsh Liberal Parliamentary party and the ' Welsh Liberal Council. and that it should not have been done through the intervention of 0 Cabinet Minister. Mr. Williams, who is an employer of labour i and popular in the constituency, is the son 0 a former member, Mr. William Williams. Maesygwernen.

NITRATES FOR GERMANY CAPTURED.

A Valuable Cargo. The German sailing ship Melpomene, 109 tons, and carrying 3,000 tons of nitrates, bound for Hamburg from the West Coast of America, has arrived as a prize at Swansea. After her seizure she had an adventurous voyage, the tow-rope breaking off Land's End. and the vessel running to Lundy under bare poles. ' The value of her cargo is over ..c3o,ffffe, and the ship herself is worth another 4MOOO.

WAR AND EDUCATION.

Reforms Deferred in Lancashire.

At a meeting of the Lancashire Education Committee at Preston on Monday, Sir 11. nib- bert, M.P., presiding, Mr. J. J. Cockshott, chair- man of the Elementary Education Sub-com- mittee, moved that the Board of Education be asked to suspend the undertaking of March, 1910, that the Lancashire Committee should spend between £50,000 and £60,000 a year in the erection of Council schools, until the expiration of one year after the end of the war, and also that the period for dealing with unsatisfactory school premises as scheduled by the Board in March, 1910, should be extended from March 31, 1917, until three years after" the end of the war. According to the original schedule, he said, there were 20.5 unsatisfactory schools or school departments, and up to the present 184 schools had been dealt with. The Committee considered that they ought not to be tied to the time-table under the special circumstances of to-day. With regard to the erection of new Council schools, there Were now five schools in course of erection, and these would be proceeded with, but they proposed with regard to five others that the contracts should not be let at present. In addition there are the schools to be built by the Voluntary managers, one of which was in course of erection. It was proposed that this building should be completed, but the com- mittee felt it would be impossible to ask the managers to proceed with the furnishing or to find money during the war for the building, en- largement, or improvement of other schools. Several other schools had been built in' the county which were not included in the pro- gramme, and between 100 and 150 others had been improved and extended, so that the posi- tion of school buildings in the county was in- finitely better than it was five years ago. The committee had already done much more than the Board of Education had asked them to do.

The committee agreed to the prnosaL A decrease was reported in the number of necessitous school children.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
Al PRINCELY GIFT.

Maharajah of Gwalior's Present to the King. The King on Monday at Buckingham Palace accepted from his Highness the Maharajah Scindia of Gwalior a Christmas gift of a fleet of motor ambulances, auxiliary cars, and motor cycles. The gift was offered to him by Lieuten- ant Colonel Sir David Barr, who represented the Maharajah. The King was accompanied by the Queen, Princess Mary and Prince Henry, and Sir James Dunlop Smith. The fleet consists of 41 ambulance cars, four officers' cars, five motor lurries, and ten motor cycles. Each car has attached to it a brass plate bearing the name of the donor, " Scindia." The cars entered the Palace grounds by the Grosvenor Gardens Gate, and filed past the King and Queen, who had taken up their stand near the King's deor, afterwards leaving the grounds by the Garden Gate, and passing to the Mall through the courtyard. The King accepted the gift, and placed it at the disposal of the naval and military authori- ties. His Majesty intimated his intention of telegraphing his thanks personally to the Maharajah.

SWANSEA ELECTION.

Mr. Masterman's Nomination

Confirmed.

A" PAINORITY'S PROTEST.

The conference of Liberal delegates was held at Neath on Saturday to select a candidate in succession to Sir David Brynmor Jones, who has retired from the representation of the Swansea District. Mr. Dan Thomas, private secretary to. Sir Alfred Mond, the member for Swansea Town, retired from the -contest, and the remaining candidates were Mr. C. F. G. Masterman and Mr. T. Jeremiah ,Williams, of Morriston.

Mr. Edward Harris alleged that slim tactics had been introduced into the selection, and undue official influence had been used in favour ef Mr. Masterman. A private letter, said Mr. Harris, had been published with no other object in view. The speaker was interrupted with cries of "No" and "Sit down," and proceeded amid a din which lasted several minutes. When order was restored the Chairman called for nominations, whereupon Mr. Moses Thomas, - of Aberavon, announced that the seventeen Aberavon delegates were for Mr. Masterman.

THE INTERVENTION OF MR. LLOYD GEORGE. When the uproar had subsided the nomina- tions were proceeded with. They underwent but slight revision, and eventually of the votes 80 were cast for Mr. Masternian and 64 for Mr. Williams.

An attempt was then made to make lir. Afasterruan the unanimous choice of the con-

ference. A resolution with that object wus Proposed by Mr. Parry Evans, of Neath, sec- onded by the Rev. T. W. George, of Neath. Mr. Harris said that much as he admired Mr. Lloyd George he would rather throw him over than Mr. Williams on this occasion. Mr. Lloyd George had risen to the top of the ladder with the help of Wales and Welsh Nationalism. Now he was pushing a friend before a Welshman and a Welsh Nationalist. (" Hear, hear," and cries of No, no," and disorder.) "You can say what you like," concluded Mr. Harris, "but the facts speak for themselves." Mr. Richard Buckland, of Morriston, said that if matters had been fair and square in this contest he would willingly have fallen in with the motion, but such had not been the case. The letter received by the Chairman showed that undue influence had been at work.

From this point to the conclusion the proceed- ings were of a stormy character; but eventually when the resolution was put it was carried, although there was a strong negative vote. The net result was that Mr. Masterman was elected as Liberal candidate by a majority. _ . .

During the week district meetings had been held at which nominations had been received, a' '1 delegates numbering 144 were appointed, those delegates constituting Saturday's confer- cnett. The district voting returns showed 78 rotes for Mr. Afasterman and 66 for Mr. Williams, with Mr. Dan Thomas last.

TIIE VERY BEST AT THE LOWFST RATES.

FOR FUNERAL FURNISHING

REFINLIECO

" The moment has now arrived for us to make use of the weakness which he has now shown. After having reinforced ourselves with men as well as with material, the hour of attack has arrived. We have kept the German forces in check, and it is now our business definitely to clear our fatherland of the invader. "Soldiers, more than ever before, Prance relies on your courage, your energy, and your desire to conquer at any cost. You have already been victorious on the Marne, on the Yser, at Ypres, and in Lorraine. You will know how to conquer un' d the final triumph. " JOFrRE." According to Monday's news, the Allied troops gained ground in Belgium. There was some progress in the region of Lom- bartzyde and St. George's and to the south-east of Korteker and the south-east of Biasehoote, the occupation of some houses at Zwarletem, south of Zillebeke, and the bombardment by the enemy of the hospital of Ypres. The British troops delivered an attack and recovered most of the trenches that had been previously lost.

WARSHIPS INTERVENE. British warships have again bombarded the Belgian coast towns of Zeebrugge and Heyst.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0175.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR TERRITORIALS. Treated Well At Tunbridge.

A Warrington Territorial at Tunbridge Wells, writing home, says : " We are all getting as hard as nails, and only waiting orders to do our share in defeating the Kaiser's hordes. The people of Tunbridge Wells are doing their utmost to make our stay here as pleasant as possible. There are between 40 and 50 soldiers' clubs for the use of the troops. Concerts are held every evening in at least one of these clubs, and there is a grand weekly concert in the Great Hall, and refresh- ments are provided free. The general public, too, are very kind, and individual soldiers are invited out to tea at most of the private houses in the town. In a Ward, we are quite enjoying our stay at this historic old place, where our old-time kings and queens were wont to visit and talk politics (perhaps) in the old Pump Room."

PRAISE FOR W.%IIIIIN6TON 11EN.

Good Soldiers—and Gentlemen.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
A letter recently appeared in a Tunbridge Wells newspaper, from which we extract the following : " I agree with all that has been said with reference to the fine physique and general ap- pearance of the Liverpool Scottish men, who, after a few days' stay amonW us, have now gone to the front to upheld the honour of their country; and I share the confidence everyone feels that they will, when the opportunity comes, distinguish themselves in the field as their brothers, the London Scottish, have no nobly done. At the same time, so much has been said about the Scottish men that a stranger might imagine that they were the only military in the town, and I write this letter in order to remind your readers that the 4th and sth South Lanes. are entitled to as much recognition as the Liverpool Scottish. "It is proverbially odious to make compari- sons, but having come into daily, I might almost say hourly, touch with the men of the 4th South Lancs. Battalion, I contend that all that has been said in praise of the Liverpool Scottihh might with equal truth be said of them. "It has been our privilege to open my late mother's house in Oakdale-road for reading, writing, and recreation, and there we have seen the men, when off duty, and not under military discipline. What higher praise can I give than to say that their behaviour has always been that of gentlemen in the highest sense of the word? From rill that I can hear both from the officers and men, they will undoubtedly, when their time comes, prove to friend and foe alike that the men of Warrington and the stir- rmi,ding districts in South Lancashire are as well qualified to defend all that England ho! Is dear an any other Battalion in the King's Army. "All honour to the men who are fighting our battles, whatever their regiment. "Jwo. B. SN'ELL."

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY. DECEMBER 26, 1914.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0179.txt | included=true | included =====
PROGRESS OF THE WAR.

Involved Areas.

TWENTY-F 1

IRST WEEK.

AN ADVANCE.

The Allies Move Forward in Flanders.

A despatch from the "Eye Witness present with General Headquarters" issued in London on Sunday night describes recent events in Flanders. The beginning of the Allied offen- sive is the main subject. Until last Thursday progress had been slow, not much ground had been gained, and nowhere had the positions taken been of critical ifflportance. An advance has now been made at many points, but we are warned that the Germans have put the past two months to use in Belgium and have made the most formidable entrenchments. _

Along the Arras, Ypres, Dismude, Nieuport front last week's gains were in the sum con- siderable. Berlin says the British were repulsed on Saturday, and lost 200 prisoners, and Paris says the British have been driven from some trenches they had taken. Further ground was gained before Nieu port and St. Georges, as well as east and south of Ypres, north of La. Bassee, and north-west of Arras. The positions east of Vermelles have been maintained.

Summary of Authentic News From Ail

SPECIALLY COMPILED FOR " THE EXAMINER."

GOOD NEWS FOR THE ALLIES.

German Headquarters announce that the Kaiser, having fully recovered, has returned to the Front.

SOUTH AFRICA.

The South African Government announced on Sunday that reconnaissances.in force are being carried out by Union Forces in the Aus and Gliabub regions of German South-West Africa. This announcement shows that enemy territory has already been penetrated to a considerable distance.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0180.txt | included=true | included =====
METRE'S ORDER. Time Arrhed to Drhe Out Invader.

The report issued on Monday by the German Headquarters and circulated from Berlin by wireless, states that "the great activity shown by the French along our entire front may be ex- plained by the following Army Order, dated December 17th, and signed by General Jere, which we found on a French officer"— "During three months the enemy has made tier and numerous attacks without being able to break through our lines.. Everywhere he has been victoriously 'repulsed.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0181.txt | included=true | included =====
GENERAL

ADVANCE.

FRENCH PROGRESS ALL ALONG THE LINE.

The French report on Monday said that in the Champagne and in the Argonne the advance was appreciable. North-east of Beausejoui, as well as at Prosnes and at Perthes, in the Argonne, and east again in the Varennes region, the front has been thrust forward.

In the Wood of La Grurie, in the Argonne, a sap which was being driven by the enemy against the Allied line, was blown up and the Allies established themselves in the excavations thus made.

The same communique described incidents in Belgium north of the Lys, and anAounced that the first line of German trenches south-west of Lens had been carried. _

On the Meuse, both at Brabant and north of Fort Troyon, the Allies have been active, suc- cessfully attacking positions held by the Ger- mans for some time past.

"The Times" correspondent at Petrograd maintained that these backward movements were due to strategical considerations and had been deliberately 'undertaken by the Russian Higher Command. The German attack north of the Vistula seems to have been completely defeated.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
POLAND.

Russia Not Defeated.

Russia's main armies were engaged again last week-end, proving that Berlin wildly exagger- ated Von Hindenburg's achievement on the Vis- tula-Lodz front. His -.victory " never promised to end the campaign. as the newspapers pre- tended, and actually has led only to new battles in another place. The Russians retreated in some places about ten miles, but the armies which Von Hindenburg thought he had crushed are holding new positions on the lizura, Rawka, and Nida rivers stoutly. and for the time at least successfully. The Germans were thus still 30 to 40 miles away from Warsaw, with power- ful armies in their front.

RUSSIAN STRATEGY, NOT RUSSIAN DEFEAT.

Monday's communique from Petrograd said confidently that there was nothing of import- ance to report from the battlefields on the left bank of the Vistula, thus doing away with Ger- many's boast of victory. But it was evident that the Russians have had to retire in the centre of their front. They still held the line of the Bzura from its junction with the Vistula. Further south they were posted on the banks of the Rawka, and, south again, they had retired from Piotrkow to Opoczno—a withdrawal of 'very considerable distance, which was due to a new German ad- vance from Wielun.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0183.txt | included=true | included =====
AUSTRIA.

On the Eastern front the Austrians claim on behalf of themselves and their German Allies that the Russians are being pressed back along the whole line from the Vistula to the Car- pathians.

In Galicia the beleaguered garrison of Przemysl made a sortie westward in great strength, evidently with the intention of trying to escape. That is usually a sign that the end is at hand, not that the investing forces are being beaten, as the Austrians allege.

44 TOMMY " WILL REVENGE SCARBOROUGH. Sergt. Joynson, of the Ist Wiltshire Regiment. told our reporter that the men did not mind the fighting, but they found the cold weather and the flooded trenches decidedly uncomfort- able. "When they hear of the affair at Scar- borough," he added, " our lads won't hall' let the Germans have it." iWe were n the trenches for 16 days," he continued, " but if the recruits would come fast enough, there would be no need for U 9 to stay in for so long at a time. The Germans are very quick to Pick up their wounded and remove the dead, so that We cannot ascertain how many men they have lost. We did not know the King had been to the front until after he had returned, but I am sure his visit has put even more cour- age into the men. The Indian troops deserve every praise., but (It ieather is against them. I saw one of the " Johnsons" blow up two men. one of whom was killed and the other wounded."

Him Was It. Foshan Beene, at a hunt breakfast on Long Island, talked of the Quorn pack. " The last time I hunted with the Quornites," he said, "I crossed a. turnip patch side by side with a red-faced, white whiskered old chap on a big grey. We'd best go careful here,' I said to the old fellow. 'They tell me these turnips belong to an old curmudgeon who raises the very deuce if the hunt hurts his crop.' bein' as him's me,' said grey whis- kers, , I guess he won't mind to-day.' "

===== 0002601_19141226_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
AUSTRIANS AGAIN DEFEATED.

In the extreme south the movement of the Austrians through the Bukla Pass of the Car- pathians has been defeated, and the garrison of Przemysl has been hurled back from its sortie into the besieged city. ' The armies moving over the Carpathians and from behind Cracow against the Russians have been brought to a stand, while the Przemsyl garrison, which attempted concurrently to break out through the besiegers and join its friends, is back again in the fortress after suffering heavy losses.

===== 0002601_19141226_art0185.txt | included=true | included =====
VICTORIOUS SERVIANS.

STUBBORN AUSTRIAN RESISTANCE

-THEN PANIC.

"The Times" correspondent with the Servian Army sends a dispatch in which he sketches the main features of the action fought by the Servians to retake Belgrade. The Austrians made a most stubborn resist- ance, but suddenly gave way. A panic followed and the Servians, pressing their advantage, drove the enemy in disorder over the river.

THE FLIGHT FROM BELGRADE.

Leaving— Valjevo Oa the lath, he says, and proceeding over a. route strewn with the litter of discarded Austrian impedimenta, I arrived at Belgrftdo yesterday on the heels of the vic- torious Servian army. The recapture of the capital was not accom- plished without a desperate struggle. Until i demoralisation set n the Austrians put up an exceedingly good fight, but they were unable to withstand the repeated onslaughts of the Ser- vians. The Austrians had been steadily removing their stores in preparation for retreat. A bridge had been thrown across the Save near the railway bridge, but when the news of the loss of Torlak arrived there began a general " sauve qui pent." Officers at mess left their soup half consumed and, wine half drunk and decamped in. a hurry. The multifarious races of the Empire commenced fighting amongst themselves who should go first. The attempt of the Servians to get round on the left and destroy the bridge was foiled by the resistance of the Austrians on the position at Topchider, and the retreat, though wild and disorganised, was able to continue.

SERVIANS' GREAT HAUL. The first Servians entered the city on the moral rig of the. 15th. They were followed by King Peter. while fighting was still proceeding near the bridge. The Servitors then shelled and broke the bridge. At that moment a great num- ber of Austrian troops and, a mass of transport were still south of the Save, and according to the latest count 150 officers, 10,000 men, eight cannon. 250 transport waggons, and 1,000 horses fell into Semi," .bands. Many cannon were thrown into the river.•

===== 0002601_19141226_art0186.txt | included=true | included =====
WAR ITEMS.

SOLDIER WHO HAS SEEN GERMANS FIRE ON AMBULANCES. , In conversation with an " Examiner" repre- sentative, soldiers at Raddon Court related some of their experiences, and Private llarold Smith, of the Ist Battalion Rifle Brigade, stated that he had seen the ambulances tired upon by the Germans. "The enemy are frightened of the bayonet," he declared, " and it is laughable to see them come about ten yards out of the trenches and then run back."

Saved by a Tobacco Box. Private Smith produced a battered tobacco box, which, he declared, had been struck by shrapnel—a piece of which he was saving as a souvenir whilst it was in his pocket. "The Germans won't let us get our wounded out of the trenches," said Private Boodle, who had been all along the point from Mons to Ypres.

THANKS TO WARRINGTON.

Gratitude of the Committee at Fazaherley Hospital.

fThe following is the full text of the letter of thanks received by the lfa,yoress from Lieut.- Colonel Nathan Raw:—

West Lancashire Territorial Nursing Service Committee of the West Lancashire Association, 66, Rodney-street, Liverpool, 9th December, 1914. To the Mayoress of Warrington. Dear Madam,—ln consequence of the excel- lent report submitted at a meeting o the above on December 2nd, by Mrs. Edelsten, of the good work done by the town of Warrington in dis- patching so many hundreds of garments from the Town Hall to the First' Western General Hospital at Fazakerley, I am writing to ex- press the deep gratitude of the committee to you and all in Warrington who are contributing to this splendid work.

Mrs. Edelsten particularly brought before the committee the name of Mr. Fairhurst, the Borough Treasurer, to whom she is indebted for kindly and energetic help; she also wished to take this opportunity of thanking the Misses White and those who attend weekly at the Town Hall and assist her in receiving and despatch- ing the clothing. I might add that the needs of the Base Hos- pital increase rather than diminish, and the committee confidently looks to Warrington for a continuation of its generous and much appre- ciated support —1 am, yours faithfully, NATHAN RAW, MM. Secretary. It will doubtless interest all those who have contributed so generously to the Base Hospital to know that more than 3,050 garments have been sent from Warrington.

" TOMMY'S " GRATITUDE.

Liberal Women Workers Receive Many Letters of Thanks. JUST THE TIME FOR MORE "COMFORTS."

At the Crosfield Memorial Hall on Tuesday evening, the last "Gloves and Mittens" classes before the break-up for the Christmas holidays was held, and another large consignment of the very appreciable comforts was forwarded to the headquarters of the Grand Duke Michael's Scheme.

Although the energetic workers have the op- portunity of a well-deserved respite, however, many of them are too enthusiastic to take ad- vantage of it, and Mrs. Osborne continues to receive numbers of parcels—which will shortly be in the hands of the " TOMMiel4" away in the trenches and elsewhere. Many of the girls em- ployed at the works participating in the War- rington scheme, at present have, of course, more leisure time than usual, and they are very suit- ably passing it by the knitting of the orMed gloves and mittens.

Needless to point out, now is the very time for the soldiers to receive these comforts—the win- try weather being at present very severe. The Women's Liberal Association Sewing Class, although officially, so to speak, taking a brief holiday during the Christmas week, like- wise continues its labour of love in various ways.

In addition to the usual contributions to the Mayoress's and other funds, preparations are being made to assist the Veterinary Surgeons' Fund, whilst large consignments of garments are also to be forwarded to the soldiers per Miss Truda Crosfield—who is, of course, serving in France with the Red Cross.

POOR "KIDDIES" ALSO REMEMBERED. With great pleasure, Mrs. Osborne—the ener- getic secretary—has just received a gift of forty pairs of good, warm stockings for distributioti amongst poor Warrington children. Of this considerate gift, the generous donor is Mrs. Ross, of Southport and Iforsetharket-street, Warrington.

"JUST THINK OF IT." "Just think of it—a full Christmas dinner for a shilling—soup, turkey, plum-pudding, pine- apple, coffee—" " Where?" "I don't know where; but just think of it!" GOT WHAT HE WANTED. He was a poor man, but a contented one. Santa Claus came to him, and said: "What do you want, my friend?" "Nothing," replied the man, hoping his modesty would be richly rewarded. And dear old Santa Claus was so pleased that he gave it to him, and passed on. THE ELEPHANTS AND THE MOON. This is the fourth and last •of our series of stories which used to be told in India three thousand years ago. It tells about some ele- phants; who, in time of drought, were hard pressed to find water to drink. At last, how- ever, they discovered a pool, near which lived a colony of hares; and, in going to and fro, the elephants used to trample upon several of the bares every day. The matter at last became so serious that a meeting of the hares was held; and, after a lot of talk, they decided to send the oldest hare to stop the elephants using the pool. The following day; as all the elephants ap- proached the pool, the old hare stood upon a hillock and said: "Sirs, I have been sent by the moon, to tell you that this is his pool; and that the hares, whom you are driving away, are it guardians." "But," replied the elephants, "how are we to know that is the truth?" "Well," said the old hare, "if you will come here to-night, you will see the moon in the pool, shaking with rage." That night all the elephants came to the pool, and in it saw the reflection of the moon, which quaked as the water rippled. Then, in great fear, they all promised not`to trespass on the pool any more. Superstition often makes the mighty tremble. THE DOG AND THE PROVERB. A Frenchman did not like the look of a bark- ing dog barring his way. "It's all right," said the host; "don't you know the proverb, 'Bark- ing dogs don't bite?":' "Ah, yes," said the Frenchman, "I know ze proverbe; you know ze proverbe; but ze dog—does he know ze pro- verbe?" TWO SMART BOYS. Teacher: "Now, can any boy finish this sentence: England expects Willie: "To win!" Teacher: "So you've come to school without a pen, Tommy! What would you say if one of our soldiers went to France without a gun?" , Tommy: "Please, • sir, I should say he was in officer." "ONLY TWO OF US." Tommy was describing a splendid feast he had had on Christmas. "Yes," he declared, smacking his lips: "I never enjoyed a dinner so much. Oh, that turkey! What a bird! It was absolutely deli- cious, and there wasn't a bit left but the bones!" "How many were there of you?" someone asked. "Oh, only two of us." was the startling reply. " What ! Only two?" "Yes, two. Why not? The turkey and I!" HE DESERVED ONE. It was Christmas morning, and Mr. Brownton was taking the air, when a seedy-looking man strode up the garden path." "Merry Christmas, guy'nor," he said: "I've called for my Christmas box." "Christmas box?" echoed Mr. Brownton : Why, I don't know you. Are you the village dustman?" , "No, sir. I'm the chap that played the cornet outside here last year, and you told me to take toy 'ook." "Oh, you are, are you?" said our hero, shud- dering to think about it: "And why, pray, should I give you a Christmas box?" " Well, guv'nor, I haven't been here playing this year." And,.w;lising what a lot of agony he had been spt:redi. ‘‘. thankfal gentleman par*l with a Christmas sixpence. - STORY OF THE GLOW WORM. Many children who live in the North of Eng- land have never seen a glow-worm, that queer- looking little grub which at night sends out of its body a wonderful radiant light like a tiny fairy-lamp. The poet Shelley speaks of A glow-worm golden, In a dell of dew, Scattering unbeholden Its aerial hut Among the leaves and grass That screen it from the view. This is the beautiful legend of how the glow- worm got its wonderful light: It was the Holy Night. The new-born Babe lay on the straw in the manger, and the blessed Mother sheltered him as best she could from the piercing wind. The ex and the ass drew

near and lay down to form a bulwark from the draught, that the heat of their warm bodies might be given to the little King. The little birds filled up the crevices of the stable and then came and nestled close that their little downy wings might protect Him still further from the cold night air. Now in the stable there crept a little lowly worm, so poor an insect there seemed nothing that it could possibly do for the new-born King of Love. With longing the little worm looked on at all these offerings of the animals and birds, and cast about for some service that could be rendered by one so weak and small as himself. At length, with great labour and perseverance, he managed to lift one leaf from the poor and lay it on the little One's soft limbs. As he did so, the Babe stretched out His hand and laid one tiny finger upon the worm. The glow of that touch has never faded, and to this day the glow-worm has kept its lustrous light to remind people for ever of that loving act of service. IN SAFE KEEPING. "Grannie," said little Maggie, as she counted over her nuts on Christmas morning, "can you eat nuts?" "No, pet," said the old lady. "I've no teeth." "Then," said Maggie, emptying her pinafore in Grannie's lap. "I'll give you these to mind till I come back." HIS CHRISTMAS LIST. "I am really delighted at the interest my boy Tommy is taking in his writing," sajiTlfr. Hickleby. "He spends two hours a day at it." "Really? How very strange! How did you get him to do it?" "Oh, RS for that, a week ago I told him to write me out a list of everything he wanted for Christmas, and he's still at it." A PLOT PREPARED. Uncle: "You want me to tell you a story. What sort of one would you like?" Bertie: "The one about the good uncle, who gave his nephew a new bike for Christmas," "LIGHT" ON THE PUDDING. Mistress (shouting to servant in kitchen): "Why do you not bring in the plum pudding, Jane?" Jane: "I'm bringing it now, mum. We couldn't get the brandy to light, but it's all right now—we poured a little paraffin over it." A NEW WORD GAME. Here is a new kind of game in word-building. Make as long a sentence as you can out of ten names taken from any war map, using only the letters employed in the names chosen. First write down the ten names you are going to use, and then make the most sensible sentence you can from the letters employed in them. ONE SHILLING A BOX. Some recruits were being drilled by a very impatient cavalry drill-sergeant. After about an hour of hard drilling at the sword exercise, the sergeant gave the order, "Stand at ease." No sooner were the men standing at ease than the sergeant commenced to complain of their attitudes and wound up by saying, "Why, you fellows are like a lot of dummies. I can get a smarter lot of soldiers at a shilling per box." After a minute's duration, one of the recruits remarked: "I suppose there would be sergeants among them?" LOST AND FOUND. This beautiful song was penned by an Ameri- can, whose name I do not know: I lost the brook as it wound its way Like a thread of silver hue; Through greenwood and valley, through meadow gay, It was hidden from my view: But I found it again in a noble river, Sparkling and broad and free, Wider and fairer, growing ever, Tilt it reached the boundless sea. I lost a tiny seed I sowed With many a sigh and tear, And vainly ,waited through sunshine and cold For the young green to appear: ' But surely after many long days The blossom and fruit will come, And the reapers on high the sheaves will rai,e For a joyful harvest home. , • I lost a love that made my life, A love that was all for me. Oh, vainly I sought it amid the strife On this dark and raging sea: But deeper and purer I know it waits Beyond my wistful eyes; I shall find it again within .the gates Of the garden of paradise. CHRISTMAS. Although this number of "The Examiner" is dated Saturday, December 26th, it is published TIIURSDAY, DECEMBER 24th, because Christmas Day and Boxing Day fall on Friday and Saturday, the usual days upon which the paper is printed and published.

being that they had thrown over their belief in Jesus Christ. They were intent, not upon the peace and spiritual joy of Christmas, but upon crushing this country. • It was saddening to think how they had plunged Europe into this great conflict. The people of this country had no desire to interfere with Germany's prosperity, yet in pursuance of their desire for world power they had brought about the war. Although this Christmas, the Rector added, would be a sad one in many respects, that should not prevent Christians from rejoicing in the commemoration of the Incarnation of their Saviour. He appealed to his hearers not only to make their home life as bright as possible in the circumstances, but to keep the day in the Church's way. GOLBORNE-STREET BAPTIST. An excellent musical programme was rendered at the Golborne-street Baptist Church on Sun- day evening. Mrs. Heywood gave "He shall feed his flock" and "He was despised" in most effective manner, whilst Miss Bates, in spite of a slight cold, sang "Come unto Him" very sweetly. The choir, under the leadership of Mr. H. Barnes, rendered "Oh, Thou that bringest good tidings," "Thus spake the seraph," and "We have seen His Star in the East," with great effect. Mr. S. Bickers pre- sided at the organ. The Rev. H. Ogden read an extract from a letter written by Captain Chap- lain Tattersall, a personal friend, who is serv- ing with the forces at the front. Next Sunday evening a carol service will be held. PUBLIC AMUSEMENTS. HOLIDAY .ATTRACTION AT THE ROYAL COURT. The brilliant light opera, "The Marriage Market," will be presented at the Royal Court Theatre on Boxing Day and throughout next week by Messrs. Macdonald and Young's Com- pany of talented artistes. There will be a matinee at 2.30 on Boxing Day, Monday and New Year's Day, in addition to the usual evening performances at 7.30.

I WARRINGTON'S BEST FOR BATLEY. An Interesting Game in Mud, Fog and Rain. JOLLEY BADLY MAULED IN A RUSH. • Notes by"Centre.!' • • The first encounter with Batley this season ended disastrously for Warrington, the York- shire men, playing on their own ground, collar- ing the League points, after a strenuously con- , • • tested game, in which the lads from Wilderspool tried desperately hard, but found the fates against them—as well as the weather; though, of course, that was really against both teams. • A thick, pea-soupy fog hung over the field. So ‘, dense was it that whilst in the earlier stages it : was not easy for the spectators to follem the, play, towards the end it became almost impos- sible for them to dd. so; the final whistle being sounded in semi-darkness. In addition, lain , fell, so that, on the whole, the conditions were ' just about as remote from the ideal as they could possibly have been. Still, what was . visible of the game was decidedly interesting from the spectators' point of view, clever rounds of passing on both sides lending it a touch of distinction. Unfortunately, neither side was able to finish the game with a full complement of players, for, just prior to the interval, Thomas, of Batley, sustained a severe injury to his ribs, as the result of a, collision with Brookes, whilst, about half=way through the second moiety, Jolley was badly mauled in stopping a Batley rush and had to. retire from the. game.

WARRINGTON "OFF FORM" AND TIMOROUS. When the whistle signalled the cessation of hostilitieS the score-board read: Batley, a goals 2 tries (10 Points); Warrington, nil—and one could not but feel that the result just 'about represented the respective merits of the . two a; ea , teams, judged on the afternoon's play, and judged on that only, for Warrington were not o in their best form. Batley- had the advantage er er —for what it is worth--of playing on . their native heath, so to speak; whilst in the second, id and scoring, half they had also the help of the in of downward slope—a help which is a help indeed is in the closing stages of a fast game on heavy ground and amid depressing atmospheric sur- roundings. The enforced retirement of Jolley was a big blow to Warrington, for in many 4 tight corner he had been showing wonderful

" TIIE INDOMITABLE VIGIL." We gather round our Christmas tires again To hail, with grateful hearts, the Heavenly Birth; Our listening ears amid the dark we strain. To catch the angel-song of "Peace on earth." But a strange shadow broods across the skies, Lit long ago by Christ's own shining' Star, And from the East we only catch the cries Of strong men wounded in a world at war. We tremble 'neath the sacred mistletoe; Its pallid fruit reminds us of the dead; And, as *one sang of Christmas whom I know, The holly hurts—" its berries are too red." But still for us the Christmas tires are warm, And still the ancient Christmas feast invites; We reek not of the rising of the storm, And drown our sorrows in the old delights. We sit at ease, our roof-trees firm and strong, Our cattle safe, our children by our side; While thousands from their ruined cities throng In dens and caves and hedges fain to hide. In quietness and confidence we lie By friends surrounded, while in trenches drear, Where hissing shrapnel lights the flaming sly, Our soldiers stand to keep us safely here. The very camp fires scarce for them may burn At Christmas, lest their watchful foes should see; Their hearts are with us while their eyes they turn To face the crash of Hell's artillery, Or, wounded, shiver in some roofless barn. Or fall, untended, on the bloody field— Heroes of Mons, and Ypres, and the Marne. Willing to die, but not prepared to yield. And what of those who on the wild North Sea Their stern, "indomitable vigil" keep Night after night, day after day, that we At home in England may in safety sleep? Out on the heaving waters dark and chill, When the tumultuous tempest loudly roars, With nerves unshaken and with iron will, Through the long weeks they guard our island shores. With straining eyes they gaze towards the laud Where envious rivals in the darkness lurk, Or, drenched with rain, upon the bare decks stand, Where deadly mines may do their devil.i work. Dreaming of home, as the slow months go by. While stealthy death may stab them tinals:.re, Or some dim monster, soaring through the sky, Creep near and siDite them with the bombs it bears. And always watching for the shadowy ships , That sneak out some defenceless town to raid; Waiting the thunder of the iron lips Whose utmost wrath would find them undis- mayed— Patient, undaunted, sleepless, tireless, brave, "Out of the limelight," ready, hour by hour, Whether the bellowing tempests round them rave, Or the fierce snakes of ocean crawl and cower. Bold are the lads who on the bleak earth lie, Wet to the skin or frozen to the bone; But not less bold the men who death defy Far out upon the heaving waters lone. " They also serve who only stand and wait "-- Easier to face red battle's devilish roar Than month by month to guard our island gale And keep the foiled invader from our shore. Shall we forget them as, secure at home, Around the festive board once more we meet? Secure alone because, beyond the foam, These sons of Nelson man that silent Fleet. " here's to the soldier in his sodden trench"- 11 raise our glasses and, with eyes aglow, WC% _,Proudly drink to "Tommy" and to Ireneh— Shall we forget brave "Jack" and jellicoe' ARTHUR BENNEW. *Richard he tiallienne: "Cliristma,; in War-time."
